Bifurcations of embedded curves and towards an extension of Taubes’ Gromov invariant to Calabi–Yau 3 3 -folds
Shaoyun Bai, Mohan Swaminathan

Abstract
. We define an integer-valued virtual count of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves of two times a primitive homology class and arbitrary genus in symplectic Calabi–Yau 3 3 -folds, which can be viewed as an extension of Taubes’ Gromov invariant. The construction depends on a detailed study of bifurcations of moduli spaces of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves which is partially motivated by Wendl’s recent solution of Bryan–Pandharipande’s super-rigidity conjecture.



1.
Introduction

Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold, i.e., it is a closed symplectic manifold of (real) dimension
6
with
c_{1}(TX,\omega)=0\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. After choosing an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, the genus
g
Gromov–Witten invariant of
(X,\omega)
of class
0\neq A\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
with no marked points, denoted by
\text{GW}_{A,g}(X)
, is defined by certain counts of stable
J
-holomorphic maps into
X
and it is independent of
J
. In general,
\text{GW}_{A,g}(X)\in\mathbb{Q}
because some
J
-holomorphic maps may possess non-trivial automorphism groups.

On the other hand, Taubes’ Gromov invariants
[
24
]
define integer-valued invariants for symplectic
4
-manifolds by counting
embedded
pseudo-holomorphic curves. The construction heavily relies on intersection theory in dimension
4
. The goal of this paper is to partially generalize Taubes’ Gromov invariants to define invariants of symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-folds by studying moduli spaces of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves.

1.1.
Statement of the main Theorem

We start by introducing some notions important for our discussion. The term
J
-curve
throughout this paper stands for a simple
J
-holomorphic map with smooth and connected domain into an almost complex manifold
(X,J)
. Any closed connected embedded
2
-dimensional almost complex submanifold
C\subset X
, gives rise to a
J
-curve unique up to the reparametrization of the domain. In the following, unless otherwise specified,
(X,\omega)
will denote a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold.

Definition 1.1
.

Define
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
to be the subset of almost complex structures
J
for which there exists either a simple and non-embedded
J
-curve or two simple
J
-curves with distinct but intersecting images.

It is a basic fact (see
[
9
, Theorem 2.4.3]
for example) that
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
is of codimension
2
in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
. Thus, along generic paths
\gamma\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, we may assume that all simple curves are embedded and pairwise disjoint by appealing to the Sard--Smale theorem.
1
1

1

Strictly speaking, in order to apply the Sard–Smale theorem, we need to pass to appropriate Banach completions, refer to Section
1.4
for details.

Definition 1.2

(Normal deformation operator)
.

Let
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
and let
C\subset X
be a smooth closed embedded
J
-curve, i.e. the tangent bundle of
C
, written as
T_{C}
, is invariant under the
J
–action. The normal bundle
N_{C}=T_{X}|_{C}/T_{C}
is then naturally a complex vector bundle. The linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator on
T_{X}|_{C}
descends to an operator

\displaystyle D^{N}_{C,J}:\Omega^{0}(C,N_{C})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,N_{C})

(1.1)

called the
normal deformation operator
of the embedded curve
C
.

Given an embedded
J
-curve
C
, the Fredholm index of the operator (
1.1
) is
0
. When
\ker D^{N}_{C,J}=0
, and thus
{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}=0
, recall that we can define the sign of
C
as a point in the moduli space of
J
-curves as follows.

Definition 1.3

(Sign)
.

Let
C\subset X
be an embedded
J
-curve such that
D^{N}_{C,J}
is an isomorphism. We then define the sign of the curve
C
, denoted by

\displaystyle\text{sgn}(C)\in\{-1,+1\}

(1.2)

as follows. We deform
D^{N}_{C,J}
in a
1
-parameter family to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear operator which is an isomorphism and define the sign of
C
according to the parity of the associated spectral flow
2
2

2

While the term “spectral flow” is standard, it is a slight abuse of terminology in this context as our operators are not self-adjoint and there are no eigenvalues involved. More explicitly, to define
\text{sgn}(C)
, we take a
generic

1
-parameter family
\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operators with
D_{0}=D^{N}_{C,J}
and
D_{1}
a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism and count the (parity of the) finite number of
t\in(0,1)
for which
D_{t}
fails to be an isomorphism.
. Alternatively, we consider the straight line homotopy
\{D_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}
from
D_{0}=D^{N}_{C,J}
to its
\mathbb{C}
-linear part
D_{1}
and trivialize the determinant line bundle

\displaystyle t\mapsto\det(D_{t})=\det\ker D_{t}\otimes(\det{\text{coker }}D_{t})^{*}

(1.3)

and define the sign of
C
to be the sign of the resulting isomorphism between
\det(D_{0})=\det(0)\otimes\det(0)^{*}=\mathbb{R}
and
\det(D_{1})
with the complex orientation.

The notion of super-rigidity requires that any holomorphic multiple cover of any
J
-curve
C\subset X
does not admit any non-trivial normal deformation. More precisely, we have the following definition.

Definition 1.4

(Super-rigidity)
.

Given an embedded
J
-curve
C\subset X
as in Definition
1.2
, we say that it is
super-rigid
if for every non-constant holomorphic stable map
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
, the pullback operator

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}:\Omega^{0}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N_{C})\to\Omega^{0,1}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\tilde{\varphi}^{*}N_{C})

(1.4)

is injective where
\tilde{C}^{\prime}\to\tilde{C}^{\prime}
is the normalization of
C^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\varphi}:\tilde{C}^{\prime}\to C
is the map induced by
\varphi
. We say that a given almost complex structure
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
is
super-rigid
if and only if
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)\setminus\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
and every embedded
J
-curve is super-rigid.

Remark 1.5
.

We recall the definition of
\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
in detail for the reader’s convenience. When
C^{\prime}
is non-singular, the pullback operator
\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
is uniquely characterized as follows. If
D^{N}_{C,J}-\bar{\partial}=A\in\Omega^{0,1}(U,\text{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{C}))
in a trivialization of
N_{C}
over an open subset
U\subset C
, where
\bar{\partial}
is the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator under this trivialization, then
\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}=\bar{\partial}+\varphi^{*}A
in the corresponding trivialization of
\varphi^{*}N_{C}
over
\varphi^{-1}(U)
. Holomorphicity of
\varphi
ensures that
\varphi^{*}A
is a
1
-form of type
(0,1)
. When
C^{\prime}
is nodal, the operator
\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
is (by definition) the restriction of the operator
\tilde{\varphi}^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
to the subspace
\Omega^{0}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N_{C})\subset\Omega^{0}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime*}N_{C})
consisting of those smooth sections of
\tilde{\varphi}^{*}N_{C}\to\tilde{C}^{\prime}
which descend to continuous sections of
\varphi^{*}N_{C}\to C^{\prime}
, i.e., have matching values at the two pre-images in
\tilde{C}^{\prime}
of each nodal point in
C^{\prime}
. A nearly identical discussion can be used to define the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator on
\varphi^{*}T_{X}
for the stable map
\varphi
.

Recent work of Wendl
[
27
]
shows that the set of super-rigid compatible almost complex structures is a Baire subset of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, verifying the generic super-rigidity conjecture of Bryan–Pandharipande
[
3
]
. An important consequence of super-rigidity is the following finiteness result. Suppose
J
is super-rigid, then given any class
A\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there are only finitely many embedded
J
-curves of class
A
and genus
g
and they have disjoint images in
X
, see
[
27
, Corollary 1.4]
. We remark that the work of Doan–Walpuski
[
8
, Theorem 1.6]
proves this finiteness result with only an
a priori
energy bound, without any assumption on the genus, though we do not need this stronger result in the present work.

Let
\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
denote
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
modulo its torsion subgroup
3
3

3

We work modulo torsion purely for notational convenience. With a bit more notation, it is possible to state all our results with
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
instead of
\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.
. Given a homology class
A\in\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, we say it is
primitive
if
\omega(A)>0
and we cannot write
A=kB
for any
B\in\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and integer
k\geq 2
. For a fixed primitive homology class
A
, the works of Zinger
[
28
]
and Doan–Walpuski
[
6
]
show that the signed count (c.f. Definition
1.3
) of embedded
J
-curves of class
A
and genus
h\geq 0
for
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)\setminus\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
defines an invariant of
(X,\omega)
independent of
J
. If
A
is not primitive, although there are only finitely many embedded
J
-curves of class
A
and genus
h
for a super-rigid almost complex structure
J
, the direct signed count of all such
J
-curves may not define an invariant of
(X,\omega)
because
bifurcations
could happen when varying the almost complex structures. The main result of this paper is to define a virtual count of embedded
J
-curves of two times a primitive homology class for
all genera
. We adopt the notation
Gr
in order to be consistent with Taubes’ pioneering work in dimension
4

[
24
]
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Fix a primitive class
A\in\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and an integer
h\geq 0
. For a super-rigid
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, define the
virtual count of embedded genus
h
curves of class
2A
to be the integer

\displaystyle\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,J)=\sum_{C^{\prime}:\,2A,h}\text{sgn}(C^{\prime})+\sum_{g\leq h}\,\sum_{C:\,A,g}\text{sgn}(C)\cdot\text{w}_{2,h}(D^{N}_{C,J})

(1.5)

where the first sum is over embedded
J
-curves
C^{\prime}
of genus
h
and class
2A
, the second sum is over all genera
0\leq g\leq h
and embedded
J
-curves
C
of genus
g
and class
A
,
sgn
is as in Definition
1.3
and the integers
\text{w}_{2,h}
are as in Definition
5.17
.

Then,
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,J)
is independent of the choice of super-rigid
J
and

\displaystyle\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega):=\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,J)

(1.6)

defines a symplectic invariant of
(X,\omega)
.

In other words, we construct correction terms to the naive signed count of embedded
J
-curves of class
2A
and genus
h
from embedded
J
-curves of class
A
and genus
g\leq h
to get a well-defined invariant. For a thorough discussion of the correction terms in (
1.5
), see Section
5.1
.

Remark 1.7

(Symplectic deformation invariance)
.

In fact,
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}
is a symplectic deformation invariant of
(X,\omega)
. To see this, one can use the fact that any
1
-parameter symplectic deformation
\{\omega_{t}\}
can be accompanied by a compatible family
\{J_{t}\}
of almost complex structures.

1.2.
Ingredients of the proof

The proof of Theorem
1.6
(carried out in Section
5.2
) relies on a detailed study of bifurcations of moduli spaces of embedded
J
-curves when varying
J
in a generic
1
-parameter family. To be more precise, although super-rigidity is a generic condition for
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, there are codimension
1
subsets of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
where super-rigidity fails to hold. Given a path of almost complex structures
\gamma:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
such that both
\gamma(-1)
and
\gamma(1)
are super-rigid, we need to understand the bifurcations of moduli spaces of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves when
\gamma
intersects these codimension
1
subsets.

The first key ingredient is a
necessary
condition for the occurrence of a bifurcation. For a more general and complete statement, see Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 1.8

(special case of Theorem
3.1
)
.

Given a sequence
t_{k}\rightarrow t^{\prime}
in
(-1,1)
, define
J_{k}:=\gamma(t_{k})
and
J:=\gamma(t^{\prime})
so that
J_{k}\rightarrow J
in the
C^{\infty}
-topology. Suppose
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and we have a sequence of genus
h
embedded
J_{k}
-holomorphic curves
\varphi_{k}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}\to X
converging to a stable
J
-holomorphic map
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}C\subset X
in the Gromov topology. Here
C
is an embedded
J
-curve of genus
g\leq h
and
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
is a stable genus
h
map of degree
k
with target
C
.

Then we either have
g=h,k=1
and
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
is an isomorphism, or the pullback map
\varphi^{*}:\ker D^{N}_{C,J}\to\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
is only injective but not surjective.

Theorem
1.8
allows us to exclude the possibility of bifurcations from nodal curves (possibly with ghost components) into embedded curves once the path
\gamma
is chosen generically. When the homology class represented by
\varphi_{k}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{k}\to X
is primitive, the degree
k
is then necessarily equal to
1
and Theorem
1.8
implies that the limit
\varphi
must be an isomorphism. In particular, the stable map
C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}C\subset X
does not have any ghost irreducible component. Combined with Ionel–Parker’s construction of Kuranishi models for birth-death bifurcations of embedded
J
-curves
[
16
, Theorem 6.2]
, one can prove the aforementioned result of Zinger and Doan–Walpuski regarding counting embedded
J
-curves representing primitive homology classes without appealing to Gromov–Witten theory as in
[
28
]
or the delicate gluing analysis as from
[
6
]
, see also Remark
3.2
. The full power of Theorem
1.8
is used in Section
5
, which tells us ultimately we just need to worry about bifurcations from (branched) double covers with smooth domains in order to check the invariance of
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega)
.

The second key ingredient is a
sufficient
condition for bifurcations to occur. We construct Kuranishi models of moduli spaces of embedded curves based on a detailed analytical argument. The technical condition of “elementary wall type” includes all the cases in the construction of
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega)
, and in fact is satisfied by a large class of examples, see Examples
4.2
–
4.5
.

Theorem 1.9

(see Theorem
4.12
)
.

Let
\{J_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
be a generic path in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
. Assume that there exists an embedded rigid
J_{0}
-curve
\Sigma\hookrightarrow X
along with a
d
-fold genus
h
branched multiple cover
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
which has non-trivial normal deformations. If this cover determines an elementary wall type (see Definition
4.1
), then
\text{Aut}(\varphi)\subset\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and, moreover, the change in the signed count of embedded curves of genus
h
and class
d[\Sigma]
near
\varphi
is given by
\pm 2/|\text{Aut}(\varphi)|
.

The notion of “generic path” deserves some further comments. The work of Wendl
[
27
]
, recalled in Section
2.2
, specifies the codimension
1
subsets (‘‘walls”
4
4

4

These consist of almost complex structures violating super-rigidity (Definition
1.4
).
) of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
where a bifurcation could occur. Away from a subset of higher codimension, these subsets have manifold structures and we require that the path
\{J_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
only intersects this manifold locus and the intersection is transverse. However, this notion of transversality is
not
sufficient for our discussion. In fact, we define another subset of the walls with positive codimension, called the
degeneracy locus
, in Definition
4.29
and the path
\{J_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
is required to avoid the degeneracy locus as well. The condition of “elementary wall type” is needed in this step to guarantee the existence and abundance of such paths. Avoiding the degeneracy locus allows us to guarantee the non-vanishing of certain Taylor coefficients of the Kuranishi maps which leads to Theorem
1.9
.

For instance, suppose we have a path
\{J_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
and an embedded
J_{0}
-holomorphic torus
T\subset X
with
\ker(D_{T,J_{0}}^{N})=0
, i.e. the moduli space of
J_{0}
-curves is cut out transversely at
T
. Suppose there exists an unbranched holomorphic double cover from another torus
\varphi:T^{\prime}\to T
such that the normal deformation operator
\varphi^{*}D_{T,J_{0}}^{N}
satisfies
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{T,J_{0}}^{N})=1
.
Using Kuranishi reduction, one can show that the parametric moduli space of
J_{t}
-holomorphic genus
1
maps in class
2[T]
(near the
J_{0}
-holomorphic map
T^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}T\subset X
) is locally modeled on

x\cdot(ax^{2}+bt+\cdots)=0\text{ for some }a,b\in\mathbb{R}

(1.7)

for
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times[-1,1]
with a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action given by
(x,t)\to(-x,t)
. Wendl’s result allows us to ensure that
b\neq 0
by choosing the path
\{J_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
generically. However, this is
not
sufficient for our application. We argue that one can choose the path generically so that we have
a\neq 0
as well. When
a
and
b
are both non-zero, the higher order terms in (
1.7
) can be removed by a suitable coordinate change and the relevant moduli space here is indeed described by the “pitchfork bifurcation”.

We remark that our arguments fill in a technical gap in Taubes’ paper
[
24
, Lemma 5.18]
and the applicability of our result goes beyond the bifurcation coming from double unbranched covers of tori as in
[
24
]
. As far as we know, this is the first result studying equivariant bifurcations of pseudo-holomorphic curves in dimension
6
.

The above two ingredients give a complete characterization of the moduli spaces of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves of class
2A
and any genus along a generic path of almost complex structures connecting two given super-rigid almost complex structures. The third key ingredient is the construction of the correction terms in (
1.6
), which are called
linear wall crossing invariants
in Definition
5.17
. These correction terms are constructed by studying the spaces of
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operators on the normal bundles of embedded
J
-curves in a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold and they should be thought of as a version of “equivariant spectral flow”. This algebraic invariant is used to compensate the change in the signed count of embedded curves of class
2A
when a bifurcation happens and it serves as the last piece of the construction of
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega)
. We remark that these linear wall-crossing invariants only suffice for our definition of
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega)
. We plan to investigate generalization of these invariants for more general homology classes in future work. See
[
1
, Section 6.2]
for related constructions in a different context, in which more complicated corrections are presented due to the occurrence of more complicated bifurcations.

Constructions of this kind probably date back to Walker’s extension of the
{\text{SU}}(2)
-Casson invariant to rational homology
3
-spheres
[
26
]
and similar ideas appear in other literature of gauge theory, including the
{\text{SU}}(3)
-Casson invariants for integer homology
3
-spheres
[
2
]
and the Seiberg–Witten invariants for homology
S^{1}\times S^{3}

[
21
]
. Recent work of Eftekhary
[
12
]
applies similar ideas to a counting problem of periodic orbits. However, in our case, even the definition of the wall crossing numbers requires some work (see Section
5.1
).

1.3.
Relations with other work

There are some works in the literature (besides Taubes’ Gromov invariant) which have close relations with the current work.

1.3.1.
Gopakumar–Vafa invariants

Given a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold
(X,\omega)
, it was conjectured by Gopakumar–Vafa
[
13
]
that the Gromov–Witten generating function of
X
satisfies the formula

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\neq 0\\
g\geq 0\end{subarray}}\text{GW}_{A,g}(X)t^{2g-2}q^{A}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\neq 0\\
h\geq 0\end{subarray}}n_{A,h}(X)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\left(2\sin\left(\frac{kt}{2}\right)\right)^{2h-2}q^{kA},

(1.8)

where the “BPS numbers”
n_{A,h}(X)
are integers and
n_{A,h}(X)=0
for a fixed
A\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
if
h
is sufficiently large. The integrality the BPS numbers was proved by Ionel–Parker
[
16
]
and the finiteness has been established by the recent work of Doan–Ionel–Walpuski
[
5
]
.

However, Ionel–Parker’s proof of
n_{A,h}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}
does not illustrate the geometric meaning of these Gopakumar–Vafa invariants. Their proof proceeds as follows.
[
16
, Equation (8.1)]
shows that the Gromov–Witten generating function satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{A\neq 0,g\geq 0}\text{GW}_{A,g}(X)t^{2g-2}q^{A}=\sum_{A\neq 0,g\geq 0}e_{A,g}(X)\cdot\text{GW}^{\text{elem}}_{g}(q^{A},t)

(1.9)

where
\text{GW}^{\text{elem}}_{g}
is some universal generating series and
e_{A,g}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}
is regarded as a “
virtual count of embedded clusters in
X
”. They also show that
n_{A,h}(X)
and
e_{A,g}(X)
actually determine each other via the formula
[
16
, Equation (8.9)]

\displaystyle n_{A,h}(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=dB\\
B\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{g=0}^{h}n_{d,h}(g)\cdot e_{B,g}(X)

(1.10)

where
n_{d,h}(g)\in\mathbb{Z}
are certain universal coefficients determined by
d,h,g
. Therefore the integrality of
n_{A,h}(X)
is a result of integrality of
e_{A,g}(X)
.

Conjecture 1.10
.

The invariant
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega)
is equal to the virtual count of embedded clusters
e_{2A,h}
from (
1.9
) for all
h\geq 0
where
A
is a primitive homology class.

We show that Conjecture
1.10
is true for
h=0
, see Theorem
6.12
. The proof relies on a wall-crossing result of virtual Euler numbers, which might be of independent interest.

Theorem 1.11

(see Theorem
6.7
)
.

Suppose
N\to C
is a rank
2
complex vector bundle of degree
2g-2
on a curve
C
of genus
g
. Let
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
be a
1
-parameter family of Cauchy–Riemann operators on
N
. Fix integers
d\geq 2
,
h\geq g
and consider the moduli stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
of stable genus
h
maps to
C
of degree
d
. Assume further that
\mathcal{D}
is
(d,h)
-rigid (see Definition
6.2
) except over a finite set

\displaystyle\Delta\times\{0\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1]

(1.11)

corresponding to holomorphic maps with smooth domains and that
\mathcal{D}
is a simple wall-crossing of type
(d,h)
at
t=0
(see Definition
6.5
).
Then, for small
t\neq 0
, the assignment

\displaystyle[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\mapsto{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D_{t})

(1.12)

gives a well-defined vector bundle on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
equipped with a natural orientation.
5
5

5

The orientation here is defined using the modified determinant line (
6.8
) and differs from the usual orientation on (
1.12
) when
\text{sgn}(D_{0})=-1
. See Remark
6.4
for details.
Denote the Euler number of this bundle by
e_{d,h}(C,D_{\pm})
for small
\pm t>0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle e_{d,h}(C,D_{+})-e_{d,h}(C,D_{-})=\sum_{p\in\Delta}\frac{2\cdot\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)}{|\text{Aut}(p)|}

(1.13)

where the numbers
\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)\in\{-1,+1\}
are determined by the local behavior of
\mathcal{D}
near
p
.

The main obstacle to proving Conjecture
1.10
in general is to drop the assumption that
\Delta
is a finite subset and corresponds to stable maps with smooth domains in the above statement.

When
(X,\omega)
is further assumed to be a smooth projective variety, Maulik–Toda
[
19
]
propose a sheaf-theoretic definition of the Gopakumar–Vafa invariants, based on earlier works of Hosono–Saito–Takahashi
[
15
]
and Kiem–Li
[
18
]
. Despite the beauty of the definition, it is not known how to prove the deformation invariance of Maulik–Toda’s ansatz and the proof of formula (
1.8
) using this definition seems to be out of reach in general. Moreover, the sheaf-theoretic definition relies crucially on the algebraicity of
X
, making it hard to generalize it to general symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-folds (equipped with not necessarily integrable almost complex structures). Conjecture
1.10
proposes a geometric definition of the Gopakumar–Vafa invariants of two times a primitive homology class via the formula (
1.10
) by counting embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves.

Remark 1.12
.

Conjecture 7.4.5 in Cox–Katz’s book
[
4
]
conjectures that the genus
0
BPS numbers should come from virtual counts of embedded genus
0

J
-curves, with correction terms coming from genus
0

J
-curves of lower degree. Later on, Katz proposes an algebro-geometric counterpart of this conjecture in
[
17
]
using Donaldson–Thomas invariants. Our conjecture and result are consistent with these expectations.

1.3.2.
Counting calibrated submanifolds

For a given symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
and an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
, almost complex submanifolds, in particular embedded
J
-curves, define
calibrated submanifolds
of
X
. Another example of calibrated submanifolds are associative submanifolds of
7
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds with holonomy group
G_{2}
.

There is a proposal from Doan–Walpuski
[
7
]
which defines weights from counting solutions to gauge-theoretic equations on the associative submanifolds such that the weighted count of associatives defines an invariant. In this work, a symplectic definition of Pandharipande–Thomas invariant is discussed, based on a weighted count of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves with weights again coming from counts of gauge-theoretic equations.

Our formula (
1.5
) could also be viewed as a weighted count of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves which defines an invariant of
(X,\omega)
. This definition bypasses the difficulty coming from compactness issues of moduli spaces of generalized Seiberg–Witten equations which enters the proposal in
[
7
]
and has a closer relation with the Gromov–Witten invariants.

1.3.3.
Study of multi-valued sections

Given an embedded
J
-curve
C
in a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold and a holomorphic (branched) cover
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
, any non-zero element
s\in\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{C,J}^{N})
defines a
multi-valued section
of the normal bundle
N
of
C
in
X
by associating any point
x\in C
with the values of
s
over the set
\varphi^{-1}(x)
. Our bifurcation result (Theorem
1.9
) can be interpreted as a sufficient condition to perturb a (holomorphic) multi-valued section into a “nearby” embedded pseudo-holomorphic curve in
X
. Moreover, the discussion in Section
5.1
which is required for the definition of linear wall crossing numbers studies some generic behaviors of these multi-valued sections.

As speculated in
[
10
]
,
2
-valued harmonic
1
-forms are important for special Lagrangian geometry. Our study of multi-valued sections might have some applicability in this context, see Remark
4.13
and Remark
5.13
.

1.4.
Technical remarks concerning transversality arguments

To carry out the numerous generic transversality arguments in this paper (and in many other places in the theory of pseudo-holomorphic curves), one needs to appeal to the Sard–Smale theorem (for Fredholm maps of second countable
Banach
manifolds) after showing that certain universal moduli spaces are smooth (respectively,
C^{\infty}
subvarieties as in
[
27
, Appendix C]
) by showing that the linearized operators associated to their defining equations are surjective (respectively, satisfy a lower bound on their rank).

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be the map for which we wish to prove that the set
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{reg}}\subset\mathcal{Y}
of regular values is a Baire subset (with
\mathcal{Y}
being a space of smooth objects and both
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
being second countable). In our applications, this will be the space of smooth almost complex structures on a manifold or Cauchy–Riemann operators on a fixed bundle or spaces of paths/homotopies in the aforementioned spaces or, in our most complicated situation, a space of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves equipped with the data of a branched cover of the domain (Definition
4.29
). The first step of the proof is always to establish
formal regularity
, i.e., surjectivity (or rank lower bounds) for the linearized operators of the defining equations of
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathcal{Y}
at the level of smooth objects (i.e. without taking any Banach space completions or restricting to any Banach subspace).

Now, for most applications (including all the applications in this paper), one wishes to prove
C^{\infty}
-genericity statements (e.g. “simple
J
-holomorphic maps are unobstructed for a
C^{\infty}
-generic
J
”). However, the space
\mathcal{Y}
does not form a Banach manifold and thus, we cannot directly appeal to the Sard–Smale theorem. Fortunately, there is a well-known technique to overcome this difficulty (i.e. to upgrade formal regularity into genericity of regular values) which we briefly outline below. For the complete details and non-trivial examples of the application of this technique, the reader is referred to the very clear and detailed exposition in
[
27
]
(specifically the proofs of Theorems 5.26 and D therein). Going from formal regularity to the statement that regular values are generic involves showing that
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{reg}}
is a countable intersection of dense open subsets of
\mathcal{Y}
.

For this, we use the popular trick (due to Taubes) of exhausting
\mathcal{X}
by a sequence of subspaces
\mathcal{X}^{1}\subset\mathcal{X}^{2}\subset\cdots
such that
f|_{\mathcal{X}^{N}}:\mathcal{X}^{N}\to\mathcal{Y}
is proper for all
N
. We then define
\mathcal{Y}^{N}_{\text{reg}}\subset\mathcal{Y}
to be the subset of points
y\in\mathcal{Y}
for which every point in
f^{-1}(y)\cap\mathcal{X}^{N}
is regular. Now, since regularity is an open property on
\mathcal{X}
and
f|_{\mathcal{X}^{N}}
is proper, it follows that
\mathcal{Y}^{N}_{\text{reg}}
is open. As a consequence,
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{reg}}=\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{Y}^{N}_{\text{reg}}
is a countable intersection of open subsets of
\mathcal{Y}
. The exact construction of the subsets
\mathcal{X}^{N}\subset\mathcal{X}
is straightforward but somewhat tedious and we refer the reader to the proofs of Theorem 5.26 and Theorem D in
[
27
]
for details.

We are left to argue that each
\mathcal{Y}^{N}_{\text{reg}}\subset\mathcal{Y}
is dense. For this, we fix any reference point
y_{\text{ref}}\in\mathcal{Y}
introduce Floer’s
C_{\epsilon}
spaces centered at
y_{\text{ref}}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{Y}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})
. These spaces are Banach manifolds, consist entirely of smooth objects close to
y_{\text{ref}}
but carry a topology depending on the parameter
\epsilon
which is much finer than the
C^{\infty}
topology. For any
x_{\text{ref}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{ref}}=f^{-1}(y_{\text{ref}})
, formal regularity and the implicit function theorem imply that for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
, the space
\mathcal{X}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})=f^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}}))
is a Banach manifold (or
C^{\infty}
subvariety of expected codimension) near
x_{\text{ref}}
. By the second countability of
\mathcal{X}_{\text{ref}}
, we can choose a uniform
\epsilon
which works for all
x_{\text{ref}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{ref}}
. Thus, we obtain an open neighborhood
\mathcal{X}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})^{\text{reg}}
of
\mathcal{X}_{\text{ref}}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})
such that the restriction of
f
as a map
\mathcal{X}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})^{\text{reg}}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})
is a smooth map of Banach manifolds. Now, the Sard–Smale theorem shows that we can find a sequence
y_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})
of regular values of this map converging to
y_{\text{ref}}
in
\mathcal{Y}
. For any
N\geq 1
, the properness of
\mathcal{X}^{N}
over
\mathcal{Y}
and the openness of
\mathcal{X}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})^{\text{reg}}\subset\mathcal{X}_{\epsilon}(y_{\text{ref}})
now show that we must have
y_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}_{\text{reg}}
for all sufficiently large
k
(depending on
N
). Since
y_{\text{ref}}
was arbitrary, this shows that each
\mathcal{Y}^{N}_{\text{reg}}
is dense in
\mathcal{Y}
.

In view of the above, we adopt the convention of only proving formal regularity in all transversality proofs that arise in this paper (and omitting the use of Floer’s
C_{\epsilon}
spaces and Taubes’ trick to upgrade from formal regularity to genericity of regular values). Consistent with this, we use the notions of codimension of a subset of
\mathcal{Y}
(or a
C^{\infty}
subvariety of
\mathcal{Y}
), the index of the map
f
etc in this formal sense as a shorthand for the more detailed proof scheme above. The corresponding notions for maps of Banach manifolds have standard definitions (a subset is of codimension
\geq k
if it is covered by the images of countably many Fredholm maps with indices
\leq-k
;
C^{\infty}
subvarieties of codimension
\geq k
are defined as in
[
27
, Appendix C]
and we note that they are, in particular, subsets of codimension
\geq k
by
[
27
, Proposition C.1]
). The above formal notions will translate to the corresponding notions for Banach manifolds when we apply the implicit function theorem in the above proof scheme to a suitable
C_{\epsilon}
space.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

We end the introduction by outlining the structure of this paper. In Section
2
, we recall the necessary background on (twisted) Cauchy–Riemann operators and super-rigidity (following
[
27
]
). Section
3
establishes a necessary condition for bifurcations to occur (Theorem
3.1
) while Section
4
gives a sufficient condition (Theorem
4.12
). In Section
5
, we apply the results of the previous two sections to construct the extension of Taubes’ Gromov invariant for symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-folds (counting curves of genus
h\geq 0
lying in two times a primitive homology class) and prove the main result of this paper (Theorem
1.6
). Finally, in Section
6
, we prove a result on the virtual Euler numbers of certain obstruction bundles (Theorem
6.7
) which allows us to identify our invariant with the Gopakumar–Vafa invariant when the genus
h
is equal to
0
(Theorem
6.12
).

Conventions.

Unless otherwise stated, all vector spaces, vector bundles and their determinants are defined over
\mathbb{R}
. Dimensions, Hom-spaces and tensor products without any subscripts should be understood to be over
{\mathbb{R}}
.
\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
will denote
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
modulo torsion.

2.
Review of embedded curves and super-rigidity

In this section, we will summarize the background on super-rigidity in a form relevant for our discussion in sections
4
–
5
. The notation closely follows that of
[
27
, Section 2]
. We will begin by recalling some basic facts about branched covers of curves and Cauchy–Riemann operators (in §
2.1
) and then move onto super-rigidity (in §
2.2
).

2.1.
Cauchy–Riemann operators and Galois covers

We will consistently use the word ‘curve’ to mean ‘irreducible smooth projective algebraic curve over
\mathbb{C}
’ (i.e. a closed connected smooth Riemann surface). A ‘morphism’ between curves will mean a ‘non-constant holomorphic map’.

Definition 2.1

(Function field)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a curve. Define
\mathbb{C}(\Sigma)
to be the
function field
of
\Sigma
(i.e., the field of meromorphic functions on
\Sigma
). Given a degree
d\geq 1
morphism of curves
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
, we get the associated degree
d
field extension
\varphi^{*}:\mathbb{C}(\Sigma)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. It is a basic fact from algebraic geometry that this is a contravariantly functorial one-to-one correspondence (i.e. an equivalence of categories) between curves and fields of transcendence degree
1
over
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 2.2

(Galois closure)
.

Let
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
be a morphism of curves. We say that
\varphi
is
Galois
if and only if the associated field extension is Galois. In general,
\varphi
may not be Galois and we can form a Galois closure, a morphism
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Sigma
, by taking a Galois closure of the function field extension
\varphi^{*}:\mathbb{C}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{C}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. For a Galois cover
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
, we define the Galois group
\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
to be the group of automorphisms of
\tilde{\Sigma}
over
\Sigma
(or equivalently the Galois group of the associated function field extension).

Given a morphism
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
of curves, we call its critical points as
ramification points
and its critical values as
branch points
. The map
\varphi
restricts to a covering map with connected domain over the complement of (the pre-images of) the branch points, and this is called
the associated covering space
. It is a basic fact that
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
is Galois if and only if the associated covering space is normal. Moreover, the associated covering space of a Galois closure of
\varphi
is a normal closure of the associated covering space of
\varphi
.

Definition 2.3

(Branched covers and Galois groups)
.

Let the following data be given: integers
g\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
and
branching data

{\bf b}=({\bf b}_{1},\ldots,{\bf b}_{r})
where each
{\bf b}_{i}=(b_{i}^{1},\ldots,b_{i}^{q_{i}})
is a tuple of positive integers adding up to
d
with
b_{i}^{1}\geq\cdots\geq b_{i}^{q_{i}}
and
b_{i}^{1}\geq 2
. Define

\displaystyle b=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{q_{i}}(b_{i}^{j}-1)

(2.1)

and define
h
using the formula
2h-2=b+d(2g-2)
. Assume that
h
is a non-negative integer. Now, let
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
be a morphism of curves of
type

(g,{\bf b})
, i.e.,
h=\text{genus}(\Sigma^{\prime})
,
g=\text{genus}(\Sigma)
,
d=\deg(\varphi)
and
\varphi
has exactly
r
branched points
w_{1},\ldots,w_{r}\in\Sigma
with
\varphi^{-1}(w_{i})=\{\zeta_{i}^{1},\ldots,\zeta_{i}^{q_{i}}\}
and
\varphi
is
b_{i}^{j}
-to-
1
in a punctured neighborhood of each
\zeta_{i}^{j}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
. We define the
generalized automorphism group
of
\varphi
to be the Galois group of a Galois closure of
\varphi
. The isomorphism class of the generalized automorphism group is independent of the choice of Galois closure.

Given a finite group
G
and a genus
g
curve
\Sigma
, define
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
to be the orbifold of pairs
(\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
, up to isomorphism, where
\varphi
is a morphism of type
(g,{\bf b})
and
\iota
is an isomorphism of
G
with the generalized automorphism group of
\varphi
(defined using a Galois closure
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Sigma
). Under
\iota
,
\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma^{\prime})\subset\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
corresponds to
H\subset G
which is called
subgroup of
G
corresponding to
\varphi
. The orbifold structure is explained in Lemma
2.4
below.

Lemma 2.4
.

\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
is naturally an orbifold.

Proof.

A point of
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
explicitly consists of a morphism
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
of type
(g,{\bf b})
, a Galois closure
\tilde{\varphi}:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
for
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
(with a factorization
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
) and an isomorphism
\iota:G\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
. An isomorphism between points
(\varphi,\tilde{\varphi},\iota)
and
(\phi,\tilde{\phi},\hat{\iota})
is the data of an isomorphism of the Galois covers
\tilde{\varphi}
and
\tilde{\phi}
over
\Sigma
, which descends to an isomorphism between
\varphi
and
\phi
over
\Sigma
and is compatible with the isomorphisms
\iota
and
\hat{\iota}
.

To describe the orbifold structure of
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
, we show how to get local charts near any given point
(\varphi,\tilde{\varphi},\iota)
. Using
[
27
, Example 3.6]
, and the discussion immediately preceding it, we obtain a family
\tau\in B^{2r}\mapsto[\Sigma^{\prime}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{\tau}}\Sigma]
parametrizing all morphisms to
\Sigma
of type
(g,{\bf b})
near
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
. Further, by construction, this family comes with a (smooth) family of Galois closures
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}}\Sigma
(called
minimal regular presentations
in
[
27
]
). The given isomorphism
\iota:G\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
uniquely deforms to isomorphisms
\iota_{\tau}:G\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}/\Sigma)
. The family
\{(\varphi_{\tau},\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau},\iota_{\tau})\}_{\tau\in B^{2r}}
almost gives us the desired orbifold chart for
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
near
(\varphi,\tilde{\varphi},\iota)
except that it doesn’t account for the isomorphisms of
\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}/\Sigma)
with
G
other than
\iota_{\tau}
(as well as the action of the group of automorphisms of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\tau}/\Sigma
which descend to
\Sigma^{\prime}_{\tau}/\Sigma
on these data). Let
H\subset G
be the subgroup corresponding to
\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma^{\prime})\subset\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
under
\iota
. The group of automorphisms of
\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma
which descend to
\Sigma^{\prime}/\Sigma
gets identified under
\iota
with the normalizer
N_{G}(H)
of
H\subset G
.

Using
\{(\varphi_{\tau},\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau},\iota_{\tau})\}_{\tau\in B^{2r}}
, it now follows that
[(B^{2r}\times\text{Aut}(G))/N_{G}(H)]
provides a local orbifold chart for
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
near
(\varphi,\tilde{\varphi},\iota)
where
N_{G}(H)
acts on
B^{2r}
trivially and acts on the finite set
\text{Aut}(G)
via the group homomorphism
N_{G}(H)\to\text{Aut}(G)
which maps any
\gamma\in N_{G}(H)
to conjugation by
\gamma
. Observe that the orbifold structure on
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
is
purely ineffective
with every point having isotropy group isomorphic to the center
Z_{G}
of
G
. In particular, the underlying coarse space of the orbifold is actually a manifold, i.e., has no quotient singularities.
∎

We will next discuss Cauchy–Riemann operators on curves and their pullbacks along Galois covers. Denote by
E
a complex vector bundle over a curve
\Sigma
. Recall that an
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator
is a first-order real linear partial differential operator
D:\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E)\to\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,E)
such that for any smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
and section
s\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E)
, we have

D(fs)=(\overline{\partial}f)s+fD(s),

(2.2)

where
\overline{\partial}f=\frac{1}{2}(df+i\cdot df\circ j)
and
j
denotes the complex structure on
\Sigma
.

Definition 2.5

(Serre dual)
.

Let
D:\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E)\to\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,E)
be an
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator. Define the
Serre dual bundle
of
E
to be

\displaystyle E^{\dagger}=\Lambda^{1,0}T^{*}_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E^{*}

(2.3)

and the
Serre dual operator

D^{\dagger}
to be the
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator on
E^{\dagger}
determined by the following condition: for all
s\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E)
and
s^{\dagger}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E^{\dagger})
,

\displaystyle\text{Re}\left(\int_{\Sigma}\langle Ds,s^{\dagger}\rangle+\langle s,D^{\dagger}s^{\dagger}\rangle\right)=0.

(2.4)

In (
2.4
), the
\mathbb{C}
-linear pairing
\langle Ds,s^{\dagger}\rangle\in\Omega^{1,1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C})
between the sections
Ds\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,E)
and
s^{\dagger}\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E^{\dagger})=\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,E^{*})
is defined by the wedge product followed by the natural pairing between
E
and and its
\mathbb{C}
-linear dual
E^{*}
. The
\mathbb{C}
-linear pairing
\langle s,D^{\dagger}s^{\dagger}\rangle
between the sections
s\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E)
and
D^{\dagger}s^{\dagger}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,E^{\dagger})=\Omega^{1,1}(\Sigma,E^{*})
is defined analogously.

The
\mathbb{R}
-linear pairing
\text{Re}\int_{\Sigma}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
gives the
Serre duality
isomorphisms

\displaystyle\ker D^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=({\text{coker }}D)^{*}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\ker D

\displaystyle=({\text{coker }}D^{\dagger})^{*}.

(2.6)

Definition 2.6

(Twisted operators)
.

Let
D:\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,E)\to\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,E)
be an
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator on the curve
\Sigma
and let
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
be a Galois cover with Galois group
G=\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
. We can then form the pullback operator

\displaystyle\psi^{*}D:\Omega^{0}(\tilde{\Sigma},\psi^{*}E)\to\Omega^{0,1}(\tilde{\Sigma},\psi^{*}E)

(2.7)

which is
G
-equivariant with respect to the natural linear
G
-actions on the source and target. Let
\theta
be a linear
G
-representation over
\mathbb{R}
. We define the
twisted operator

D_{\psi,\theta}:\text{Hom}^{G}(\theta^{*},\Omega^{0}(\tilde{\Sigma},\psi^{*}E))\to\text{Hom}^{G}(\theta^{*},\Omega^{0,1}(\tilde{\Sigma},\psi^{*}E))

(2.8)

by applying the functor
\text{Hom}^{G}(\theta^{*},-)=(\theta\otimes-)^{G}
to (
2.7
).
The operator
D_{\psi,\theta}
is naturally linear over the endomorphism algebra
\mathbb{K}_{\theta}=\text{End}^{G}(\theta)
.

Remark 2.7
.

This definition is seen to be equivalent to the twisted operators from
[
27
, §2.2 and §3.4]
as follows. We may regard the elements of
\text{Hom}^{G}(\theta^{*},\Omega^{0}(\tilde{\Sigma},\psi^{*}E))=(\theta\otimes\Omega^{0}(\tilde{\Sigma},\psi^{*}E))^{G}
as sections of
(\tilde{\Sigma}\times V)/G
on the orbifold
\tilde{\Sigma}/G
. The natural map
\tilde{\Sigma}/G\to\Sigma
is an isomorphism except over the branch points. The quotient
(\tilde{\Sigma}\times V)/G
defines a vector bundle on the complement of the branch points and coincides with the twisted bundle of
[
27
]
. The advantage of our formulation over the one in
[
27
]
is that it avoids the need to introduce punctures and weighted Sobolev spaces.

Remark 2.8
.

Suppose
G\to G^{\prime}
is a quotient map between groups, corresponding to a factorization
\tilde{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Sigma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\psi^{\prime}}\Sigma
of
\psi
as the composition of two Galois covers
\varphi
,
\psi^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}=\text{Gal}(\Sigma^{\prime}/\Sigma)
. Also, suppose that
\theta^{\prime}
is a
G^{\prime}
-representation and that
\theta
is its pullback to
G
. Then,
\varphi^{*}
gives a natural map of complexes from
D_{\psi^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}}
to
D_{\psi,\theta}
and this map is seen to be a quasi-isomorphism (using the removable singularity theorem near the branch points of
\varphi
for
D
and
D^{\dagger}
). Similarly, if
H\subset G
is any subgroup and
\tilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}=\tilde{\Sigma}/H
with the associated morphism
\psi^{\prime}:\tilde{\Sigma}^{\prime}\to\Sigma
, then the natural pullback map from
(\psi^{\prime})^{*}D
to
(\psi^{*}D)^{H}
is also a quasi-isomorphism (i.e. an isomorphism on kernels and cokernels).

The following result, which appears in
[
9
, Remark 1.4.7]
in a slightly different form, will also be useful for the discussion below.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\theta_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,p
be an enumeration of all the irreducible representations of
G
over
\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbb{K}_{i}=\text{End}^{G}(\theta_{i})
. Then, the tautological map

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}\theta_{i}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}D_{\psi,\theta_{i}}\to\psi^{*}D

(2.9)

of
G
-equivariant two term complexes is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

This is because for any finite dimensional
\mathbb{R}[G]
-module
V
, the tautological map
\bigoplus_{i}\theta_{i}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}\text{Hom}^{G}(\theta_{i}^{*},V)\to V
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}[G]
-modules by Maschke’s theorem. We apply this to the (co)kernels of the operators in question.
∎

Given a family of curves and a corresponding family of Cauchy–Riemann operators, we introduce the following definition which measures the variation of the Cauchy–Riemann operators on a particular finite-dimensional vector space. It will be used later in the analysis of Kuranishi maps.

Let
\pi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{B}
be a smooth family of curves, i.e.,
\pi
is a proper submersion between two manifolds
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{B}
with fibers of
\mathbb{R}
-dimension
2
along with a smooth endomorphism
j
of
\ker(d\pi)=T_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}}
satisfying
j^{2}=-1
. Let
E\to\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-vector bundle and set

\displaystyle F=\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B},j}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}E.

(2.10)

Assume that we have a smooth family
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{b}\}_{b\in\mathcal{B}}
of Cauchy–Riemann operators

\displaystyle D_{b}:\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{b},E_{b})\to\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{b},F_{b})

(2.11)

where
\mathcal{C}_{b}=\pi^{-1}(b)
,
E_{b}=E|_{\mathcal{C}_{b}}
and
F_{b}=F|_{\mathcal{C}_{b}}
for
b\in\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 2.10
.

For any point
a\in\mathcal{B}
, define the
linearized map
at
a
associated to the family
\mathcal{D}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|_{a}:T_{a}\mathcal{B}\to\text{Hom}(\ker D_{a},{\text{coker }}D_{a})

(2.12)

as follows. Given
\sigma\in\ker D_{a}
, extend it arbitrarily to a section
\tilde{\sigma}\in\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C},E)
. Then, the section
\tilde{\tau}=\mathcal{D}(\tilde{\sigma})\in\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C},F)
defined by
\tilde{\tau}|_{\mathcal{C}_{b}}=D_{b}(\tilde{\sigma}|_{\mathcal{C}_{b}})
for
b\in\mathcal{B}
vanishes along
\mathcal{C}_{a}
and thus, has a well-defined normal derivative

\displaystyle\nabla\tilde{\tau}|_{a}\in\text{Hom}(N_{\mathcal{C}_{a}/\mathcal{C}},F_{a})=\text{Hom}(T_{a}\mathcal{B},\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{a},F_{a}))

(2.13)

where we have used the identification
N_{\mathcal{C}_{a}/\mathcal{C}}=\pi^{*}(T_{a}\mathcal{B})
in the last equality. Now, for any
v\in T_{a}\mathcal{B}
we set
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|_{a}(v)\cdot\sigma\in{\text{coker }}D_{a}
to be the projection of the section
\nabla\tilde{\tau}|_{a}\cdot v\in\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{a},F_{a})
.

The operator (
2.12
) is well-defined because if
\sigma=0
, then any choice of
\tilde{\sigma}
has a well-defined normal derivative

\displaystyle\nabla\tilde{\sigma}|_{a}\in\text{Hom}(N_{\mathcal{C}_{a}/\mathcal{C}},E_{a})=\text{Hom}(T_{a}\mathcal{B},\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{a},E_{a}))

(2.14)

which satisfies
D_{a}(\nabla\tilde{\sigma}|_{a}\cdot v)=\nabla\tilde{\tau}|_{a}\cdot v
for all
v\in T_{a}\mathcal{B}
.
Note that if a finite group
G
acts compatibly on
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
E
,
F
, with the action on
\mathcal{B}
being trivial, such that the family
\mathcal{D}
is
G
-equivariant, then (
2.12
) factors through
\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker D_{a},{\text{coker }}D_{a})
.

Remark 2.11
.

It can be seen from Definition
2.10
that we can define the linearized map
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|_{a}
by first choosing suitable trivializations to regard
\mathcal{D}
as a family of
G
-equivariant operators

\displaystyle b\mapsto D_{b}:\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{a},E_{a})\to\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{a},F_{a})

(2.15)

and setting
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|_{a}(v)\cdot\sigma
to be the projection of
\left[\frac{d}{dt}D_{\gamma(t)}\sigma|_{t=0}\right]
onto
{\text{coker }}D_{a}
, where
\gamma
is any smooth path in
\mathcal{B}
with
\gamma(0)=a
and
\dot{\gamma}(0)=v
.

2.2.
Super-rigidity

For the remainder of this section, we assume that the manifold
X
is a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold with symplectic form
\omega
and, moreover, all almost complex structures under consideration will be assumed to be
\omega
-compatible.

Fix
g,d,\bf b
as in Definition
2.3
and a finite group
G
. Now, given
A\in\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, define the universal moduli space

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)

(2.16)

to consist of tuples
(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
up to isomorphism where
J\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
,
\Sigma\subset X
is an embedded
J
-curve of genus
g
in class
A
and
\varphi
is a branched cover of type
(g,\bf b)
along with an isomorphism
\iota
between
G
and its generalized automorphism group (this space becomes a purely ineffective smooth Banach orbifold if we impose appropriate
C_{\epsilon}
regularity conditions as explained in §
1.4
). Given any such tuple, we can then form the associated Galois closure
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
and consider the
G
-equivariant operator
\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
. Its kernel and cokernel are finite dimensional real
G
-representations.

Definition 2.12

(Loci of failure of super-rigidity)
.

Given two nonzero finite dimensional
G
-representations
\bf k
and
\bf c
over
\mathbb{R}
, define the universal moduli space

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)

(2.17)

to be the subset of
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))
consisting of those tuples
(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
, with associated Galois cover
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
, for which we have isomorphisms
\ker\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}\simeq\bf k
and
{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}\simeq\bf c
of
G
-representations.

Remark 2.13
.

Observe that if the domain of (
2.17
) is non-empty, then
\bf k
must in fact be a
G
-sub-representation of
\bf c
and, therefore,
\text{Hom}^{G}({\bf k},{\bf c})\neq 0
. Indeed, when we decompose
\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
as in Lemma
2.9
, each twisted operator appearing in the decomposition has index
\leq 0
by
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
.

We now state the version of
[
27
, Theorem D]
that we will need for the rest of the article. Some immediate corollaries are also explained below.

Theorem 2.14

(Stratification)
.

In the setting of Definition
2.12
, away from an exceptional subset of infinite codimension of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, the subspace

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})\subset\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))

(2.18)

is a sub-orbifold
6
6

6

Again, as explained in §
1.4
, this should be interpreted to mean that we get a Banach sub-orbifold after passing to suitable
C_{\epsilon}
completions.
of codimension given by
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\text{Hom}^{G}({\bf k},{\bf c})
.

Remark 2.15
.

We explain in detail why our version (Theorem
2.14
) follows from the somewhat differently formulated
[
27
, Theorem D]
. Arguing exactly as in the proof of Lemma
2.4
, we find that the natural forgetful map from our universal moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))
to Wendl’s universal moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{\bf b}(\mathcal{M}_{g,m}(A;\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m}))
, with
m=\ell_{1}=\cdots=\ell_{m}=0
, is a finite-to-one local diffeomorphism. The finite fibers correspond to the different choices of isomorphism between the fixed group
G
and the generalized automorphism groups of the covers in question.

Keeping track of this explicit isomorphism allows us to globally define the loci
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
for a pair of non-trivial
G
-representations
{\bf k},{\bf c}
as in Definition
2.12
. Since
[
27
]
doesn’t record the isomorphism of the generalized automorphism group with
G
explicitly, the analogous spaces
P({\bf k},{\bf c})
are defined only locally (see
[
27
, Section 3.5]
for their definition). It is then proved in
[
27
, Lemma 3.24]
that
P({\bf k},{\bf c})
is locally characterized by constancy of the dimension of the kernel of the normal deformation operator pulled back to a Galois closure. By Remark 2.14 and the discussion immediately following Definition 6.1 in
[
27
]
, we see that
P({\bf k},{\bf c})
are exactly the “walls” to which the codimension formula of
[
27
, Theorem D]
applies. Finally, observe that the codimension formula (introduced in
[
27
, Definition 2.11]
) coincides with the dimension of
\text{Hom}^{G}({\bf k},{\bf c})
. In fact, for any point
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
lying in the domain of (
2.17
), the vector spaces
\text{Hom}^{G}({\bf k},{\bf c})
and
\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J},{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J})
are isomorphic, where
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
is the associated Galois cover as in Definition
2.12
. The latter vector space is the normal space to the inclusion (
2.18
) at
p
.

The exceptional subset of infinite codimension referenced in the statement of Theorem
2.14
is explained in the following remark. We conclude that Theorem
2.14
is indeed a consequence of
[
27
, Theorem D]
.

Remark 2.16
.

In the statement of Theorem
2.14
, we are using the fact that the Petri condition (see
[
27
, Section 5]
for more details) holds on
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
away from an exceptional subset which has infinite codimension. In practice, we can ignore this exceptional set as in the next corollary.

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
P
be a second countable manifold and let
\mathcal{J}_{P}(X,\omega)
denote the space of smooth
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures parametrized by
P
with the
C^{\infty}
topology. Let
\mathcal{P}=\{J_{s}\}_{s\in P}
be an element in
\mathcal{J}_{P}(X,\omega)
for which the associated map
P\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
is transverse to the forgetful map (
2.17
) over a closed submanifold
Q\subset P
. Define
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)_{\mathcal{P},Q}
to be the space of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{P}(X,\omega)
which coincide with
\mathcal{P}
over
Q\subset P
. Then, the subset of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)_{\mathcal{P},Q}
consisting of those
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for which the associated map
P\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
is transverse to (
2.17
) is a Baire set, and therefore, dense.

Definition 2.18

(Wall)
.

For
\delta=(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
as in Definition
2.12
, in particular with
{\bf k}
and
{\bf c}
being nonzero
G
-representations, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)

(2.19)

to be the image of the forgetful map (
2.17
). Define the
wall

\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
to be the set of all
J
which are not super-rigid. We then have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}\cup\bigcup_{\delta}\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})

(2.20)

by passing to Galois covers in Definition
1.4
. We refer to each term in the union in (
2.20
) as a
stratum
of the wall
\mathcal{W}
.

Remark 2.19
.

Even though we find it convenient to occasionally speak of the wall strata as if they were submanifolds of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, in practice we will be using the maps (
2.17
) rather than their images (the wall strata) in all our arguments. For example, when we discuss transversality of a path in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
to a wall stratum, we actually mean that the path is transverse to the corresponding map (
2.17
). Similarly, when we speak of the normal space to the wall inside
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
at a point, we will actually mean the normal space of the map (
2.17
) at a point where it is an immersion.

We will now determine a way to separate
\mathcal{W}
into those strata which have codimension
1
in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
and those which have codimension
\geq 2
. We do this essentially by rewording the proof of
[
27
, Theorem A]
for
X
and following the remarks from
[
27
, §2.4]
.

Lemma 2.20

(Wall: codimension 1 strata)
.

Away from a codimension
2
subset of
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, the codimension
1
stratum of
\mathcal{W}\subset J(X,\omega)
is a union of those strata
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})
which are non-empty such that

(i)

each
b_{i}^{j}\in\{1,2\}
, i.e., each ramification point is simple and,

(ii)

the
G
-representation
\bf k
is faithful, irreducible and of real type, i.e., its equivariant endomorphism algebra
\text{End}^{G}({\bf k})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Begin by noting that
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
is of codimension
2
in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
. Now, take
J
in
\mathcal{W}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
and not in the exceptional set from Theorem
2.14
. This means that we can find a minimum possible
d\geq 1
, an embedded
J
-curve
\Sigma\subset X
and a
d
-fold branched cover
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
such that
\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}\neq 0
. Let
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
be a Galois closure of
\varphi
and let
G=\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma)
while
H=\text{Gal}(\tilde{\Sigma}/\Sigma^{\prime})
. Let
\theta_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,p
denote the complete list of irreducible representations of
G
over
\mathbb{R}
. For each
i
, recall that we have the twisted operators
D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}
, as in
[
27
, §2.2 and §3.4]
and Definition
2.6
, whose kernels (and similarly cokernels) are related to
\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
by the following formulas

\displaystyle\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}

\displaystyle=\text{Hom}^{G}(\theta_{i}^{*},\ker\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J})

(2.21)

\displaystyle\ker\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i}\theta_{i}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}

(2.22)

where
\mathbb{K}_{i}=\text{End}^{G}(\theta_{i})
, and (
2.21
), (
2.22
) are isomorphisms of
\mathbb{K}_{i}
-modules and
G
-representations respectively. Now, the assumption implies that we can find an
1\leq j\leq p
such that
(\theta_{j}^{*})^{H}\neq 0
and
\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{j}}\neq 0
. We contend that
\theta_{j}
must be a faithful
G
-representation. If not, let
(1)\neq K\lhd G
be such that
\theta_{j}
descends to
G/K
. Note that
K\not\subset H
(since
H
has trivial normal core) and thus,
D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
pulled back to
\tilde{\Sigma}/HK
has a non-trivial kernel (contradicting the minimality of
d
). Now that we know that
\theta_{i}
is faithful, we can use
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
to get

\displaystyle\text{ind}_{\mathbb{K}_{j}}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{j}}\leq-2r

(2.23)

where
r
is the number of distinct branch points of
\varphi
. Moreover, this estimate is strict in case
\mathbb{K}_{j}=\mathbb{R}
unless all the ramification points of
\varphi
are simple (i.e. they’re all locally
2
-to-
1
). Now, set
t_{i}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{K}_{i}
,
k_{i}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}
and
c_{i}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}
and note that the codimension of
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})
near
J
is

\displaystyle\geq-2r+\sum_{i}t_{i}k_{i}c_{i}\geq-2r+t_{j}k_{j}(k_{j}+2r)\geq 1

(2.24)

using Theorem
2.14
. Note that the shifting
-2r
comes from the dimension of the moduli space of branched covers. (Here,
\bf b
is constructed using
\varphi
,
A=[\Sigma]
,
g=\text{genus}(\Sigma)
,
{\bf k}=\ker\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
and
{\bf c}={\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
.) Moreover, to obtain the equality in the last estimate, we must have
t_{j}=k_{j}=1
,
k_{i}=0
for all
j\neq i
(this last part follows since
\text{ind}_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}\leq 0
by
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
) and
\text{ind}_{\mathbb{K}_{j}}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{j}}=-2r
.
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
now implies that all the ramification points of
\varphi
must be locally
2
-to-
1
.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

The reader may observe that in the proof of Lemma
2.20
we concluded that the branched cover in question must have
simple ramification
using
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
which seems to make the stronger claim of
simple branching
. This is a result of a difference in terminology. Indeed, the proof of
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
(given in
[
27
, Cororally 4.2]
) shows that near each ramification point the cover is locally
2
-to-
1
and but
does not show that there is a unique ramification point lying over each branch point
. Example
4.7
exhibits some branched covers with simple ramification (but not simple branching) allowed by Lemma
2.20
.

3.
A necessary condition for bifurcations

In this section, we will describe a necessary condition for a sequence of simple pseudo-holomorphic curves (defined with respect to a sequence of almost complex structures) to converge to a multiple cover of an embedded curve.

We emphasize that the main result of this section (Theorem
3.1
) is independent of
[
27
]
and holds for almost complex manifolds in any dimension.

Fix an even dimensional manifold
X^{2n}
for the rest of this section. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:V\to\mathcal{J}(X)

(3.1)

be a smooth family of almost complex structures on
X
parametrized by a smooth finite dimensional manifold
V
(i.e.
\mathcal{F}
defines a smooth section
\pi_{X}^{*}\text{End}(T_{X})
with
\mathcal{F}^{2}=-\text{id}
with
\pi_{X}:V\times X\to X
being the projection). Given a smooth projective curve
C
, denote by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)
the moduli stack of genus
h
stable maps of degree
k
with target
C
.

Theorem 3.1

(Necessary condition for bifurcation)
.

Let
x_{\nu}\to x
be a convergent sequence in
V
, with
J_{\nu}:=\mathcal{F}(x_{\nu})
for
\nu\geq 0
and
J:=\mathcal{F}(x)
. Let
h\geq 0
be an integer and suppose that we have a sequence
(J_{\nu},\varphi_{\nu}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{\nu}\to X)
of simple
J_{\nu}
-curves of genus
h
converging, in the Gromov topology, to a stable map

\displaystyle(J,C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}C\subset X)

(3.2)

with
C
being a smooth embedded
J
-curve of genus
g\leq h
and
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
being an element of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)
for some integer
k\geq 1
. Then, exactly one of the following must be true.

(i)

We have
g=h
,
k=1
and
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

The natural pullback map

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}:\ker D^{N}_{C,J}\to\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}

(3.3)

is injective but not surjective.

The alternative (
ii
) provides a necessary condition for bifurcations of the moduli spaces of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves to occur. Before proceeding to the proof, we make a few remarks comparing this to similar statements in the literature and point out a natural extension of this result.

Remark 3.2
.

(a)

If we further suppose that there exists a sequence
\{C_{\nu}\}
of embedded
J_{\nu}
-curves of genus
g
converging to
C
(this is ensured, for example, if
D^{N}_{C,J}
is surjective), then the rescaling argument used to prove
[
27
, Proposition B.1]
yields the weaker conclusion
\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}\neq 0
.

(b)

Theorem
3.1
rules out the possibility of a sequence of simple curves of genus
h
converging in the Gromov topology to an embedded curve of genus
g<h
with some ghost components attached. In particular, if
A\in\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
is a primitive homology class in a
6
-dimensional symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
with
c_{1}(A)=0
, then for any path
\gamma
in
\mathcal{J}_{\text{emb}}(X,\omega)
(in the sense of
[
6
, Definition 2.36]
), the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{h}^{\text{emb}}(X,\gamma,A)
of embedded genus
h
curves of class
A
is compact. This can be used to recover
[
6
, Theorem 1.5(1)]
.

(c)

The restriction in Theorem
3.1
to finite dimensional families of almost complex structures suffices for all applications we are aware of but it should be possible to extend our proof to the infinite dimensional case as well. We would have to replace the appeal to Brouwer’s Invariance of Domain (as in §
3.5
) by a more careful analysis of the differential of the inclusion from Lemma
3.8
. To deal with the lack of differentiability of the moduli space near curves with nodal domains, one would have to use suitable relative tangent bundles associated to canonical “rel–
C^{\infty}
structures” on the moduli spaces as in
[
23
]
.

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.1
. The main idea of the argument is given in §
3.1
and involves counting dimensions of moduli spaces. However, there are some issues related to transversality which we need to overcome before this outline can be turned into a rigorous proof. In §
3.2
–§
3.4
, we show how to tackle these issues and complete the proof in §
3.5
.

3.1.
Outline of the argument

Set
A=[C]\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F},A)

(3.4)

be the moduli space of pairs
(y,\Sigma)
, where
y\in V
and
\Sigma\subset X
is an embedded
\mathcal{F}(y)
-curve of genus
g
in class
A
. Also consider the moduli space

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(X,\mathcal{F},kA)

(3.5)

consisting of pairs
(y,\varphi^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X)
, where
y\in V
and
\varphi^{\prime}
is a stable
\mathcal{F}(y)
-holomorphic map in
X
of genus
h
and class
k[C]
and the moduli space

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F},A),k)

(3.6)

consisting of triples
(y,\Sigma,\psi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma)
, where
(y,\Sigma)
lies in (
3.4
) and
(\Sigma^{\prime},\psi)
lies in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\Sigma,k)
. There is a natural inclusion of (
3.6
) inside (
3.5
), given by

\displaystyle(y,\Sigma,\psi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma)\mapsto(y,\Sigma^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\psi}\Sigma\subset X),

(3.7)

and the proof of Theorem
3.1
will follow by showing that codimension of (
3.6
) inside (
3.5
) at the point
(x,C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}C\subset X)
is given by the dimension of the cokernel of (
3.3
). However, both (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) are not smooth in general therefore the notion of codimension is not well-defined. To remedy the situation, we will need to thicken the moduli spaces (in the sense of
[
22
, Definition 9.2.3]
) compatibly so that (
3.7
) becomes an inclusion of manifolds, at least near the point
(x,\varphi)
, whose codimension can then be computed.

3.2.
Preparation

Lemma 3.3

(Transversality for embedded curves)
.

We can find a smooth manifold
V^{\prime}
containing
V
as a submanifold and a smooth family
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\to\mathcal{J}(X)
extending
\mathcal{F}
such that the moduli space

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)

(3.8)

is a smooth manifold of the expected dimension
=\dim V^{\prime}+\text{ind }D^{N}_{C,J}
near
(x,C)
.

Proof.

This is a variant of the argument in
[
20
, Proposition 3.2.1]
. Indeed, by the implicit function theorem, it suffices to arrange that the linearized map

\displaystyle T_{x}V^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}

(3.9)

\displaystyle v^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto\textstyle\frac{1}{2}(d\mathcal{F}^{\prime}|_{x}\cdot v^{\prime})\circ d\iota\circ j

(3.10)

is surjective, where
\iota
denotes the inclusion
C\subset X
and
j
denotes the restriction of
J
to
T_{C}
. The right side of (
3.10
) is an element of
\Omega^{0,1}(C,T_{X}|_{C})
which we are projecting to
\Omega^{0,1}(C,N_{C})
and then to
{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}
. Note that the restriction map

\displaystyle T_{J}\mathcal{J}=\overline{\text{End}}_{J}(T_{X})\to\overline{\text{Hom}}_{\mathbb{C}}(T_{C},N_{C})=\Omega^{0,1}(C,N_{C})

(3.11)

is surjective and thus, we may lift a (finite) basis of
{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}
to
T_{J}\mathcal{J}
. It is now easy to define
V\subset V^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
extending
\mathcal{F}
such that (
3.9
) is surjective.
∎

Before proceeding further, we pick some auxiliary data.

(1)

Choose a Riemannian metric on
X
such that we can identify the unit disc bundle in
N_{C}
with a tubular neighborhood of
C\subset X
, with fiber over
p\in C
denoted by
\Delta_{p}
(a codimension
2
submanifold with boundary of
X
).

(2)

Choose an integer
m\geq 0
and finitely many points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in C
(which are not the images of critical points of
\varphi
or nodal points of
C^{\prime}
) such that

(a)

(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
is a stable curve and,

(b)

the evaluation map

\displaystyle\ker D^{N}_{C,J}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}N_{C,p_{i}}

(3.12)

is injective.

Note that for all
(y,\Sigma)
in (
3.4
) close to
(x,C)
, the curve
\Sigma
intersects each
\Delta_{i}:=\Delta_{p_{i}}
transversally at a unique point (and is disjoint from
\partial\Delta_{i}
). The submanifolds
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}
will serve as “stabilizing divisors” in the sense of
[
22
, Lemma 9.2.8]
.

(3)

Choose a complex manifold
\mathscr{M}
and a complex analytic family
\pi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{M}
of genus
g
curves with sections
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
(corresponding to
m
marked points which stabilize the fibers), equipped with a basepoint
*\in\mathscr{M}
and an isomorphism

\displaystyle i:(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathscr{C}_{*},\sigma_{1}(*),\ldots,\sigma_{m}(*))

(3.13)

such that the resulting map
\mathscr{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{g,m}
is étale at
*
(i.e. provides a local orbifold chart near
*
). Here,
\mathscr{C}_{s}
denotes the curve
\pi^{-1}(s)
for
s\in\mathscr{M}
. The choice of the family
(\pi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{M},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m})
is analogous to choosing a local Teichmüller slice at
(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
.

(4)

For
1\leq i\leq m
, choose a numbering
\varphi^{-1}(p_{i})=\{q_{i}^{1},\ldots,q_{i}^{k}\}
. Note that each
q_{i}^{j}
must be a smooth point of
C^{\prime}
with
d\varphi(q_{i}^{j})\neq 0
. Moreover,
(C^{\prime},\{q_{i}^{j}\})
is a stable curve. Choose a complex manifold
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
and a (flat) complex analytic family
\pi^{\prime}:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
of (possibly nodal) arithmetic genus
h
curves with sections
\tau_{i}^{j}
(corresponding to
km
marked points which stabilize the fibers), equipped with a basepoint
\bullet\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
and an isomorphism

\displaystyle i^{\prime}:(C^{\prime},\{q_{i}^{j}\})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{\bullet},\{\sigma_{i}^{j}(\bullet)\})

(3.14)

such that the resulting map
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h,km}
is étale at
\bullet
(i.e. provides a local orbifold chart near
\bullet
).

(5)

Let us denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\times X

(3.15)

the universal embedded curve. As a subset, it is given by the union of the subsets
\{(y,\Sigma)\}\times\Sigma
over all
(y,\Sigma)\in\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)
. The first projection of (
3.15
) defines a smooth family of curves (i.e., Riemann surfaces) over
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)
and thus, there is a smooth classifying map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

(3.16)

defined near
(x,C)
which maps
(y,\Sigma)\in\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)
to the point of
\mathscr{M}
corresponding to
(\Sigma,\Sigma\cap\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Sigma\cap\Delta_{m})
and specializes to the isomorphism (
3.13
) over
(x,C)
.

Remark 3.4
.

All the assertions for the remainder of the argument are to be understood to hold only when we are sufficiently close to the basepoints
(x,C)
,
(x,C,\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C)
,
(x,C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}C\subset X)
,
*
and
\bullet
. The size of these neighborhoods may shrink from one line to the next.

Lemma 3.5

(Auxiliary embedding)
.

The map

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\to V^{\prime}\times\Delta_{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{m}

(3.17)

defined in a neighborhood of
(x,C)
by

\displaystyle(y,\Sigma)\mapsto(y,\Sigma\cap\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Sigma\cap\Delta_{m})

(3.18)

is a smooth embedding near
(x,C)
.

Proof.

By the inverse function theorem, it will suffice to check that the differential

\displaystyle T_{(x,C)}\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\to T_{x}V^{\prime}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}N_{C,p_{i}}

(3.19)

of (
3.17
) at
(x,C)
is injective. This follows from the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\ker D^{N}_{C,J}\to T_{(x,C)}\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\to T_{x}V^{\prime}

(3.20)

and the fact that (
3.12
) is injective.
∎

3.3.
Thickened moduli space of covers

As an intermediate step to tackling (
3.6
), we first study the moduli space

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{M},k)

(3.21)

which consists of pairs
(s,\psi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\mathscr{C}_{s})
, where
s\in\mathscr{M}
and
(\Sigma^{\prime},\psi)
is in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{C}_{s},k)
.
7
7

7

Recall that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{C}_{s},k)
consists of holomorphic genus
h
stable maps
\psi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\mathscr{C}_{s}
in the class
k[\mathscr{C}_{s}]
. Observe that
\mathscr{C}_{s}
is smooth but
\Sigma^{\prime}
could be nodal. If
\Sigma^{\prime}
has a ghost component with positive genus, then
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{M},k)
will not be a smooth orbifold near
(s,\psi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\mathscr{C}_{s})
. In view of this, we need to thicken the moduli space by considering (
3.27
).
We note that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A),k)=\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\times_{\mathscr{M}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{M},k)

(3.22)

where
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{M},k)\to\mathscr{M}
is the natural projection while
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\to\mathscr{M}
is the classifying map from (
3.16
). Since
\pi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{M}
is a local universal family for
\mathcal{M}_{g,m}
at
*
, the identification (
3.22
) follows by observing that the pair
(\Phi_{(y,\Sigma)},\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)})
of classifying maps from (
3.16
) is completely determined for all
(y,\Sigma)
near
(x,C)
once we specify that it agrees with the isomorphism (
3.13
) for
(y,\Sigma)=(x,C)
and that it is induced by the stable pointed curve obtained by intersecting
\Sigma
with
\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{m}
. Now, let

\displaystyle D\bar{\partial}|_{\varphi}:C^{\infty}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{C})\to C^{\infty}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\Omega^{0,1}_{\tilde{C}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}T_{C})

(3.23)

be the linearized Cauchy--Riemann operator
8
8

8

See the discussion in Remark
1.5
for more details on how to define Cauchy–Riemann operators on nodal curves.
associated to the holomorphic map
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
, where
\tilde{C}^{\prime}
is the normalization of the curve
C^{\prime}
. Choose
E
to be a finite dimensional vector space and

\displaystyle\lambda:E\to C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathscr{C},\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}T_{\mathscr{C}/\mathscr{M}})

(3.24)

to be a linear map (where
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is the complement of the nodal points of the fibers of
\pi^{\prime}
), with
\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)\to\mathscr{M}^{\prime}\times\mathscr{M}
being a proper map, such that its restriction along the map
C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times\mathscr{C}
given by
i^{\prime}\times(i\circ\varphi)
yields an isomorphism

\displaystyle E={\text{coker }}D\bar{\partial}|_{\varphi}.

(3.25)

Now introduce the moduli space

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{M},k)_{\lambda}

(3.26)

consisting of tuples
(s\in\mathscr{M},s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime},\psi:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{C}_{s},e\in E)
satisfying the
\lambda
-thickened Cauchy–Riemann equation (in the sense of
[
22
, Definition 9.2.3]
)

\bar{\partial}\psi+\lambda(e)(\cdot,\psi(\cdot))=0

(3.27)

and incidence conditions

\psi(\tau_{i}^{j}(s^{\prime}))=\sigma_{i}(s)\quad\text{transverse}

(3.28)

for each
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq k
. The word
transverse
in (
3.28
) indicates that
d\psi|_{\tau_{i}^{j}(s^{\prime})}\neq 0
.

Remark 3.6
.

The linearization of (
3.27
) at
(*,\bullet,i\circ\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0)
is identified, under the isomorphisms
i
and
i^{\prime}
, with the operator

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{C})\oplus E\to C^{\infty}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\Omega^{0,1}_{\tilde{C}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}T_{C})

(3.29)

which, on the first summand, is the operator
D\bar{\partial}|_{\varphi}
from (
3.23
) and, on the second summand, is the restriction of
\lambda
along the map
C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times\mathscr{C}
given by
i^{\prime}\times(i\circ\varphi)
. We emphasize that since the linearization is computed at a point with vanishing
E
-coordinate, no covariant derivatives of the
\lambda
-term (arising from variations in the
\psi
-coordinate) appear in the linearization. In particular, the linearization
completely ignores
the values of
\lambda
outside the image of
C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times\mathscr{C}
. The isomorphism (
3.25
) now shows that (
3.29
) is surjective.

It now follows from
[
22
