, Proposition 9.2.6 & Appendix B]
that (
3.26
), near the point
(*,\bullet,i\circ\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0)
, is a topological manifold of the expected dimension with the projection to
\mathscr{M}
being a topological submersion. More explicitly, the dimension of (
3.26
) relative to
\mathscr{M}
is
=\text{vdim }\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)+\dim E
where we have set

\displaystyle\text{vdim }\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)=2r

(3.30)

with
r=(2h-2)-k(2g-2)
being the expected complex dimension of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)
.

We can now define the
\lambda
-thickened moduli space

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A),k)_{\lambda}=\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\times_{\mathscr{M}}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathscr{M},k)_{\lambda}

(3.31)

which is also a topological manifold, near the point
(x,C,\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C,0)
. Moreover, its natural projection to
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)
is a topological submersion of relative dimension
2r+\dim E
near this point.
The loci in (
3.26
) and (
3.31
) where the
E
-coordinate vanishes respectively form local étale charts for the orbispaces (
3.21
) and the analogue of (
3.6
) with
\mathcal{F}
replaced by
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.

Pulling back (
3.24
) along (
3.16
), we obtain a thickening datum given by a linear map

\displaystyle E\to C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A),\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}T_{\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)/\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)})

(3.32)

which we continue to denote by the same symbol
\lambda
. We record here, for later use, that we have an embedding

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)

(3.33)

given by
i^{\prime}\times((x,C)\times\varphi)
where we are identifying
\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)
with a subset of
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)\times X
as in (
3.15
).

3.4.
Thickened moduli space of stable maps

We will now define a new thickening datum given by a linear map

\displaystyle\mu:E\to C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times X\times V^{\prime}\times\Delta_{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{m},\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}T_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/V^{\prime}})

(3.34)

which will be obtained from the thickening datum
\lambda
of (
3.32
) via the procedure described in the next paragraph. Before that, we first explain the notations in (
3.34
). Firstly,
T_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}
denotes the relative tangent bundle of the family
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=X\times V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
of almost complex manifolds defined by
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
(i.e. it is the vertical tangent bundle of
X\times V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
with fiberwise almost complex structures specified by the family
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
). Secondly, the bundle
\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}T_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}
is defined on
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times(X\times V^{\prime})
and the right side of (
3.34
) is to be interpreted as the space of sections of this bundle pulled back along the coordinate projection
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times X\times V^{\prime}\times\Delta_{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{m}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times X\times V^{\prime}
.

We now explain how
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
. Combining the embeddings (
3.15
) and (
3.17
), we get compatible embeddings of submanifolds

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times V^{\prime}\times\Delta_{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}\times\Delta_{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{m}}

(3.35)

which are fiberwise pseudo-holomorphic, i.e., their vertical differential induces a fiberwise injective
\mathbb{C}
-linear map from
T_{\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)/\mathcal{M}^{\text{emb}}_{g}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)}
to the pullback of
T_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}\subset X\times V^{\prime}\times\Delta_{1}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{m}
denote the (locally closed) submanifold which is the image of
\mathcal{C}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A)
under the embedding (
3.35
). Via (
3.35
), we regard
\lambda
from (
3.32
) as a section of
\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}T_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/V^{\prime}}
defined over the submanifold
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathcal{Z}
. Using a partition of unity argument (concerning extension of a vector bundle section from a submanifold to the ambient manifold), we can now find a linear map
\mu
as in (
3.34
) such that the restriction
\mu|_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathcal{Z}}
agrees with the linear map
\lambda
coming from (
3.32
) in a neighborhood (in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathcal{Z}
) of the image of the map

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathcal{Z}

(3.36)

given by (
3.33
) followed by
\text{id}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}\times\eqref{extension-emb}
.

Next, choose
E^{\prime}
to be a finite dimensional vector space and

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times X\times V^{\prime},\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}T_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/V^{\prime}})

(3.37)

to be a linear map inducing an isomorphism

\displaystyle E^{\prime}=\frac{{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}}{{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}}

(3.38)

when restricted along the map
C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times X\times V^{\prime}
given by
i^{\prime}\times\varphi\times x
. Here, we view
{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}
as a linear subspace of
{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
via the pullback along
\varphi
.

Now, we may define the
(\mu\oplus\mu^{\prime})
-thickened moduli space

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},kA)_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}

(3.39)

to consist of all tuples
(y\in V^{\prime},s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime},f:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to X,e\in E,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle f_{*}[\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}]=kA

(3.40)

\displaystyle r_{i}^{j}:=f(\tau_{i}^{j}(s^{\prime}))\in\Delta_{i}\quad\text{transverse intersection}

(3.41)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(y)}f+\mu(e)(\cdot,f(\cdot),y,r_{1}^{1},\ldots,r_{m}^{1})+\mu^{\prime}(e^{\prime})(\cdot,f(\cdot),y)=0.

(3.42)

The words
transverse intersection
in (
3.41
) indicate that the image of
df|_{\tau_{i}^{j}(s^{\prime})}
and
T_{r_{i}^{j}}\Delta_{i}
together span
T_{r_{i}^{j}}X
. Note that the expected dimension of (
3.39
) is

\displaystyle(n-3)(2-2h)+2c_{1}(kA)

\displaystyle+\dim(E\oplus E^{\prime})+\dim V^{\prime}

(3.43)

\displaystyle=(n-1)(2-2h)+2k(c_{1}(A)+2g-2)+2r

\displaystyle+\dim(E\oplus E^{\prime})+\dim V^{\prime}

(3.44)

\displaystyle=\text{ind }\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C}+\text{vdim }\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)

\displaystyle+\dim(E\oplus E^{\prime})+\dim V^{\prime}

(3.45)

where
r
is as in (
3.30
).
Moreover, the locus in (
3.39
) where the
(E\oplus E^{\prime})
-coordinates vanish gives a local étale chart for the orbispace
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},kA)
near
(x,\bullet,\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0,0)
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},kA)_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
is a topological manifold of the expected dimension near
(x,\bullet,\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0,0)
.

Proof.

By
[
22
, Proposition 9.2.6 & Appendix B]
, it is enough to check that the relevant linearized operator at
(x,\bullet,\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0,0)
is surjective. Via
i^{\prime}
, this is identified with the operator

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{X})\oplus T_{x}V^{\prime}\oplus E\oplus E^{\prime}\to C^{\infty}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\Omega^{0,1}_{\tilde{C}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}T_{X})

(3.46)

which can be described explicitly as follows. On the first summand, (
3.46
) is given by the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator
D\bar{\partial}_{J}|_{\varphi}
on
\varphi^{*}T_{X}
. On the second summand, corresponding to variation of the almost complex structure on
X
, the operator (
3.46
) is given by the expression

\displaystyle v^{\prime}\mapsto\textstyle\frac{1}{2}(d\mathcal{F}^{\prime}|_{x}\cdot v^{\prime})\circ d\varphi\circ j_{C^{\prime}},

(3.47)

i.e., the pullback under
\varphi
of the expression (
3.10
). On the third summand, since
\mu
was constructed to agree with
\lambda
on the image of (
3.36
), the operator (
3.46
) coincides with the second component of (
3.29
) followed by the inclusion
\varphi^{*}T_{C}\subset\varphi^{*}T_{X}
. In more detail, exactly as in Remark
3.6
, it is important to notice that the values of
\mu
at points not on the image of (
3.36
), and also the covariant derivatives of
\mu
, are irrelevant for computing the linearization, since we are at a point where the
(E\oplus E^{\prime})
-coordinates vanish. Finally, on the fourth summand, the operator (
3.46
) is given by restricting
\mu^{\prime}
along the embedding
C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times X\times V^{\prime}
given by
i^{\prime-1}\times\varphi\times x
(again, as in Remark
3.6
, only the restriction of
\mu^{\prime}
to the image of this embedding is relevant for computing the linearization).

As the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator
D\bar{\partial}_{J}|_{\varphi}
on
\varphi^{*}T_{X}
restricts to
D\bar{\partial}|_{\varphi}
from (
3.23
) on
\varphi^{*}T_{C}
and induces
\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
on the quotient
\varphi^{*}N_{C}
, we can show surjectivity of (
3.46
) as follows. By Remark
3.6
and the explicit description of (
3.46
) in the previous paragraph, we see that the subspace
C^{\infty}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{C})\oplus E
maps onto the subspace
C^{\infty}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\Omega^{0,1}_{\tilde{C}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}T_{C})
under (
3.46
). Thus, we are left to show that the induced operator

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N_{C})\oplus T_{x}V^{\prime}\oplus E^{\prime}\to C^{\infty}(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\Omega^{0,1}_{\tilde{C}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}N_{C})

(3.48)

on quotients is surjective. The operator (
3.48
) can be described explicitly as follows. On the first summand, it is given by
\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
while on the second and third summands, it is the projection to
\varphi^{*}N_{C}
of the corresponding components of (
3.46
). Thus, we need to show that the map

\displaystyle T_{x}V^{\prime}\oplus E^{\prime}\to{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}

(3.49)

induced from (
3.48
) is surjective. Restricted to the first summand, (
3.49
) maps onto the image of
\varphi^{*}:{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{C,J}\to{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}
by the argument of Lemma
3.3
. The isomorphism (
3.38
) now concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.8

(Compatibility of thickenings)
.

The assignment

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},A),k)_{\lambda}&\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(X,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},kA)_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}\\
\left((y,\Sigma),(s,s^{\prime},\psi:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{C}_{s},e)\right)&\mapsto(y,s^{\prime},\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)}^{-1}\circ\psi:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to\Sigma\subset X,e,0)\end{aligned}

(3.50)

defines a continuous injective map from a neighborhood of
((x,C),(*,\bullet,i\circ\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0))
to a neighborhood of
(x,\bullet,\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0,0)
. Here
\Psi
is the classifying map from (
3.16
).

Proof.

We first show that the map is well-defined. Let us assume that
\left((y,\Sigma),(s,s^{\prime},\psi:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to\mathscr{C}_{s},e)\right)
is a point in (
3.31
). Setting
\{\hat{p}_{i}\}=\Delta_{i}\cap\Sigma
, this means that we have
\Phi(y,\Sigma)=s
, a biholomorphism

\displaystyle\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)}:(\Sigma,\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{m})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathscr{C}_{s},\sigma_{1}(s),\ldots,\sigma_{m}(s))

(3.51)

of stable curves and that the equations (
3.27
) and (
3.28
) are satisfied. We then need to verify that the tuple
(y,s^{\prime},\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)}^{-1}\circ\psi:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to\Sigma\subset X,e,0)
satisfies the equations (
3.40
)–(
3.42
). If
(y,\Sigma)
is sufficiently close to
(x,C)
, then we have
[\Sigma]=[C]=A
and thus, (
3.40
) is satisfied. (
3.28
) has (
3.41
) as an immediate consequence. To deduce (
3.42
) from (
3.27
), it will suffice to check that

\displaystyle\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)}^{*}\left[\lambda(e)(\cdot,\psi(\cdot))\right]=\mu(e)(\cdot,\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)}^{-1}\circ\psi(\cdot),y,\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{m})

(3.52)

since
\hat{p}_{i}=\Psi^{-1}_{(y,\Sigma)}(\sigma_{i}(s))=\Psi_{(y,\Sigma)}^{-1}\circ\psi(\tau_{i}^{1}(s^{\prime}))
for
1\leq i\leq m
. By construction,
\mu|_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times\mathcal{Z}}
agrees with
\lambda
via the embedding (
3.35
) in a neighborhood of the image of (
3.36
) and now (
3.52
) follows from this immediately. Note that continuity and injectivity are evident and this concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

The map from Lemma
3.8
is an inclusion of codimension given by

\displaystyle\dim\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}-\dim\ker D^{N}_{C,J}\geq 0.

(3.53)

Proof.

The dimension of (
3.31
) is given by

\displaystyle\dim V^{\prime}+\text{ind }D^{N}_{C,J}+\text{vdim }\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)+\dim E

(3.54)

while the dimension of (
3.39
) is given by

\displaystyle\dim V^{\prime}+\text{ind }\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{C,J}+\text{vdim }\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,k)+\dim E+\dim E^{\prime}

(3.55)

and thus, from (
3.38
), we find that the codimension of (
3.31
) in (
3.39
) is precisely given by (
3.53
).
∎

3.5.
Concluding the proof of Theorem
3.1

We will now prove Theorem
3.1
by contradiction. To this end, assume that (
3.53
) is zero. Then, the map from Lemma
3.8
is an injective continuous map between topological manifolds of the same dimension and thus, by Brouwer’s Invariance of Domain (
[
14
, Theorem 2B.3]
), its image must cover an open neighborhood of
(x,\bullet,\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1},0,0)
. Restricting this over
V\times 0\times 0\subset V^{\prime}\times E\times E^{\prime}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{\text{emb}}(X,\mathcal{F},A),k)=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(X,\mathcal{F},kA)

(3.56)

in a neighborhood of
(x,\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C\subset X)
. This is a contradiction to the existence of the sequence
(J_{\nu},\varphi_{\nu}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{\nu}\to X)
of simple
J_{\nu}
-curves of genus
h
converging to
(J,C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varphi}C\subset X)
unless
h=g
and
k=1
(in which case
\varphi
must also be an isomorphism). ∎

4.
Bifurcation analysis

We begin by describing the class of bifurcations that we will treat in this article. We restrict throughout this section to (branched) covers with smooth and connected domains. Given any
\delta=(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
as in Definition
2.12
, let
r,h
and
H\subset G
be the number of branch points, domain genus and the (conjugacy class of) subgroup corresponding (in the sense of Definition
2.3
) to the
d
-fold covers in question. We remind the reader that, as in §
2.2
, we will be assuming throughout this section that
(X,\omega)
is a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold.

Definition 4.1

(Elementary wall type)
.

We say that
\delta
is an
elementary wall type
if it satisfies conditions
(1)–(2)
below.

(1)

d\geq 2
and either
r=0
, i.e., unbranched covers, or for each
1\leq i\leq r
, we have
b_{i}^{1}=2
and
b_{i}^{j}=1
for
1<j\leq q_{i}
, i.e., branched covers with only simple
branch points
.

(2)

The
G
-representation
{\bf k}
is faithful, irreducible and of real type. Moreover, if
r=0
, then we have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\bf k}^{H}\leq 1
.

In this case, the subset
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
from Definition
2.18
is called an
elementary wall
.
If we also have
{\bf k}^{H}=0
, then we say that the elementary wall type
\delta
is
trivial
.

We now briefly explain the conditions appearing in Definition
4.1
. Note that condition (1) in Definition
4.1
is a strengthening of condition (i) in Lemma
2.20
: we ask for a unique critical point of order
2
over each branch point. This has the consequence that any small deformation of a morphism
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
of curves of type
(g,{\bf b})
is again of the same type.
On the other hand, condition (2) in Definition
4.1
is a strengthening of condition (ii) in Lemma
2.20
. The relevance of the
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\bf k}^{H}\leq 1
requirement in condition (2) is that it prevents the pulled back normal Cauchy–Riemann operator from having a kernel of dimension greater than one. This is important in both the unbranched and branched cases but, in the latter case, it turns out to be a formal consequence of the rest of Definition
4.1
(see Lemma
4.15
below for more details).
Finally, using
[
27
, Appendix B]
and Lemma
2.9
, the non-triviality of
\delta
is a necessary condition for a bifurcation to occur (see Lemma
4.14
below for more details).

Definition
4.1
covers many cases, including the following examples. The examples given below are abstract and do not necessarily appear as elementary wall types associated to a given Calabi–Yau
3
-fold.

Example 4.2
.

Any degree
2
morphism
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
of curves gives an elementary wall type
\delta
, with
G=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and
\bf k
being the unique one-dimensional non-trivial representation of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. A result analogous to Theorem
4.12
below in the case of unbranched double covers of tori in dimension
4
appears in
[
24
]
.

Example 4.3
.

For any
d\geq 2
, consider a degree
d
morphism
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
with distinct simple branch points with generalized automorphism group being the whole symmetric group
S_{d}
. This gives rise to an elementary wall type with
G=S_{d}
,
H=S_{d-1}
and
\bf k
being the subrepresentation
\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}):\sum_{i}x_{i}=0\}
of the tautological representation of
S_{d}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The representation
\bf k
is faithful and the following computation of the character of its complexification
{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}={\bf k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle|\chi_{{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}}|^{2}+1=|\chi_{\mathbb{C}^{d}}|^{2}=\frac{1}{|S_{d}|}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{d}}\left|\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}:\sigma(i)=i\}\right|^{2}=2

(4.1)

shows that
{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}
is irreducible implying that
{\bf k}
itself is irreducible and of real type. In the first equality of (
4.1
), we have split the tautological
S_{d}
-representation
\mathbb{C}^{d}
into the trivial representation and
{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}
while for the last equality we have used Burnside’s orbit-counting lemma for the diagonal action of
S_{d}
on
\{1,\ldots,d\}\times\{1,\ldots,d\}
which has precisely two orbits.

Note that covers with generalized automorphism group
S_{d}
always exist if

\displaystyle 2h-2-d(2g-2)=r\geq 2d-2

(4.2)

where
h=\text{genus}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and
g=\text{genus}(\Sigma)
. Explicitly, if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
are distinct points in
\Sigma
, then we may define
\Sigma^{\prime}
to be the
d
-fold branched cover of
\Sigma
, associated to the
d
-fold unbranched cover of
\dot{\Sigma}:=\Sigma\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
given by the permutation representation

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle:\pi_{1}(\dot{\Sigma})

(4.3)

\displaystyle=\langle a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g},c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}:\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{g}[a_{i},b_{i}]\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{j}=1\rangle\to S_{d}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\rho(a_{i})=\rho(b_{i})=1\quad\text{for $1\leq i\leq g$}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\rho(c_{2j-1})=\rho(c_{2j})=(j\,j+1)\quad\text{for $1\leq j\leq d-1$}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\rho(c_{k})=(d-1\,d)\quad\text{for $2d-2<k\leq r$}.

(4.7)

Example 4.4
.

For any
n\geq 1
, take
d=2n+1
and consider a degree
d
unbranched cover
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
of a genus
g\geq 2
curve given by the permutation representation

\displaystyle\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g})

\displaystyle=\langle a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g}:\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{g}[a_{i},b_{i}]=1\rangle\to S_{2n+1}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\rho(a_{1})

\displaystyle=\rho(b_{1})=(1\,2)(3\,4)\cdots(2n-1\,2n)

(4.9)

\displaystyle\rho(a_{2})

\displaystyle=\rho(b_{2})=(2\,3)(4\,5)\cdots(2n\,2n+1)

(4.10)

and
\rho(a_{i})=\rho(b_{i})=1
for
i>2
. Note that the generalized automorphism group
\text{im }\rho=G\subset S_{2n+1}
of this cover is isomorphic to the dihedral group

\displaystyle D_{2n+1}=\langle\tau,\theta:\tau^{2}=\theta^{2n+1}=1,\tau\theta\tau^{-1}=\theta^{-1}\rangle

(4.11)

with
\tau=(1\,2)(3\,4)\cdots(2n-1\,2n)
and
\tau\theta=(2\,3)(4\,5)\cdots(2n\,2n+1)
. The subgroup
H
is given by
\langle\tau\rangle\subset D_{2n+1}
. The standard real
2
-dimensional faithful representation
\bf k
which realizes
D_{2n+1}
as the isometry group of a regular
(2n+1)
-gon in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
gives rise to a non-trivial elementary wall type. Indeed, if the complexification
{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\bf k
splits as the direct sum of
D_{2n+1}
-invariant complex lines, then the relation
\tau\theta\tau^{-1}=\theta^{-1}
forces that
\langle\theta\rangle
must act trivially on both, which is absurd. We conclude, as in Example
4.3
, that
\bf k
is irreducible and of real type. Also,
{\bf k}^{H}
is
1
-dimensional over
\mathbb{R}
as it can be identified with an axis of symmetry of the regular
(2n+1)
-gon.

Example 4.5
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer and let
d=2n
. For integers
g,s\geq 1
, define a degree
d
branched cover
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
of a genus
g
curve with
2s
distinct simple branch points
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{2s}\}\subset\Sigma
by the permutation representation

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle:\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{2s}\})

(4.12)

\displaystyle=\langle a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{g},b_{g},c_{1},\ldots,c_{2s}:\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{g}[a_{i},b_{i}]\prod_{j=1}^{2s}c_{j}=1\rangle\to S_{d}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\rho(a_{i})=\rho(b_{i})=(1\,2\,\cdots\,n)(n+1\,\cdots\,2n),\quad\rho(c_{j})=(1\,\,n+1)

(4.14)

for all
1\leq i\leq g
and
1\leq j\leq 2s
. Note that the generalized automorphism group
\text{im }\rho=G\subset S_{d}
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
via

\displaystyle((0,0,\ldots,0),1)

\displaystyle\mapsto(1\,2\,\cdots\,n)(n+1\,\cdots\,2n)

(4.15)

\displaystyle((1,0,\ldots,0),0)

\displaystyle\mapsto(1\,\,n+1)

(4.16)

and the subgroup
H\subset G
is given by
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n-1}\times\{0\}
. The group
G
has a faithful representation
{\bf k}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
acts by cyclically permuting the coordinates while
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}
acts by the matrix
\text{diag}[(-1)^{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}]
, and this gives rise to a non-trivial elementary wall type. As before, to establish that
\bf k
is irreducible and of real type, we will show that the complexification
{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}
is irreducible. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle|\chi_{{\bf k}_{\mathbb{C}}}|^{2}=\frac{1}{n2^{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(n-2k)^{2}=1

(4.17)

where the first equality holds because all elements in
G\setminus(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}
have zero trace on
\bf k
and the second equality comes by computing
(z\partial_{z})^{2}(z+z^{-1})^{n}
at
z=1
in two ways: by direct differentiation or by first using the binomial expansion.

The next two examples are included for completeness and their purpose is to show that the conditions in Definition
4.1
are strictly stronger than the conditions of Lemma
2.20
.

Example 4.6

(Non-elementary, unbranched)
.

Consider the unbranched cover
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma
given by the Galois closure of the unbranched cover
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
from Example
4.4
. Let
\bf k
be the
2
-dimensional representation of the group
G=D_{2n+1}
described in Example
4.4
. It is immediate that the conditions of Lemma
2.20
are satisfied but we do not have an elementary wall type since the subgroup
H\subset G
is trivial in this case implying that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\bf k}^{H}=2
.

Example 4.7

(Non-elementary, branched)
.

For any
n\geq 2
, take
d=2n+1
and four distinct points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{4}
on
\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Consider the
d
-fold branched cover
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
associated to the unbranched cover of
\dot{\Sigma}=\Sigma\setminus\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{4}\}
given by the permutation representation

\displaystyle\rho:\pi_{1}(\dot{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=\langle a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}:\textstyle\prod_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}=1\rangle\to S_{2n+1}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\rho(a_{1})

\displaystyle=\rho(a_{2})=(1\,2)(3\,4)\cdots(2n-1\,2n)

(4.19)

\displaystyle\rho(a_{3})

\displaystyle=\rho(a_{4})=(2\,3)(4\,5)\cdots(2n\,2n+1).

(4.20)

Taking the same group
G=D_{2n+1}
and representation
\bf k
as in Example
4.4
, we see that the conditions of Lemma
2.20
are satisfied but we do not have an elementary wall type since
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
does not have simple branching.

Let
J_{+},J_{-}\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
be super-rigid almost complex structures (which will remain fixed for the rest of this section). Define
\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
to be the space of smooth paths
\gamma:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
with
\gamma(\pm 1)=J_{\pm}
. Before giving the statement of our main result on bifurcations, we need some preliminaries which we now turn to.

Lemma 4.8

(Generic transversality to wall strata)
.

There is a Baire subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})

(4.21)

such that any
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
has the following property. The image
\gamma([-1,1])\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
avoids
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
and
\gamma
is transverse to each of the maps
(
2.17
)
. In particular, whenever the Fredholm index of (
2.17
) is
\leq-2
, the image
\gamma([-1,1])
avoids the image
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})
of (
2.17
).

Proof.

Note that
J_{\pm}
are not contained in the wall
\mathcal{W}
(see Definition
2.18
). Applying the Sard–Smale theorem (countably many times) to suitable universal moduli spaces over
\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
, see e.g.,
[
9
, Section 2.10]
, we obtain a Baire subset
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
consisting of paths
\gamma
which are transverse to
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
as well as to each of the maps (
2.17
). Since
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
is a subset of codimension
\geq 2
(as are those
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})
for which (
2.17
) has Fredholm index
\leq-2
), transversality implies that
\gamma([-1,1])
avoids all of these.
∎

Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be a path from
J_{-}
to
J_{+}
. Suppose that we have
t_{0}\in(-1,1)
and
p=(\gamma(t_{0}),\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
with
\delta=(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
being an elementary wall type. Let
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
be a Galois closure of
\varphi
with
H\subset G
being such that
\Sigma^{\prime}=\tilde{\Sigma}/H
. Introduce the abbreviation

\displaystyle D=D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{0})}.

(4.22)

Lemma
4.8
guarantees that
\gamma
and (
2.17
) are transverse at
(t_{0},p)
. When
\delta
is also assumed to be non-trivial (in the sense of Definition
4.1
), this yields a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle T_{t_{0}}\gamma\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)/T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)

(4.23)

between the tangent space of
\gamma
at
t_{0}
and the normal space of the map (
2.17
) at
p
, where we have used Remark
2.15
to compute the normal space
9
9

9

In more detail, Remark
2.15
shows that the normal space of the inclusion (
2.18
) is
\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)
. To obtain the normal space of (
2.17
) from this, we must quotient
\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)
by the vertical tangent space at
p
of the projection obtained by the composition
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))\to\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A)\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
. Since we have
\ker D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{0})}=(\ker\psi^{*}D)^{G}={\bf k}^{G}=0
, the second arrow is seen to be a local diffeomorphism at
(\gamma(t_{0}),\Sigma)
. The vertical tangent space of the first arrow is seen to be
T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
. Another version of this argument also appears in Lemma
4.16
below.

and Lemma
4.15
(proved later in this section) to make the natural identification

\displaystyle\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)=\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)

(4.24)

given by restriction to the
H
-fixed subspace.
Orient the two sides of (
4.23
) by identifying
T_{t_{0}}\gamma
with
\mathbb{R}
in the obvious way, using the complex structure on the orbifold
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)
and using the standard orientation on the determinant line
\det(\varphi^{*}D)
, which is obtained via the spectral flow when we deform
\varphi^{*}D
to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator.

Definition 4.9

(Sign of wall crossing)
.

With notation as above, we call the pair
(t_{0},p)
an
elementary wall crossing
along
\gamma
. When
\delta
is non-trivial, the
sign
of this wall crossing is defined to be the sign of the isomorphism (
4.23
) and is denoted by

\displaystyle\text{sgn}(\gamma,t_{0};p)\in\{-1,+1\}.

(4.25)

We call the wall crossing
positive
or
negative
in accordance with its sign.

If
\delta=(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
is an elementary wall type for which (
2.17
) has Fredholm index
\leq-1
, then we have a natural codimension
\geq 1
subspace

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}\subset\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})

(4.26)

consisting of points which are “degenerate”. (See Definition
4.29
for details; for the proof of the codimension
\geq 1
property, see Lemma
4.38
.) Denote its image under (
2.17
) by
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}
and note that it is a subset of codimension
\geq 2
in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
.

Lemma 4.10

(Generic non-degeneracy)
.

There is a Baire subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})

(4.27)

with the property that every
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
has image disjoint from
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}
for each elementary wall type
\delta=(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
for which the map (
2.17
) has Fredholm index
\leq-1
.

Proof.

This follows by an application of the Sard–Smale theorem (countably many times) to suitable universal moduli spaces over
\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
. The key observation is that the subsets
\mathcal{W}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(g,A,{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}
have codimension
\geq 2
in
\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, which follows from Lemma
4.38
as noted above.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

By definition, the subsets
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
are both invariant under smooth reparametrizations of paths. Therefore, to simplify the notation below, we will assume that in Definition
4.9
we have
t_{0}=0
.

Theorem 4.12

(Elementary bifurcations)
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
be a path lying in the Baire subset
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
. Consider a point

\displaystyle p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})

(4.28)

such that
(0,p)
is an elementary wall crossing.

We can then find an open neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X
in the Gromov topology and an
\epsilon>0
such that the parametric moduli space

\displaystyle\left((-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times\mathcal{O}\right)\cap\mathcal{M}^{*}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)\to[-1,1]

(4.29)

of smooth
embedded

\gamma(t)
-curves, with
|t|<\epsilon
, of genus
h
and class
dA
, lying in
\mathcal{O}
has the following properties. The fiber of (
4.29
) over
t=0
is empty and (
4.29
) is a smooth oriented
1
-dimensional manifold, with the projection to
(-\epsilon,0)\cup(0,\epsilon)
being a proper submersion. Let
m_{\pm}
and
n_{\pm}
denote the unsigned and signed number, respectively, of points in the fiber of (
4.29
) over
t
for
\pm t>0
.

(i)

We have
m_{\pm}=0
if
\delta
is trivial, or if
r=0
and
H\subset G
is not a maximal subgroup such that
{\bf k}^{H}\neq 0
.

(ii)

If we are not in either of the cases from
(i)
, then we have
\Gamma=\text{Aut}(\varphi)\subset\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, the estimate
|\Gamma|\cdot|m_{\pm}|\leq 2
and the identity

\displaystyle n_{+}-n_{-}=-\frac{2\cdot\text{sgn}(\gamma,0;p)}{|\Gamma|}.

(4.30)

Moreover, if
(t_{i},\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}\subset X)
is any sequence of points in (
4.29
) with
t_{i}\to 0
, then the Gromov limit of this sequence exists and is given by
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X
.

Remark 4.13
.

Note that for
J=\gamma(0)
, an element
\sigma\in\ker(\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,J})
can be viewed as a
d
-valued holomorphic section of the bundle
N_{\Sigma}
over the curve
\Sigma
with singular points given by the branched points of the cover
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma
. Theorem
4.12
asserts that by varying
J
along a generic path
\gamma
, we can obtain a
1
-parameter family of solutions to the non-linear Cauchy–Riemann equations in
X
which give rise to embedded
\gamma(t)
-holomorphic curves of class
d[\Sigma]
. We expect the proof of Theorem
4.12
(which is carried out in the next section via Kuranishi models) to be useful in other similar situations. For instance, it is speculated in
[
10
, Section 1]
that a
2
-valued harmonic
1
-form defined over a special Lagrangian submanifold
M
in a Calabi–Yau manifold
(X,\omega,\Omega)
should give rise to a
1
-parameter family of special Lagrangian immersions
\iota_{t}:\tilde{M}\rightarrow X
, where
\tilde{M}
is the branched double cover of
M
branching along the singular set of the given harmonic
1
-form. The similarity is quite transparent, though the analysis needed to carry out the program in
[
10
]
is still under development.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4.12
. To orient the reader, we start with a brief plan of the argument. In §
4.1
, we deal with the trivial case of the theorem and focus our attention on the non-trivial case for the remainder. In §
4.2
and §
4.3
, we use the implicit function theorem to construct a Kuranishi model for the moduli space of interest. The detailed analysis of the Kuranishi model, which is the heart of the proof, is carried out in §
4.4
. The proof is completed in §
4.4.1
and §
4.4.2
(modulo a crucial technical result whose proof is carried out in §
4.4.3
).

Throughout the argument, we will be freely using the notation from the statement of Theorem
4.12
. In particular, the super-rigid almost complex structures
J_{\pm}
, the elementary wall type
\delta=(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
, the path
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
and the point
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
with
J=\gamma(0)
will remain fixed for the remainder of this section. We will be abbreviating
D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
to
D
and will sometimes denote the almost complex structure
\gamma(t)
by the symbol
J_{t}
.

4.1.
Simplifications

Let
r,h
and
H\subset G
be the number of branch points, domain genus and the (conjugacy class of) subgroup corresponding to the
d
-fold cover
\varphi
(as in Definition
4.1
). We will study what happens to the moduli space

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)\to[-1,1]

(4.31)

of embedded genus
h
curves of class
dA
(lying in a neighborhood of
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X
in the Gromov topology) as we go from
t<0
to
t>0
.

Write
\bf k=\theta^{*}
for some irreducible faithful representation
\theta
of
G
of real type. At the point
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
, with associated Galois closure
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta}=1
and

\displaystyle\ker\psi^{*}D=\theta^{*}\otimes\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta}.

(4.32)

Since
d=[G:H]\geq 2
, it follows that
G
is non-trivial (note that we study bifurcation analysis only in the multiply covered case since
[
16
, Section 6]
already treats the corresponding analysis in the somewhere injective case). Taking
G
-invariants in (
4.32
) implies that
\ker D=0
. There is therefore a unique embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve
\Sigma_{t}
close to
\Sigma
for each
|t|
small enough (obtained using the inverse function theorem).

Lemma 4.14

(No bifurcations for trivial
\delta
)
.

If
\delta
is a trivial elementary wall type, then we can find
\epsilon>0
and a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X
in the Gromov topology such that (
4.31
) has no points contained in
(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

Assume to the contrary that we can find a sequence
(t_{i},\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}\subset X)
of embedded
\gamma(t_{i})
-curves of genus
h
and class
dA
converging in the Gromov topology to
(0,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X)
. Now, exactly as in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition B.1]
, we may suitably rescale
\Sigma^{\prime}_{i}
in the normal direction to the embedded
\gamma(t_{i})
-curve
\Sigma_{t_{i}}
of genus
g
and class
A
to obtain a nonzero element in
\ker\varphi^{*}D=(\ker\psi^{*}D)^{H}
in the Gromov limit as
i\to\infty
. Now, (
4.32
) shows that
{\bf k}^{H}\neq 0
, which implies that
\delta
is non-trivial.
∎

Lemma
4.14
completes the proof of Theorem
4.12
in the case when
\delta
is trivial. Thus, for the rest of this section, we shall focus exclusively on the case when
\delta
is non-trivial, i.e.,
(\theta^{*})^{H}\neq 0
.
We record below an important consequence of
\delta
’s non-triviality.

Lemma 4.15
.

The non-triviality of
\delta
implies that
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
satisfies the following.

(i)

We have
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta^{*})^{H}=1
.

(ii)

For each irreducible representation
\rho
of
G
over
\mathbb{R}
with
\rho\not\simeq\theta
, we have either
\text{ind}_{\mathbb{R}}D^{N}_{\psi,\rho}=0
or
(\rho^{*})^{H}=0
.

As a consequence, we have the identifications

\displaystyle\ker\varphi^{*}D

\displaystyle=(\theta^{*})^{H}\otimes\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta}

(4.33)

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D

\displaystyle=(\theta^{*})^{H}\otimes{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta}.

(4.34)

Moreover, the natural map

\displaystyle\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)

(4.35)

given by restriction to
H
-fixed points is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We use Lemma
2.9
and Remark
2.8
to deduce the quasi-isomorphism

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}\,(\theta_{i}^{*})^{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}\to\varphi^{*}D,

(4.36)

which implies that we have the identity

\displaystyle\text{ind}_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi^{*}D)=\sum_{i}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta_{i}^{*})^{H}\cdot\text{ind}_{\mathbb{K}_{i}}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta_{i}}.

(4.37)

Now, a simple computation using the Riemann–Roch formula (combined with the Riemann–Hurwitz formula for
\varphi
and condition (1) of Definition
4.1
) shows that the left side of (
4.37
) is
-2r
.

On the other hand, by the first inequality in
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
, we know that each term on the right side of (
4.37
) is non-positive since
\text{ind}_{\mathbb{R}}D=0
. Moreover, using the second inequality in
[
27
, Lemma 2.15]
, we see that

\text{ind}_{\mathbb{R}}(D^{N}_{\psi,\theta})\leq-2r
since
\theta
is faithful, irreducible, and of real type. Since
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta^{*})^{H}\geq 1
, the above argument implies that for all
\theta_{i}\neq\theta
in (
4.37
), the corresponding term on the right side vanishes, and the term corresponding to
\theta_{i}=\theta
is equal to
-2r
.

If
r>0
, this immediately gives us (i) and (ii). If
r=0
, then we get (ii) as above and (i) follows from Definition
4.1
and the non-triviality of
\delta
. Now, (
4.33
) and (
4.34
) are formal consequences of statements (i), (ii), and (
4.36
). Finally, for the isomorphism (
4.35
), observe that

\displaystyle\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)

\displaystyle=\text{Hom}(\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta},{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta})

(4.38)

\displaystyle=\text{Hom}((\theta^{*})^{H}\otimes\ker D^{N}_{\psi,\theta},(\theta^{*})^{H}\otimes{\text{coker }}D^{N}_{\psi,\theta})

(4.39)

\displaystyle=\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)

(4.40)

where we use the fact that
\text{End}^{G}(\theta^{*})=\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
\theta^{*}
is of real type for the first equality and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta^{*})^{H}=1
for the second equality.
∎

To summarize the discussions above, we note for future reference that

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker D

\displaystyle=0,

(4.41)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker\varphi^{*}D

\displaystyle=1.

(4.42)

These will be used below without further comment.

4.2.
Preparation

Since
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, we know that it is transverse to the map (
2.17
) at
t=0
and
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
. Let us clarify the meaning of this transversality statement a little further. Choose
\epsilon>0
small enough so that for
|t|<\epsilon
the inverse function theorem may be applied to produce an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve
\Sigma_{t}
(of genus
g
and class
A
) close to
\Sigma
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)

(4.43)

to be a
1
-parametric form of Definition
2.3
over
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
. Explicitly, it consists of pairs
(t,\varphi^{\prime\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma_{t})
such that
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma_{t})
as in Definition
2.3
(where we are suppressing the isomorphism of
G
with the generalized automorphism group from the notation) and is equipped with the projection
(t,\varphi^{\prime\prime})\mapsto t
. Being a smooth orbifold (which is proved exactly as in Lemma
2.4
), it has a well-defined tangent space at each point (which may be regarded as representations of the isotropy group at that point). In fact, since
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
may be viewed as a
1
-parameter family of complex structures on a single smooth surface, it follows that (
4.43
) is a smooth locally trivial bundle of orbifolds over
(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. Thus, we have a natural short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)\to T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)\to\mathbb{R}_{t}\to 0

(4.44)

relating the tangent spaces of the fiber, the total space and the base.

Let
\psi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
be a Galois closure of
\varphi
. Define

\displaystyle T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)\to\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)

(4.45)

to be the linearized map, in the sense of Definition
2.10
, associated to the family of
G
-equivariant Cauchy–Riemann operators on
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)
given by

\displaystyle p^{\prime}=(t,\varphi^{\prime\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma_{t},\iota_{t})\mapsto\psi_{p^{\prime}}^{*}(D^{N}_{\Sigma_{t},\gamma(t)})

(4.46)

where
p^{\prime}\mapsto(\psi_{p^{\prime}}:\tilde{\Sigma}^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma_{t})
is a smooth family of Galois closures extending
\psi
at
p
, obtained as in the discussion preceding
[
27
, Example 3.6]
. Transversality of
\gamma
and (
2.17
) at
(0,p)
then has the following consequence.

Lemma 4.16
.

The map (
4.45
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Using
\ker D={\text{coker }}D=0
and the inverse function theorem, we may identify a neighborhood of
(0,\varphi)
in
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)
with a neighborhood of
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
inside the pullback of
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))
under the path
\gamma:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
.

Transversality at
(0,p)
of the path
\gamma
and the map (
2.17
) of Fredholm index
-1
shows, by pullback along
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, that the map
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)\to\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A))
and the inclusion (
2.18
) have an isolated transverse intersection at
((0,\varphi),p)
. This gives an isomorphism between the tangent space
T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)
and the normal space at
p
of the inclusion (
2.18
). The normal space of (
2.18
) at
p
is indeed given by the right side of (
4.45
) using Remark
2.15
. The description of this isomorphism as the linearized map (
4.45
) from above relies on the proof of Theorem
2.14
or equivalently
[
27
, Theorem D]
. In more detail, we use the explicit description (from
[
27
, Section 3.5]
) of the (surjective) linearization of the equations defining the inclusion (
2.18
) and this completes the proof.
∎

Next we will study the stable map moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
locally near
(0,\varphi)
as an intermediate step to understanding (
4.29
). The strategy is to use the implicit function theorem to first produce a Kuranishi model and then explicitly analyze the resulting Kuranishi map. Before proceeding to this, let us establish the notations for the domain and target of the Kuranishi map (which are respectively the kernel and cokernel of the corresponding linearized operator).

4.2.1.
Domain of the Kuranishi model

To describe the domain explicitly, let us denote the inclusion of
\Sigma
in
X
by
i
and the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator on
i^{*}T_{X}
(resp.
\varphi^{*}T_{X}
) by
\tilde{D}_{i}
(resp.
\tilde{D}_{\varphi}=\varphi^{*}\tilde{D}_{i}
). Moreover, write
K=\dot{\gamma}(0)
. The domain of the Kuranishi map is then an open neighborhood of
0
in the vector space

\displaystyle T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)

(4.47)

which consists of “cocycle” triples
(\xi,y,t)
, where
\xi\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{X})
,
y\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma^{\prime},T_{\Sigma^{\prime}})
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
are such that

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{\varphi}\xi+\textstyle\frac{1}{2}(J\circ d\varphi\circ y+tK\circ d\varphi\circ j_{\Sigma^{\prime}})=0

(4.48)

modulo the “coboundary” triples
(\frac{1}{2}d\varphi(V),j_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\bar{\partial}V,0)
where
V\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},T_{\Sigma^{\prime}})
and
\bar{\partial}
stands for the Cauchy-Riemann operator on
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},T_{\Sigma^{\prime}})
. This vector space contains

\displaystyle T^{\text{Zar}}_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,J,dA)

(4.49)

which is the subspace of
T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
defined by setting
t=0
.

Similarly, we can consider the parametrized moduli space of
d
–fold genus
h
branched covers of
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
. Its (Zariski) tangent space at
(0,\varphi)
is denoted by
T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
.
The fiber of
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
over
t=0
is

\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)
and its tangent space at
\varphi
is written as
T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)
.

Lemma 4.17
.

The vector spaces introduced above fit into the following commutative diagram with exact rows and columns (recall that
D=D^{N}_{\Sigma,J}
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\text{Zar}}_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,J,dA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\varphi^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\varphi^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

(4.50)

Proof.

We begin by explaining the maps appearing in (
4.50
). All the maps between the (Zariski) tangent spaces are given by inclusions. The horizontal maps to
\mathbb{R}_{t}
are induced by the projection to the
t
-coordinate. This implies the first two rows form a commutative diagram. The vertical maps to
\ker(\varphi^{*}D)
are defined more indirectly and we explain them later.

As in (
4.43
), the natural projection
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
is a smooth locally trivial fiber bundle of orbifolds and its fiber over
t=0
is
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)
. Linearizing this projection at
(0,\varphi)
yields the exactness of the first row. For the second row, observe that the map to
\mathbb{R}_{t}
is surjective since the corresponding map on the first row is surjective and the diagram commutes. The definition of
T^{\text{Zar}}_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,J,dA)
now shows that the second row is also exact. Moreover, the compatible short exact sequences of the first two rows induce a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\frac{T^{\text{Zar}}_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,J,dA)}{T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\frac{T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)}{T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)}

(4.51)

of quotients. It will suffice to identify the quotient on the left side of (
4.51
) with
\ker(\varphi^{*}D)
to simultaneously define the vertical maps to
\ker(\varphi^{*}D)
in (
4.50
) and establish commutativity and exactness of the two columns.

To determine the left side of (
4.51
), we use the long exact sequence associated to the short exact sequence (of Cauchy–Riemann operators on)
0\to\varphi^{*}T_{\Sigma}\to\varphi^{*}T_{X}\to\varphi^{*}N_{\Sigma}\to 0
.
∎

In view of condition (1) of Definition
4.1
, we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\simeq}T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)

(4.52)

induced by the natural map
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)
. Indeed, recall that any small deformation of the morphism
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
is again of type
(g,{\bf b})
. It follows that the natural map from the short exact sequence (
4.44
) to the upper row short exact sequence in (
4.50
) is an isomorphism and, in particular, this gives

\displaystyle T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)\xrightarrow{\simeq}T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d).

(4.53)

In fact, the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Hom}^{G}(\ker\psi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\psi^{*}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf w}_{\varphi}}

(4.54)

is commutative and, as explained below, all its arrows are isomorphisms. The left vertical arrow is the isomorphism (
4.53
), the right vertical arrow comes by restricting to the
H
-fixed subspace (which is an isomorphism by Lemma
4.15
). The top and bottom horizontal arrows come by considering the families of operators (
4.46
) and
(t,\varphi^{\prime\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\to\Sigma_{t})\mapsto\varphi^{\prime\prime*}(D^{N}_{\Sigma_{t},\gamma(t)})
on
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
and
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
respectively. The top horizontal arrow was shown to be an isomorphism in Lemma
4.16
and commutativity of (
4.54
) implies that the bottom horizontal arrow must be an isomorphism as well. For future reference, we will label the bottom horizontal arrow of this commutative diagram as
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
.

Observe that both sides of the isomorphism
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
have natural orientations. Indeed, we have the following.

(1)

\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
is fibered over the oriented interval
(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
with complex fibers of complex dimension
r
. Recall that
r=(2h-2)-d(2g-2)
is the number of branch points of
\varphi
.

(2)

An orientation of the vector space

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)=(\ker\varphi^{*}D)^{*}\otimes{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D

(4.55)

is the same as an orientation of its determinant line

\displaystyle(\ker\varphi^{*}D)^{\otimes 1+2r}\otimes\det({\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)

(4.56)

where we recall that
\dim\ker\varphi^{*}D=1
and
\dim{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D=1+2r
.
Since the tensor square of a
1
-dimensional space is canonically oriented, it follows that we simply need an orientation of the determinant line

\displaystyle\det(\varphi^{*}D)=\det(\ker\varphi^{*}D)\otimes\det({\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)^{*}

(4.57)

of the Cauchy–Riemann operator
\varphi^{*}D
. This can be defined in the usual manner by homotoping
D
to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator and using the spectral flow.

Thus, the isomorphism
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
has a well-defined sign. Notice that the isomorphism (
4.23
) for
t_{0}=0
, whose sign is referenced in Theorem
4.12
, can be derived as follows from
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
by passing to the quotient
\mathbb{R}_{t}
of
T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
, using the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)\to T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\to\mathbb{R}_{t}\to 0

(4.58)

and making the identification
T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\Sigma,\gamma)\xrightarrow{\simeq}T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
given by (
4.53
). In particular, we have

\displaystyle\text{sgn}({\bf w}_{\varphi})=\text{sgn}(\gamma,0;p).

(4.59)

Without loss of generality, we will assume for the rest of this section that
(0,p)
is a positive wall-crossing, i.e.,
\text{sgn}({\bf w}_{\varphi})=+1
.
This assumption can be achieved by reversing the direction of
\gamma
if it is not already satisfied.

4.2.2.
Target of the Kuranishi model

The target of the Kuranishi map is the space
E_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
of all
\eta\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{X})
modulo all elements of the kind that appear on the left side of (
4.48
). In fact, in view of the surjectivity of
T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)\to\mathbb{R}_{t}
, which follows from (
4.50
), we can restrict attention to just elements as in the left side of (
4.48
) for which
t=0
. Using the long exact sequence associated to the short exact sequence (of Cauchy–Riemann operators on)
0\to\varphi^{*}T_{\Sigma}\to\varphi^{*}T_{X}\to\varphi^{*}N_{\Sigma}\to 0
, it now follows that we have a natural identification

\displaystyle E_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)={\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D.

(4.60)

4.3.
Implicit function theorem

We will now explain how to construct the Kuranishi model for
\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
near
(0,\varphi)
using the implicit function theorem on an appropriate map
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
of Banach spaces (defined on a neighborhood of
0
). Let
m\geq 2
be an integer. Define
\mathcal{Y}
to be the space of sections of
\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}T_{X}
of class
L^{2}_{m}
(i.e.
m
derivatives in
L^{2}
). Note that
\mathcal{Y}
has a natural action of

\displaystyle\Gamma=\text{Aut}(\varphi)=N_{G}(H)/H

(4.61)

We define
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{\text{vf}}\oplus\mathcal{X}_{\text{acs}}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{t}
, where the summands are defined as follows. Choose a finite
\Gamma
-invariant set of points
M\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
where
\varphi
is unramified such that
\Sigma^{\prime}
has no automorphisms fixing
M
. For each
[z]\in M/\Gamma
, choose a complement
\Delta_{[z]}\subset T_{\varphi(z)}X
for
d\varphi_{z}(T_{z}\Sigma^{\prime})
. Define
\mathcal{X}_{\text{vf}}
to be the kernel of the
\Gamma
-equivariant evaluation map

\displaystyle L^{2}_{m+1}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}T_{X})\to\bigoplus_{z\in M}(T_{\varphi(z)}X)/\Delta_{[z]}.

(4.62)

Define
\mathcal{X}_{\text{acs}}
to be a (finite dimensional)
\Gamma
-invariant complement of the map

\displaystyle\Omega^{0}_{M}(\Sigma^{\prime},T_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}}\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma^{\prime},T_{\Sigma^{\prime}})

(4.63)

consisting of sections compactly supported in
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus M
. Here,
\Omega^{0}_{M}(\Sigma^{\prime},T_{\Sigma^{\prime}})
is the space of vector fields on
\Sigma^{\prime}
which vanish on
M
.

To define the map
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, we need to fix the following choices.

(1)

A metric on
X
with its associated exponential map
\exp
.

(2)

A
J
-linear connection
\nabla
on
T_{X}
.

(3)

A smooth
1
-parameter family of complex vector bundle isomorphisms

\displaystyle I_{X,t}:(T_{X},J)\xrightarrow{\simeq}(T_{X},J_{t})

(4.64)

with
I_{X,t}=\text{id}+\frac{1}{2}JA_{X,t}
, where
A_{X,0}=0
and
A_{X,t}
is
J
-antilinear for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
with
|t|<\epsilon
(after shrinking
\epsilon
if necessary). Explicitly, we can define the endomorphism
A_{X,t}
by the formula

\displaystyle A_{X,t}=-2J(\text{id}+JJ_{t})(\text{id}-JJ_{t})^{-1}=-2J(J_{t}-J)(J_{t}+J)^{-1}.

(4.65)

Note that we have
\dot{\gamma}(0)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}J_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}A_{X,t}=K
.

(4)

A smooth family
y\mapsto j_{y}
of almost complex structures on
\Sigma^{\prime}
parametrized by
y\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{acs}}
with
\frac{d}{d\tau}|_{\tau=0}j_{\tau y}=y
and
j_{0}=j_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
being the given almost complex structure on
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The concrete choice we shall work with is given by declaring the following to be an isomorphism of complex vector bundles

\displaystyle I_{\Sigma^{\prime},y}=\text{id}+\textstyle\frac{1}{2}j_{\Sigma^{\prime}}y:(T_{\Sigma^{\prime}},j_{\Sigma^{\prime}})\to(T_{\Sigma^{\prime}},j_{y})

(4.66)

for
y\in\mathcal{X}_{\text{acs}}
with small
|y|
.

We can now define the map
\Phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
for small
(\xi,y,t)\in\mathcal{X}
as

\displaystyle\Phi(\xi,y,t)=PT_{\nabla}\left[I_{X,t}^{-1}\circ(\bar{\partial}_{J_{t},j_{y}}\exp_{\varphi}\xi)\circ I_{\Sigma^{\prime},y}\right].

(4.67)

Note that
\Phi
is a smooth map of Banach manifolds (see e.g. the discussion in
[
20
, page 43]
or
[
22
, Section B.5]
).
Here, the symbol
\exp_{\varphi}\xi
denotes the map
z\mapsto\exp_{\varphi(z)}\xi(z)
of regularity
L^{2}_{m+1}
(i.e.
m+1
derivatives in
L^{2}
), the symbol
\bar{\partial}_{J_{t},j_{y}}
denotes the non-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator

\displaystyle u\mapsto\textstyle\frac{1}{2}(du+J_{t}\circ du\circ j_{y})

(4.68)

and
PT_{\nabla}
denotes the backward parallel transport along the paths
\tau\mapsto\exp_{\varphi(z)}{\tau\xi(z)}
induced by the connection
\nabla
. Clearly,
\Phi
is
\Gamma
-equivariant,
\Phi(0)=0
and it is immediate that a neighborhood of
(0,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
is identified homeomorphically with a neighborhood of
[0]\in\Phi^{-1}(0)/\Gamma
.

To apply the implicit function theorem to
\Phi
, we first note that the linearization
\tilde{D}=D\Phi(0):\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is given by the following formula (compare (
4.48
) from §
4.2.1
)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}(\xi,y,t)=\tilde{D}_{\varphi}\xi+\textstyle\frac{1}{2}(J\circ d\varphi\circ y+tK\circ d\varphi\circ j_{\Sigma^{\prime}})

(4.69)

where recall (from §
4.2.1
) that
\tilde{D}_{\varphi}
denotes the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator on
\varphi^{*}T_{X}
(defined with
j_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
and
J
held fixed).
We can now readily see that we have natural identifications

\displaystyle\ker\tilde{D}

\displaystyle=T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)

(4.70)

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}\tilde{D}

\displaystyle=E_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA).

(4.71)

Split the maps
\ker\tilde{D}\to\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}\to{\text{coker }}\tilde{D}
by choosing a
\Gamma
-invariant complement
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
for
\ker\tilde{D}
and a
\Gamma
-invariant lift
E\subset\mathcal{Y}
of
{\text{coker }}\tilde{D}
.

Lemma 4.18
.

There are
\Gamma
-invariant neighborhoods
0\in U\subset\ker\tilde{D}
and
0\in V\subset\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
such that
\Phi^{-1}(E)\cap(U\times V)
is given by the graph of a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth function
\Psi:U\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
satisfying
\Psi(0)=0
and
D\Psi(0)=0
.

Proof.

Apply the implicit function theorem to the
\Gamma
-equivariant map
\Phi
.
∎

Definition 4.19

(Kuranishi map)
.

Define the
\Gamma
-equivariant map
F:U\to E
by

\displaystyle F(\kappa)=\Phi(\kappa+\Psi(\kappa)).

(4.72)

This map
F
yields the germ at
0
of a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth map from
\ker\tilde{D}
to
{\text{coker }}\tilde{D}
or equivalently

\displaystyle T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)\to E_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)

(4.73)

which we call a
Kuranishi map
for
(0,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
.

Remark 4.20
.

For a more general abstract discussion of Kuranishi reduction and some of its properties, see Appendix
A
.

It is clear from Lemma
4.18
that there is a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
[0]\in F^{-1}(0)/\Gamma
and a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
of
(0,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
along with a homeomorphism

\displaystyle\Xi:\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}

(4.74)

mapping
[0]
to
(0,\varphi)
. We may assume, after shrinking
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
and
\epsilon
, that we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=((-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times\mathcal{O})\cap\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)

(4.75)

for a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to X
in the Gromov topology (see the discussion following
[
22
, Definition 9.1.3]
for an explicit description of a basis of neighborhoods).

4.4.
Analysis of the Kuranishi map

All functions in this subsection are to be understood to be germs near
0
of smooth functions which vanish at
0
.

Definition 4.21
.

Given two finite dimensional vector spaces
W_{1},W_{2}
, two functions
F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}:W_{1}\to W_{2}
and an integer
l\geq 1
, we write

\displaystyle F^{\prime}\equiv_{l}F^{\prime\prime}

(4.76)

to mean that the
i^{\text{th}}
derivatives of
F^{\prime}-F^{\prime\prime}
vanish at
0
for all
0\leq i\leq l
. Clearly,
\equiv_{l}
is an equivalence relation.

We will now analyze the Kuranishi map
F
from Definition
4.19
. From Lemma
4.18
, it follows that
F(0)=0
and
DF(0)=0
. We fix some notations first. Recalling
\tilde{D}
from (
4.69
), let

\displaystyle\varsigma

\displaystyle:\ker\tilde{D}\to\ker(\varphi^{*}D)

(4.77)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle:\ker\tilde{D}\to\mathbb{R}_{t}

(4.78)

be the natural projections (see the exact commutative diagram (
4.50
) for more details, recalling that
T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)=\ker\tilde{D}
). Using Lemma
4.17
, we fix a splitting

\displaystyle\ker\tilde{D}=T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\oplus\ker\varphi^{*}D=\mathbb{R}_{t}\oplus T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)\oplus\ker\varphi^{*}D

(4.79)

compatible with the inclusions
T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)\subset T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\subset\ker\tilde{D}
and the projections (
4.77
), (
4.78
).

Observe that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\ker\tilde{D}=T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
contains a
\Gamma
-invariant submanifold
Z
(passing through
0
) corresponding to
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
under
\Xi
with

\displaystyle T_{0}Z=T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\subset\ker\tilde{D}.

(4.80)

Choosing
\varsigma^{\prime}:\ker\tilde{D}\to\ker(\varphi^{*}D)\simeq\mathbb{R}
to be a smooth function such that, near
0
,
Z=(\varsigma^{\prime})^{-1}(0)
is a regular level set, we can then find a smooth function (which we call the “reduced” version of the Kuranishi map
F
)

\displaystyle F_{1}:\ker\tilde{D}\to(\ker\varphi^{*}D)^{*}\otimes{\text{coker }}\tilde{D}=\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)

(4.81)

such that
F=\langle\varsigma^{\prime},F_{1}\rangle
, where

\displaystyle\langle,\rangle:\ker\varphi^{*}D\otimes\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)\to{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D

(4.82)

is the natural pairing. In (
4.81
), we identify
{\text{coker }}\tilde{D}
with
{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D
using (
4.60
) and (
4.71
). In view of (
4.80
),
Z
is given by the graph of a function

\displaystyle f:T_{0}Z\to\ker(\varphi^{*}D)

(4.83)

with
f(0)=0
and
Df(0)=0
. As a result, we may take
\varsigma^{\prime}=\varsigma-f
and, in particular, we have the relation

\displaystyle\varsigma^{\prime}\equiv_{1}\varsigma.

(4.84)

Since
DF(0)=0
, it follows that
F_{1}(0)=0
. Let
F_{1}^{\prime}
denote the function obtained by restricting
F_{1}
to the subspace
T_{0}Z\subset\ker\tilde{D}
. We then have the following.

Lemma 4.22
.

The relation

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\prime}\equiv_{1}{\bf w}_{\varphi}

(4.85)

holds, where
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
is the isomorphism introduced in diagram (
4.54
).

Proof.

Given any
b\in T_{0}Z
and
\sigma\in\ker\varphi^{*}D
, we need to show that

\displaystyle{\bf w}_{\varphi}(b)\cdot\sigma=\left(\textstyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}F_{1}(b_{\lambda})\cdot\tilde{\sigma}\right)|_{\lambda=0}

(4.86)

where
\tilde{\sigma}\in\ker\tilde{D}
is a lift of
\sigma
with
t(\tilde{\sigma})=0
(which exists by Lemma
4.17
) and
\lambda\mapsto b_{\lambda}
is a path in
Z
with
b_{\lambda}(0)=0
and
\dot{b}_{\lambda}(0)=b
.
Recall that
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
comes from linearizing the family of Cauchy–Riemann operators on
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
specified immediately after (
4.54
). Accordingly, we have

\displaystyle{\bf w}_{\varphi}(b)\cdot\sigma

\displaystyle\equiv\left(\textstyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}D\Phi(b_{\lambda}+\Psi(b_{\lambda}))\cdot\tilde{\sigma}\right)|_{\lambda=0}\pmod{\text{im }\tilde{D}}

(4.87)

\displaystyle\equiv(D^{2}\Phi)|_{0}(b,\tilde{\sigma})\pmod{\text{im }\tilde{D}}

(4.88)

where
D^{2}\Phi=D(D\Phi)
denotes the second derivative of
\Phi
, and we evaluate it at
0
along the two directions
b
and
\tilde{\sigma}
. To justify (
4.87
), we argue as follows. Lemma
4.18
shows that
b_{\lambda}+\Psi(b_{\lambda})\in\mathcal{X}
is the representative of the element
(t_{\lambda},\varphi_{\lambda}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{\lambda}\to\Sigma_{t_{\lambda}})\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)
corresponding to
b_{\lambda}\in Z
under
\Xi
. Thus, we see that
D\Phi(b_{\lambda}+\Psi(b_{\lambda}))\cdot\tilde{\sigma}
represents the application of the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator at the
\gamma(t_{\lambda})
-holomorphic map
\varphi_{\lambda}
to (the parallel transport of)
\tilde{\sigma}
. Since the result of applying the linearized Cauchy–Riemann operator at
\varphi_{\lambda}
to
\tilde{\sigma}
lifts the result of applying
\varphi_{\lambda}^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma_{t_{\lambda}},\gamma(t_{\lambda})}
to
\sigma
, (
4.87
) now follows. In (
4.88
), we are able to disregard the contribution of
\Psi
since, by Lemma
4.18
,
\Psi(0)=0
and
D\Psi(0)=0
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle F_{1}(b_{\lambda})\cdot\tilde{\sigma}=\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}F(b_{\lambda}+\mu\tilde{\sigma})|_{\mu=0}

(4.89)

for each
\lambda
, by the definition of
F_{1}
. We now compute

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle\frac{d}{d\lambda}F_{1}(b_{\lambda})\cdot\tilde{\sigma}\right)|_{\lambda=0}

\displaystyle=\textstyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda\,\partial\mu}F(b_{\lambda}+\mu\tilde{\sigma})|_{\lambda=\mu=0}

(4.90)

\displaystyle\equiv\textstyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\lambda\,\partial\mu}\Phi(b_{\lambda}+\mu\tilde{\sigma}+\Psi(b_{\lambda}+\mu\tilde{\sigma}))|_{\lambda=\mu=0}\pmod{\text{im }\tilde{D}}

(4.91)

\displaystyle\equiv(D^{2}\Phi)|_{0}(b,\tilde{\sigma})\pmod{\text{im }\tilde{D}}

(4.92)

which proves (
4.86
), where we have again used
\Psi(0)=0
and
D\Psi(0)=0
.
∎

Corollary 4.23
.

Z^{\prime}=F_{1}^{-1}(0)\subset\ker\tilde{D}
is a
\Gamma
-invariant submanifold passing through
0
and its natural projection to
\ker\varphi^{*}D
is a local diffeomorphism at
0
. In other words,
Z^{\prime}
is given by the graph of a smooth function

\displaystyle g:\ker\varphi^{*}D\to T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)

(4.93)

such that
g(0)=0
and
g
is
\Gamma
-equivariant. In particular, the submanifolds
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are transverse at
0\in\ker\tilde{D}
.

Proof.

Apply the implicit function theorem to the
\Gamma
-equivariant map
F_{1}
, noting that
DF_{1}(0)|_{T_{0}Z}={\bf w}_{\varphi}
is an isomorphism.
∎

To continue with the bifurcation analysis, we need to understand the natural projection
Z^{\prime}\subset\ker\tilde{D}\to\mathbb{R}_{t}
, which is equivalent to understanding the smooth function

\displaystyle\ker\varphi^{*}D\xrightarrow{g}T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\xrightarrow{t}\mathbb{R}_{t}.

(4.94)

This will be more convenient to do in explicit coordinates which we now introduce. Recall that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker\varphi^{*}D=1
and fix an element
0\neq\sigma\in\ker\varphi^{*}D
for the rest of this section. Using
\sigma
, we will sometimes regard the map
\varsigma
from (
4.77
) as an
\mathbb{R}
-valued linear functional on
\ker\tilde{D}
(with
\sigma
corresponding to
1
). Finally, let
{\bf z}:T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)\to\mathbb{C}^{r}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism. Thus, the splitting (
4.79
) now gives us explicit coordinates

\displaystyle\ker\tilde{D}=T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)\xrightarrow{(t,{\bf z},\varsigma)}\mathbb{R}_{t}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{r}_{\bf z}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{\varsigma}.

(4.95)

We also obtain (non-canonical) splittings
\ker\tilde{D}=T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)\oplus\ker\varphi^{*}D
and
T_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,\gamma,d)=\mathbb{R}_{t}\oplus T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,d)
which we will use without further comment below.

Orient the
1
-dimensional space
\ker\varphi^{*}D
using
\sigma
. Then the natural orientation on
\text{Hom}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)
gives rise to an orientation on
{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D
. Using the isomorphism
{\bf w}_{\varphi}
, the element
\sigma
and the coordinates (
4.95
), we can identify

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D=\mathbb{R}_{t}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{r}_{\bf z}.

(4.96)

This isomorphism preserves orientation since
\text{sgn}({\bf w}_{\varphi})=+1
.
With these choices of coordinates, we can write the Kuranishi map as

\displaystyle F(t,{\bf z},\varsigma)=(\text{id}+A(t,{\bf z},\varsigma))\cdot(\varsigma-f(t,{\bf z}))((t,{\bf z})-g(\varsigma))

(4.97)

where
f\equiv_{1}0
is as in (
4.83
),
g
is as in (
4.93
) and
A
is a
(1+2r)\times(1+2r)
real matrix valued function with
A(0)=0
. The factorization in (
4.97
) comes from the fact that the zeros of
F
are exactly the points of
Z
(zeros of the factor
\varsigma-f(t,{\bf z})
) and the points of
Z^{\prime}
(zeros of the factor
(t,{\bf z})-g(\varsigma)
). Moreover, as noted in Corollary
4.23
, the submanifolds
Z,Z^{\prime}\subset\ker\tilde{D}
have a transverse intersection at
0
. Now, recall that the zeros of
F
represent stable maps in
\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
lying close to
(0,\varphi)
in the Gromov topology and we are interested in determining which of these zeros correspond to
embedded
curves (all the other zeros must correspond to multiple covers of embedded curves since the path
\gamma
misses
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
). The points in
Z
represent multiple covers of the embedded curves
\Sigma_{t}
and we are left to determine whether or not the points in
Z^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
represent
embedded
pseudo-holomorphic curves of genus
h
and class
dA
. This depends on the section
\sigma\in\ker\varphi^{*}D
as we explain below.

We begin by recalling that the normal bundle
N_{\Sigma}
admits an almost complex structure
\hat{J}^{0}_{\infty}
such that the projection
N_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\Sigma
is pseudo-holomorphic and the element
\sigma\in\ker\varphi^{*}D
can be regarded as a
\hat{J}^{0}_{\infty}
-holomorphic map
\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N_{\Sigma}
(cf.
[
27
, Equation (B.1) and Lemma B.4]
, see also Lemma
5.9
).

Lemma 4.24
.

\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N_{\Sigma}
is a multiply covered map if and only if there is a subgroup
H\subsetneq H^{\prime}\subset G
with
(\theta^{*})^{H^{\prime}}=(\theta^{*})^{H}\neq 0
.
Moreover, in this case,

(1)

\varphi
is unramified, i.e.,
r=0
and,

(2)

if
\Sigma^{\prime}\to C\to\Sigma
is an intermediate cover to which
\sigma
descends, then any element of
{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D
also descends to
C
. In this case,
C\to\Sigma
also determines a non-trivial elementary wall type.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma^{\prime}=\tilde{\Sigma}/H\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Sigma
be a Galois closure.
If there exists a subgroup
H\subsetneq H^{\prime}\subset G
with
(\theta^{*})^{H^{\prime}}=(\theta^{*})^{H}\neq 0
, then
\zeta:\tilde{\Sigma}/H^{\prime}\to\Sigma
corresponds to a non-trivial elementary wall type. Indeed, condition (1) of Definition
4.1
holds for
\zeta
since it does for
\varphi
and condition (2) follows from
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta^{*})^{H^{\prime}}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(\theta^{*})^{H}=1
. Equation (
4.33
) applied to
\varphi
and
\zeta
now yields the identification
\ker\zeta^{*}D=\ker\varphi^{*}D
given by pullback along
\Sigma^{\prime}=\tilde{\Sigma}/H\to\tilde{\Sigma}/H^{\prime}
. Thus,
\hat{\sigma}
(or equivalently
\sigma
) descends to
\tilde{\Sigma}/H^{\prime}
and thus,
\hat{\sigma}
is a multiply covered map.

Conversely, suppose
\hat{\sigma}
is multiply covered. Then, it must descend to
\zeta:\tilde{\Sigma}/H^{\prime}\to\Sigma
for some subgroup
H\subsetneq H^{\prime}\subset G
. This implies that we have
(\theta^{*})^{H^{\prime}}\neq 0
and thus,
(\theta^{*})^{H^{\prime}}=(\theta^{*})^{H}
since the latter is
1
-dimensional by Lemma
4.15
. This, in particular, shows that
\zeta
is elementary by the argument of the previous paragraph. It follows that the (co)kernels of
\varphi^{*}D
and
\zeta^{*}D
are identified, using formulas (
4.33
) and (
4.34
). This proves the statement about the descent of elements in
{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D
. It also follows that the indices of
\varphi^{*}D
and
\zeta^{*}D
are the same, i.e., the number
n_{\varphi}
of zeros of
d\varphi
is the same as the number
n_{\zeta}
of zeros of
d\zeta
(when counted with multiplicity). This cannot happen with
[H^{\prime}:H]>1
, unless
\varphi
is unramified.
∎

We now split our discussion into two cases depending on whether
\hat{\sigma}
is a simple or multiply covered
\hat{J}^{0}_{\infty}
-holomorphic map.

4.4.1.
Case 1 (
\hat{\sigma}
is multiply covered)

By Lemma
4.24
we can now assume that
\varphi
is unramified and that both
\ker\varphi^{*}D
and
{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D
are pulled back from some intermediate cover
\Sigma^{\prime}=\tilde{\Sigma}/H\to C=\tilde{\Sigma}/H^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\zeta}\Sigma
with
\zeta
being of degree
d^{\prime}
and
C
being of genus
h^{\prime}
. Since both
\varphi
and
\zeta
determine non-trivial elementary wall types, we can compare the Kuranishi models for
(0,\varphi)
and
(0,\zeta)
and we find that they are compatible under the natural pullback maps

\displaystyle T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\zeta)}\mathcal{M}_{h^{\prime}}(X,\gamma,d^{\prime}A)

\displaystyle\to T^{\text{Zar}}_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)

(4.98)

\displaystyle E_{(0,\zeta)}\mathcal{M}_{h^{\prime}}(X,\gamma,d^{\prime}A)

\displaystyle\to E_{(0,\varphi)}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)

(4.99)

both of which are isomorphisms. Thus, every curve close to
(0,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
is a
(d/d^{\prime})
-fold cover of a curve close to
(0,\zeta)\in\mathcal{M}_{h^{\prime}}(X,\gamma,d^{\prime}A)
. Thus, the space
\mathcal{M}_{h}^{*}(X,\gamma,dA)
is empty near
(0,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.12
(i) in view of Lemma
4.24
.

4.4.2.
Case 2 (
\hat{\sigma}
is simple)

Lemma 4.25
.

\Gamma=\text{Aut}(\varphi)\subset\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\Gamma
acts faithfully on the
1
-dimensional space
(\theta^{*})^{H}
. Suppose to the contrary that there is an element
h^{\prime}\in N_{G}(H)\setminus H
which acts trivially on
(\theta^{*})^{H}
. Letting
H^{\prime}
be the subgroup of
N_{G}(H)
generated by
H
and
h^{\prime}
, we find that
(\theta^{*})^{H^{\prime}}=(\theta^{*})^{H}\neq 0
. By Lemma
4.24
, we see that this is a contradiction to the simplicity of the
\hat{J}^{0}_{\infty}
-holomorphic map
\hat{\sigma}
.
∎

Lemma 4.26

(Nearby embedded curves)
.

All points in
Z^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
which are sufficiently close to
0\in\ker\tilde{D}
are mapped into
\mathcal{M}_{h}^{*}(X,\gamma,dA)
by the map
\Xi
from (
4.74
).

Proof.

Since
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, it avoids
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
and it is therefore enough to show that the curves in question are simple. We argue by contradiction. To this end, suppose
(t_{n},\varphi_{n}^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to X)
is a sequence in
\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\gamma,dA)
converging to
(0,\varphi)
such that for each
n\geq 1
, we have the following properties.

(1)

(t_{n},\varphi_{n}^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to X)
is the image under
\Xi
of a point
(t_{n},\varphi_{n},\sigma_{n})\in\ker\tilde{D}
lying in
Z^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
. Here, we are using the splitting (
4.79
).

(2)

\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
is a multiply covered
J_{t_{n}}
-holomorphic map.

The submanifolds
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
of
\ker\tilde{D}
have a transverse intersection at
0
(by Corollary
4.23
) and the points of
Z
correspond to multiple covers of the embedded
\gamma(t)
-curves
\Sigma_{t}
defined in the discussion following (
4.32
). Thus, (1) implies that, for
n\gg 1
, the
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
are not covers of the embedded
\gamma(t_{n})
-curves
\Sigma_{t_{n}}\subset X
. Arguing as in the proof of
[
27
, Theorem B.1]
, we conclude that by rescaling the
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
(and the almost complex structures
J_{t_{n}}
) suitably in the normal directions to
\Sigma_{t_{n}}
(and passing to a subsequence), we obtain a Gromov limit
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N_{\Sigma}
which is
\hat{J}^{0}_{\infty}
-holomorphic and corresponds (under
[
27
, Lemma B.4]
) to an element
0\neq\sigma^{\prime}\in\ker(\varphi^{*}D)
. Since
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker\varphi^{*}D=1
and
\hat{\sigma}
is simple, it follows that
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
is also simple. Since being a simple map is an open property, it follows that the rescalings of the
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}
in the convergent subsequence are also simple maps for
n\gg 1
. As a result, for
n\gg 1
, the maps
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to X
themselves are also simple
J_{t_{n}}
-holomorphic maps and this contradicts (2).
∎

The next statement crucially depends on the technical results of §
4.4.3
and the fact that the path
\gamma
lies in the Baire set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
(by Lemma
4.10
).

Lemma 4.27
.

The map (
4.94
) has a non-trivial Taylor expansion at the origin. More precisely, there exists an integer
1\leq n\leq d
and a real number
c\neq 0
with

\displaystyle(t\circ g)(\varsigma)\equiv_{n}c\varsigma^{n}.

(4.100)

Proof.

In the course of the proof, we will be using the definitions and results of §
4.4.3
, to which we refer the reader for details. Examining Definition
4.29
, the expression (
4.97
