) for the Kuranishi map
F
and using Remark
A.3
to ignore the factor
\text{id}+A(t,{\bf z},\varsigma)
in (
4.97
), we see that the function
t\circ g
has a non-trivial
d
-jet at
0
if and only if the point
p=(J,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\iota)
does not lie on the degeneracy locus
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}
from Definition
4.29
. But this is guaranteed by the fact that
\gamma
belongs to the Baire set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
(see Lemma
4.10
). We note that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
being a Baire set ultimately rests on the fact (proved in Lemma
4.38
of §
4.4.3
below) that the degeneracy locus is of codimension
\geq 1
in
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
.
∎

Using Lemma
4.27
, we will now determine the signed count of the zeros of
F
in
Z^{\prime}
for small
t\neq 0
. We divide into cases based on the sign of
c
and the parity of
n
. (If
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, then
n
must necessarily be even.)

(1)

c\neq 0
,
n
odd
. The Taylor expansion (
4.100
) shows that for each small
t_{0}\neq 0
we have a unique
\varsigma_{0}
such that
t_{0}=(t\circ g)(\varsigma_{0})
. Moreover, since
\bf z
is a complex coordinate, the sign of this zero can be determined by looking at the unique nonzero solution
\varsigma=\varsigma_{0}=(c^{-1}t_{0})^{1/n}
of the simplified equation

\displaystyle\varsigma(t_{0}-c\varsigma^{n})=0.

(4.101)

We compute of the derivative at this point

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{d}{d\varsigma}\,\varsigma(t_{0}-c\varsigma^{n})|_{\varsigma=\varsigma_{0}}=t_{0}-(n+1)c\varsigma_{0}^{n}=-nt_{0}

(4.102)

and find that the sign of the zero is
+1
for
t<0
and
-1
for
t>0
.

(2)

c>0
,
n
even
. The Taylor expansion (
4.100
) shows that for small
t_{0}>0
, there are exactly two distinct solutions
\varsigma_{0}\sim\pm(c^{-1}t_{0})^{1/n}
to
t_{0}=(t\circ g)(\varsigma_{0})
while there are no solutions for small
t_{0}<0
. Again, we can compute the signs of these zeros, by considering the model equation (
4.101
), and we find that both of these zeros have the sign
-1
.

(3)

c<0
,
n
even
. Arguing exactly as in the previous case, we find that there are exactly two zeros
\varsigma_{0}\sim\pm(c^{-1}t_{0})^{1/n}
for small
t_{0}<0
(both with sign
+1
) and no zeros for small
t_{0}>0
.

Taking the
\Gamma
-symmetry into account and shrinking
\mathcal{O}
and
\epsilon
if necessary, we find that in all cases
the signed number of embedded curves in a sufficiently small Gromov neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
reduces by the amount
2/|\Gamma|\in\mathbb{Z}
when
\gamma
goes from
-\epsilon<t<0
to
0<t<\epsilon
. Except for the fact that Lemma
4.10
(used in the proof of Lemma
4.27
) relied on Lemma
4.38
(proved in the following subsection), we have now completed the proof of Theorem
4.12
. ∎

Figure 1.
A schematic picture of the moduli space (when
n
is odd); see the discussion below Lemma
4.27
.

4.4.3.
Non-degeneracy of Taylor expansion

For any point

\displaystyle\hat{p}=(\hat{J},C,\hat{\varphi}:C^{\prime}\to C,\hat{\iota})\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c}),

(4.103)

such that
(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
is a non-trivial elementary wall type (see Definition
4.1
), the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)
of
\hat{J}
-holomorphic maps of genus
h
and class
dA
(up to isomorphism) can be described locally near
\hat{\varphi}
, in the sense of the discussion surrounding (
4.74
), using a Kuranishi map

\displaystyle F_{\hat{p}}:T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)\to E_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)

(4.104)

with the domain and target of (
4.104
) being defined following §
4.2.1
and §
4.2.2
. In particular, we have a short exact sequence and an isomorphism

\displaystyle 0\to T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\to

\displaystyle T^{\text{Zar}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)\xrightarrow{\hat{\varsigma}}\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}\to 0

(4.105)

\displaystyle E_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)={\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}

(4.106)

where
\hat{D}=D^{N}_{C,\hat{J}}
is the normal deformation operator of embedded
\hat{J}
-curve
C\subset X
. Arguing as in the discussion surrounding (
4.81
), we express the submanifold
\hat{Z}\subset T^{\text{Zar}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)
corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
as a regular level set
\hat{Z}=(\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)
for a smooth map
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}:T^{\text{Zar}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)\to\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
satisfying
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}\equiv_{1}\hat{\varsigma}
. Using
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}
, we can write
F_{\hat{p}}=\langle\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime},F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}\rangle
with

\displaystyle F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}:T^{\text{Zar}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)\to\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})

(4.107)

being the smooth map defined in analogy with the map
F_{1}
from (
4.81
). It follows from Corollary
A.2
that the linear map

\displaystyle DF^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}}(0):T^{\text{Zar}}_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)\to\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}),

(4.108)

which can be determined from
D^{2}F_{\hat{p}}(0)
, is well-defined (independent of the choices involved in the construction of the Kuranishi map
F_{\hat{p}}
as well as the choice of the defining equation
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}
for
\hat{Z}
satisfying
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}\equiv_{1}\hat{\varsigma}
). The same argument as in Lemma
4.22
shows that the restriction of
DF^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}}(0)
to the linear subspace
T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
recovers the natural linear map

\displaystyle T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\to\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}).

(4.109)

analogous to the bottom horizontal map in (
4.54
). Denote the image of (
4.109
) by
V_{\hat{\varphi}}
. Similarly, denote the image of the linear map

\displaystyle T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\otimes\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}\to{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}

(4.110)

associated to (
4.109
) by
W_{\hat{\varphi}}
. Note that the injectivity of (
4.109
) is equivalent to that of (
4.110
) which is, in turn, equivalent to
W_{\hat{\varphi}}\subset{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
being of codimension
1
or, equivalently, to
V_{\hat{\varphi}}\subset\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})
being of codimension
1
. This is because
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}=1
(since
\hat{p}
is non-trivial elementary), the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
has complex dimension
r
, and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}=2r+1
.

Now, suppose that (
4.109
) is injective but that (
4.108
) is not an isomorphism. We further apply Kuranishi reduction to
F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}
from (
4.107
). Under these assumptions, the map
DF_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}(0)
from (
4.108
) must have
1
-dimensional kernel and cokernel with identifications

\displaystyle\ker DF_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}(0)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}

(4.111)

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}DF_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}(0)

\displaystyle=\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})/V_{\hat{\varphi}}

(4.112)

where the isomorphism (
4.111
) is induced by the projection
\hat{\varsigma}
from (
4.105
). Then (
4.111
) and (
4.112
) respectively prescribe the domain and target of this further Kuranishi reduction. In fact, (
4.111
) provides a splitting of the short exact sequence (
4.105
) and this splitting gives a preferred choice
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}=\hat{\varsigma}-\hat{f}
of defining function for
\hat{Z}
, by writing
\hat{Z}
as the graph of a function
\hat{f}:T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\to\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
with
\hat{f}(0)=0
and
D\hat{f}(0)=0
. This preferred choice of
\hat{\varsigma}^{\prime}
also determines
F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}
. Now, applying Kuranishi reduction (see Appendix
A
for a review) to the smooth map
F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}
and using the identifications (
4.111
)–(
4.112
), we obtain a corresponding Kuranishi map
10
10

10

To see the relevance of
G_{\hat{p}}
, recall from Lemma
4.27
that one is interested in the Taylor expansion of
t\circ g
at
0
. The main point is then that
G_{\hat{p}}
is obtained from
F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}
in analogy with how
t\circ g
was obtained from
F_{1}
(the reduced version of
F
) in §
4.4
.

\displaystyle G_{\hat{p}}:\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}\to\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})/V_{\hat{\varphi}}

(4.113)

with
G_{\hat{p}}(0)=0
and
DG_{\hat{p}}(0)=0
. By Corollary
A.2
, the leading order Taylor coefficient of
G_{\hat{p}}
is well-defined independent of the choices made in constructing
G_{\hat{p}}
. Explicitly, if all terms of order
<n
in the Taylor expansion of
G_{\hat{p}}
at
0
vanish for some integer
n>1
, then we may view the order
n
term in its Taylor expansion invariantly as a linear map

\displaystyle D^{n}G_{\hat{p}}(0):(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\otimes n}\to\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})/V_{\hat{\varphi}}

(4.114)

between
1
-dimensional (real) vector spaces.

Note that the Kuranishi map
G_{\hat{p}}
is defined under the assumption that (
4.108
) fails to be surjective. To avoid confusion, we introduce linear maps

\displaystyle T^{(n)}_{\hat{p}}:(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\otimes n}\to({\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})/W_{\hat{\varphi}}

(4.115)

for integers
n>1
as follows for subsequent discussions. For
n=2
, we define
T^{(2)}_{\hat{p}}
as in (
4.115
) via the map
\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}\to\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})/V_{\hat{\varphi}}
induced on quotients by
DF_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}(0)
from (
4.108
). If
T^{(2)}_{\hat{p}}
vanishes, then we are in the setting of the previous paragraph and the smooth map
G_{\hat{p}}
as in (
4.113
) can be constructed. With this in mind, whenever
n>1
is an integer with
T^{(k)}_{\hat{p}}=0
for
2\leq k\leq n
, we define
T^{(n+1)}_{\hat{p}}
as in (
4.115
) via
D^{n}G_{\hat{p}}(0)
.

Remark 4.28
.

It is conceivable that we have
T^{(n)}_{\hat{p}}=0
for all
n>1
.

Definition 4.29

(Degeneracy locus)
.

Fix a non-trivial elementary wall type
(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
.
We define the
degeneracy locus

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}\subset\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})

(4.116)

to be the subspace consisting of all points
\hat{p}=(\hat{J},C,\hat{\varphi}:C^{\prime}\to C,\hat{\iota})
such that

(1)

W_{\hat{\varphi}}
from (
4.110
) is a codimension
1
subspace of
{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
and,

(2)

the linear maps
T^{(n)}_{\hat{p}}
from (
4.115
) vanish for
2\leq n\leq d+1
.

Note that condition (1) in Definition
4.29
is open (as it is an injectivity condition) while condition (2) is closed (as it is a vanishing condition). As a result, (
4.116
) is the inclusion of a locally closed subspace.

Remark 4.30
.

The reader is justified in wondering why the quantities
T^{(n)}_{\hat{p}}
in Definition
4.29
need to be defined indirectly (using two Kuranishi reductions instead of one). A suggestion for a simpler definition (with the view of proving Lemma
4.27
) could be to project the Taylor coefficients of
F_{\hat{p}}
(or
F_{\hat{p}}^{\text{red}}
) suitably to obtain
T^{(n)}_{\hat{p}}
. However, we can exhibit an example showing that the non-vanishing of (one of) these is not sufficient to obtain Lemma
4.27
(which is the main intended application of Definition
4.29
) as follows. Using the notation of (
4.97
), suppose that we have
r=2
,
{\bf z}=(z_{1},z_{2})
and

\displaystyle F(t,z_{1},z_{2},\varsigma)=\varsigma(t-\varsigma|z_{1}|^{2}+\varsigma^{5},z_{1}-\varsigma^{2},z_{2}+\varsigma^{3}).

(4.117)

Solving
F={\bf 0}
, we either get
\varsigma=0
or we get
z_{1}=\varsigma^{2}
,
z_{2}=-\varsigma^{3}
and
t=\varsigma|z_{1}|^{2}-\varsigma^{5}=0
, violating the conclusion of Lemma
4.27
. Indeed, in this case, the function
\varsigma^{-1}F(0,z_{1},z_{2},\varsigma)=(-\varsigma|z_{1}|^{2}+\varsigma^{5},z_{1}-\varsigma^{2},z_{2}+\varsigma^{3})
is the counterpart of
F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}}
from (
4.107
) and its Kuranishi reduction
\varsigma\mapsto G_{\hat{p}}(\varsigma)
is identically zero (and, in particular, has vanishing Taylor expansion).

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to showing that (
4.116
) is an inclusion of codimension
\geq 1
(Lemma
4.38
).

Lemma 4.31
.

Let
\hat{p}=(\hat{J},C,\hat{\varphi}:C^{\prime}\to C,\hat{\iota})
be a point of
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
such that
(g,d,{\bf b},G,{\bf k},{\bf c},A)
is a non-trivial elementary wall type. Denote by
\hat{D}=D^{N}_{C,\hat{J}}
the normal deformation operator of
C
. Then, for any

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\neq\xi\in\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}

(4.118)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\neq\xi^{\prime}\in\ker(\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\dagger}=({\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{*}

(4.119)

we can find an integer
1\leq n\leq d
and a point
q\in C
over which
\hat{\varphi}
is unramified and the element

\displaystyle\sum_{\tilde{q}\in\varphi^{-1}(q)}(\xi^{\otimes n+1}\otimes\xi^{\prime})(\tilde{q})\in\text{Sym}_{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}N_{C,q}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}N^{\dagger}_{C,q}

(4.120)

is nonzero. Here, recall that the bundle
N^{\dagger}_{C}
is defined as in (
2.3
).

Proof.

Choose a Galois closure
\tilde{C}\to\tilde{C}/H=C^{\prime}\to C
. Enlarge the subgroup
H\subset G
to a maximal subgroup
H^{\prime}
such that we have
{\bf k}^{H^{\prime}}={\bf k}^{H}\neq 0
. Applying Lemma
4.24
, we see that
\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
and
{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
both descend to the intermediate cover
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}:\tilde{C}/H^{\prime}\to C
. The element defined by the formula (
4.120
) with
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}
in place of
\hat{\varphi}
differs from (
4.120
) just by a factor of
[H^{\prime}:H]\geq 1
. Thus, there is no loss of generality in assuming that
H^{\prime}=H
.

Using this assumption, it follows that for all points
q\in C
, outside of a closed and at most countable set,
\xi(\hat{\varphi}^{-1}(q))\subset N_{C,q}
has
d
distinct nonzero elements. Choose such a point
q
. By perturbing it slightly if necessary, we may also assume that
\xi^{\prime}(\tilde{q})\neq 0
for some
\tilde{q}\in\hat{\varphi}^{-1}(q)
. Enumerate the pre-images of
q
under
\hat{\varphi}
as
\tilde{q}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{q}_{d}\in C^{\prime}
. Choose linear functionals

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle:N_{C,q}\to\mathbb{R}

(4.121)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle:N^{\dagger}_{C,q}\to\mathbb{R}

(4.122)

such that the
d
numbers
x_{i}=\Lambda(\xi(\tilde{q}_{i}))
are
all distinct and nonzero
and the
d
numbers
y_{j}=\Lambda^{\prime}(\xi^{\prime}(\tilde{q}_{j}))
are
not all zero
. This implies that the product

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{2}&x_{2}^{2}&\cdots&x_{d}^{2}\\
x_{1}^{3}&x_{2}^{3}&\cdots&x_{d}^{3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{1}^{d+1}&x_{2}^{d+1}&\cdots&x_{d}^{d+1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{d}\end{bmatrix}

(4.123)

cannot be zero, since the matrix is invertible (its determinant is
\prod_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}
times the Vandermonde determinant associated to the
d
distinct numbers
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
) and the column vector is nonzero. Take
n
to be the smallest number such that the
n^{\text{th}}
entry of this matrix product is nonzero. It then follows that

\displaystyle\left\langle\text{Sym}^{n+1}_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{\prime},\sum_{\tilde{q}\in\varphi^{-1}(q)}(\xi^{\otimes n+1}\otimes\xi^{\prime})(\tilde{q})\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{n+1}y_{i}\neq 0.

(4.124)

which shows that this
n
suffices.
∎

The next remark will be useful to clarify of the statement of Lemma
4.33
.

Remark 4.32
.

Let
E\to M
be a vector bundle on a smooth manifold and let
s
be a smooth section over
M
of
E
. Suppose
n\geq 0
is an integer and
x\in M
is a point such that, in some (and therefore every) choice of local trivialization of
E
and local coordinates centered at
x
, the section
s
is represented by a function with vanishing
n
-jet. Then,
s
has a well-defined
(n+1)^{\text{st}}
derivative
D^{n+1}s|_{x}\in\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T_{x}M,E_{x})
. Moreover, a straightforward computation in local coordinates shows that for
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\in T_{x}M
,

\displaystyle D^{n+1}s|_{x}(v_{0},\ldots,v_{n})=(\nabla_{V_{0}}\cdots\nabla_{V_{n}}s)(x)

(4.125)

for any choice of connection
\nabla
on the vector bundle
E
and choices of vector fields
V_{0},\ldots,V_{n}
(extending the tangent vectors
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}
at
x
). In particular, the right side of (
4.125
) is invariant under permutation of the covariant derivatives
\nabla_{V_{i}}
. For
n=1
, an explicit way to see this is to use the identity

\displaystyle\nabla_{V_{0}}\nabla_{V_{1}}s=\nabla_{V_{1}}\nabla_{V_{0}}s+\nabla_{[V_{0},V_{1}]}s+F_{\nabla}(V_{0},V_{1})s

(4.126)

where
F_{\nabla}
is the curvature of
\nabla
. At
x\in M
, the last two terms on the right side of (
4.126
) vanish since the
1
-jet of
s
at
x
is assumed to vanish.

If we have a submanifold
x\in S\subset M
at whose points
s
has vanishing
n
-jet, a computation in local coordinates shows that
D^{n+1}s
descends to a linear bundle homomorphism
\text{Sym}^{n+1}N_{S/M}\to E|_{S}
.

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\hat{p}
,
\xi
,
\xi^{\prime}
and
1\leq n\leq d
be as in Lemma
4.31
. We can then find an element
\hat{K}\in T_{\hat{J}}\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)\subset\overline{\text{End}}_{\hat{J}}(T_{X})
such that it and its first
n
derivatives vanish along
C
and its
(n+1)^{\text{st}}
derivative, in the directions normal to
C
, projects to a smooth
\mathbb{R}
-linear bundle homomorphism

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{n+1}:\text{Sym}^{n+1}_{\mathbb{R}}N_{C}\to\overline{\text{Hom}}_{\hat{J}}(T_{C},N_{C})=\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}N_{C}

(4.127)

satisfying

\displaystyle\int_{C^{\prime}}\langle\hat{\varphi}^{*}(i\hat{K}_{n+1}),\xi^{\otimes n+1}\otimes\xi^{\prime}\rangle\neq 0

(4.128)

where
i
denotes multiplication by
\sqrt{-1}
on the fibers of
N_{C}
.

Proof.

We first construct a bundle homomorphism
L
of the type (
4.127
) satisfying (
4.128
) with
L
in place of
\hat{K}_{n+1}
. Choose a smooth linear bundle homomorphism

\displaystyle L^{\prime}:\text{Sym}_{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}N_{C}\to\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}N_{C}

(4.129)

which has positive pairing with (
4.120
) at the point
q\in C
yielded by Lemma
4.31
. Multiplying
L^{\prime}
by a non-negative cutoff function on
C
supported near
q
, we can assume that its pairing with (
4.120
) is
\geq 0
at all points of
C
and
>0
at
q
. Taking
L=iL^{\prime}
completes the construction. We refer the reader to
[
20
, Section 3.2]
for comparison and further details.

We are left to find
\hat{K}\in T_{\hat{J}}\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
such that it and its first
n
derivatives vanish along
C
and
L=\hat{K}_{n+1}
. To begin, note that
T_{\hat{J}}\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
consists of smooth sections of a vector sub-bundle
E\subset\overline{\text{End}}_{\hat{J}}(T_{X})
. The argument from
[
27
, Lemma 5.27]
shows that the natural restriction map

\displaystyle E|_{C}\to\overline{\text{Hom}}_{\hat{J}}(T_{C},N_{C})=\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}N_{C}

(4.130)

is surjective. Thus, we may lift
L
to a smooth linear bundle homomorphism
\tilde{L}:\text{Sym}^{n+1}_{\mathbb{R}}N_{C}\to E|_{C}
.

Next, choose a tubular neighborhood
\nu:N_{C}\hookrightarrow X
of
C
and an isomorphism
\pi^{*}(E|_{C})=\nu^{*}E
of vector bundles on
N_{C}
which is the identity on the zero section. Here,
\pi:N_{C}\to C
is the vector bundle projection. Viewing
\tilde{L}
as a fiberwise (real, homogeneous degree
n+1
) polynomial map
N_{C}\to E|_{C}
, we obtain a global smooth section of
\pi^{*}(E|_{C})=\nu^{*}E
. Multiplying this section of
\nu^{*}E
by a cutoff function of compact support which is
\equiv 1
near the zero section yields a section
\hat{K}
of
E
on
X
. By construction,
\hat{K}
and its first
n
derivatives vanish on
C
. This shows that
\hat{K}
has a well-defined
(n+1)^{\text{st}}
normal derivative at
C
(by Remark
4.32
) which, by construction, coincides with
\tilde{L}
. It now follows that
L=\hat{K}_{n+1}
.
∎

Now, fix any point

\displaystyle\hat{p}=(\hat{J},C,\hat{\varphi}:C^{\prime}\to C,\hat{\iota})\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}.

(4.131)

Having fixed
\hat{p}
and setting
\hat{D}=D^{N}_{C,\hat{J}}
, pick a section
0\neq\xi\in\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
(unique up to scaling). By condition (1) of Definition
4.29
, we can also pick a section, unique up to scaling,
0\neq\xi^{\prime}\in\ker(\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\dagger}=({\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{*}
which annihilates the image
W_{\hat{\varphi}}
of (
4.110
). Lemmas
4.31
and
4.33
then produce
1\leq n\leq d
and
\hat{K}
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
4.33
. We can now find a smooth path
\hat{\gamma}:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
, with
\hat{J}_{s}:=\hat{\gamma}(s)
, satisfying the following properties.

(1)

\hat{J}_{0}=\hat{J}
and
\frac{d}{ds}(\hat{J}_{s})|_{s=0}=\hat{K}
.

(2)

The path
\hat{\gamma}
fixes the
n
-jet of
\hat{J}
along
C
. More precisely, we have
|\hat{J}_{s}-\hat{J}|=O(|\nu_{C}|^{n+1})
for all
s
, where
\nu_{C}(\cdot)=\text{dist}(C,\cdot)
denotes the distance to
C
induced by the given Riemannian metric on
X
.

Following the discussion of §
4.3
, with
\hat{\gamma}
(resp.
\hat{\varphi}:C^{\prime}\to C
) taking the place of
\gamma
(resp.
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
), we choose auxiliary data (recall
\{\Delta_{[z]}\}_{[z]\in M/\Gamma}
and (1)–(4) from §
4.3
) to define Banach spaces
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{vf}}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{acs}}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{s}=\hat{\mathcal{X}}
,
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
and a smooth Fredholm map
\hat{\Phi}:\hat{\mathcal{X}}\to\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
(defined near
0
) by the formula

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi},\hat{y},s)=PT_{\nabla}\left[\hat{I}_{X,s}^{-1}\circ(\bar{\partial}_{\hat{J}_{s},j_{\hat{y}}}\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\hat{\xi})\circ\hat{I}_{C^{\prime},\hat{y}}\right]

(4.132)

analogous to (
4.67
). Here, we have used the notation

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{X,s}=\text{id}+\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\hat{J}\hat{A}_{X,s}:(T_{X},\hat{J})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(T_{X},\hat{J}_{s})

(4.133)

to denote the family of
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphisms with the tensor
\hat{A}_{X,s}
defined by the explicit formula

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{X,s}=-2\hat{J}(\text{id}+\hat{J}\hat{J}_{s})(\text{id}-\hat{J}\hat{J}_{s})^{-1}=-2\hat{J}(\hat{J}_{s}-\hat{J})(\hat{J}_{s}+\hat{J})^{-1}

(4.134)

analogous to (
4.65
). It follows from (
4.134
) that
\hat{A}_{X,s}
and its first
n
derivatives vanish along
C
. Write
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{vf}}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{acs}}
and, for each
s
, define the smooth Fredholm map

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{s}:\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{Y}}

(4.135)

by restricting
\hat{\Phi}
to
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\times\{s\}
, i.e.,
\hat{\Phi}_{s}(\hat{\xi},\hat{y})=\hat{\Phi}(\hat{\xi},\hat{y},s)
. The next observation makes precise the intuitive claim that the
n
-jet of
\hat{\Phi}_{0}
at
0
depends only on the
n
-jet of
\hat{J}
along
C\subset X
. In particular, it shows that we have

\displaystyle\hat{p}(s)=(\hat{J}_{s},C,\hat{\varphi}:C^{\prime}\to C,\hat{\iota})\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})

(4.136)

for each
s
, which defines a lift of the path
\hat{\gamma}
along the map (
2.17
).

Lemma 4.34
.

For each
s
, the smooth map

\displaystyle P_{s}=\hat{\Phi}_{s}-\hat{\Phi}_{0}:\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{Y}}.

(4.137)

satisfies
D^{k}P_{s}(0)=0
for
0\leq k\leq n
. For any
\tilde{\xi}\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, let
\hat{\xi}^{N}
denote its image under the projection
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{vf}}
followed by the projection
\hat{\varphi}^{*}T_{X}\to\hat{\varphi}^{*}N_{C}
. Then, we have the identity

\displaystyle\Pi^{N}\circ\textstyle\frac{d}{ds}|_{s=0}(D^{n+1}P_{s}|_{0}(\tilde{\xi},\ldots,\tilde{\xi}))=-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\varphi}^{*}(i\hat{K}_{n+1})(\hat{\xi}^{N},\ldots,\hat{\xi}^{N}),

(4.138)

where
\Pi^{N}
is the map on
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
induced by the projection
\hat{\varphi}^{*}T_{X}\to\hat{\varphi}^{*}N_{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\xi}=(\hat{\xi},\hat{y})\in\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
be given. It suffices to show that the function

\displaystyle\phi_{s}(\tau)=\hat{\Phi}_{s}(\tau\hat{\xi},\tau\hat{y})

(4.139)

of (small)
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
has the properties that
D^{k}\phi_{s}(0)
for
0\leq k\leq n
is independent of
s
and that
\Pi^{N}\circ\frac{d}{ds}|_{s=0}D^{n+1}\phi_{s}(0)
is given by the right side of (
4.138
). The key idea to compute the derivatives of
\phi_{s}
explicitly is to re-interpret them as covariant derivatives. For this purpose, it is convenient to assume that
\hat{\xi}
is smooth (the case of general
\hat{\xi}
follows by the density of smooth sections in
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{vf}}
). In the argument that follows,
s
will always range over
(-1,1)
, while
\tau
will range over a small interval centered at
0
(the exact size of which is irrelevant). For notational convenience, we just write
s,\tau\in\mathbb{R}
without repeatedly specifying the intervals over which
s,\tau
range.

Consider the
\mathbb{C}
-vector bundle on
X\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
given by (the pullback of)
T_{X}
, where we equip the copy of
T_{X}
over
X\times\{s\}
with the almost complex structure
\hat{J}_{s}
. Using the isomorphisms
\hat{I}_{X,s}
from (
4.133
), we can regard this bundle as the pullback of
(T_{X},\hat{J})
along the projection
X\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to X
and this endows it with a
\mathbb{C}
-linear connection by pulling back the already fixed
\mathbb{C}
-linear connection
\nabla
on
(T_{X},\hat{J})
. In particular, contracting with
\partial/\partial s
annihilates the curvature form of this pullback connection. Similarly, choose a
j_{0}
-linear connection on
T_{C^{\prime}}
and use the isomorphisms
\hat{I}_{C^{\prime},\hat{y}}
to endow the
\mathbb{C}
-vector bundle given by (the pullback of)
T_{C^{\prime}}
over
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{\tau}
, equipped with
j_{\tau\hat{y}}
over
C^{\prime}\times\{\tau\}
, with a
\mathbb{C}
-linear connection.

Next, consider the
\mathbb{C}
-vector bundle
E\to C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\mathbb{R}_{\tau}
whose fiber over a point
(z,s,\tau)
is the space of
\mathbb{C}
-anti-linear maps

\displaystyle(T_{C^{\prime},z},j_{\tau\hat{y}}(z))\to(T_{X,x},\hat{J}_{s}(x)),

(4.140)

where
x=(\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\tau\hat{\xi})(z)
.
E
has an evident
\mathbb{C}
-linear connection (which we again denote by
\nabla
) induced by the previous paragraph. Explicitly, we get the bundle
E
by taking the bundle
\tilde{E}
on
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{\tau}\times X\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
with fiber over a point
(z,\tau,x,s)
given by the space of
\mathbb{C}
-anti-linear maps (
4.140
) and then pulling back under the map

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\mathbb{R}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\to C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{\tau}\times X\times\mathbb{R}_{s}

(4.141)

\displaystyle(z,s,\tau)

\displaystyle\mapsto(z,\tau,(\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\tau\hat{\xi})(z),s)

(4.142)

which sends the vector field
\partial/\partial s
to the vector field
\partial/\partial s
. From the previous paragraph, it follows that the curvature form of
\tilde{E}
(and therefore of
E
) is annihilated by contraction with
\partial/\partial s
. In particular,
[\nabla_{s},\nabla_{\tau}]=0
on
E
.

Now,
E
has a smooth section
\psi
given by

\displaystyle\psi(z,s,\tau)=\bar{\partial}_{\hat{J}_{s},j_{\tau\hat{y}}}(\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\tau\hat{\xi})|_{z}

(4.143)

vanishing along
C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\{0\}
.
Noting that differentiating
\phi_{s}(\tau)
corresponds to
covariantly
differentiating
\psi(\cdot,s,\tau)
, we will be done once we show that
\nabla_{s}\nabla^{k}_{\tau}\psi
vanishes at
\tau=0
for each
0\leq k\leq n
and that
\nabla_{s}\nabla^{n+1}_{\tau}\psi
at
s=\tau=0
is given by the right side of (
4.138
) once we apply
\Pi^{N}
. Using the fact that
[\nabla_{s},\nabla_{\tau}]=0
on
E
, we can instead compute
\nabla^{k}_{\tau}\nabla_{s}\psi
at
\tau=0
. Below, we first compute
\nabla_{s}\psi
at
s=0
and then apply
\nabla_{\tau}
to the result (which is now defined on
C^{\prime}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}_{\tau}
)
k
times and finally set
\tau=0
.

For an
\mathbb{R}
-linear map
T
as in (
4.140
), denote its
\mathbb{C}
-linear (resp.
\mathbb{C}
-anti-linear) part by
T^{1,0}_{s,\tau}
(resp.
T^{0,1}_{s,\tau}
). A straightforward computation gives

\displaystyle T^{0,1}_{s,\tau}=\textstyle\frac{1}{2}(\text{id}+\hat{J}_{s}\hat{J})T^{1,0}_{0,\tau}+\frac{1}{2}(\text{id}-\hat{J}_{s}\hat{J})T^{0,1}_{0,\tau}.

(4.144)

Deducing
\hat{I}_{X,s}^{-1}=-\frac{1}{2}(\hat{J}_{s}+\hat{J})\hat{J}_{s}
from (
4.133
)–(
4.134
), we get

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{X,s}^{-1}\circ T^{0,1}_{s,\tau}-T^{0,1}_{0,\tau}=-\textstyle\frac{1}{4}(\hat{J}_{s}+\hat{J})(\hat{J}_{s}-\hat{J})T^{1,0}_{0,\tau}-\frac{1}{4}(\hat{J}\hat{J}_{s}+\hat{J}_{s}\hat{J}+2\cdot\text{id})T^{0,1}_{0,\tau}.

(4.145)

In formulas (
4.144
)–(
4.145
), we have omitted
z
and
x=(\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\tau\hat{\xi})(z)
from the notation for readability.
Applying (
4.145
) to
T=d(\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\tau\hat{\xi})|_{z}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{s}\psi(\cdot,0,\tau)=-\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\hat{J}\hat{K}\cdot\partial_{\hat{J},j_{\tau\hat{y}}}(\exp_{\hat{\varphi}}\tau\hat{\xi})

(4.146)

where we have used the
\hat{J}
-anti-linearity of
\hat{K}=\frac{d}{ds}(\hat{J}_{s})|_{s=0}
to eliminate the second term of (
4.145
). Since
\hat{K}
and its first
n
derivatives vanish along the image
C\subset X
of
\hat{\varphi}
, (
4.146
) shows that
\nabla^{k}_{\tau}\nabla_{s}\psi(\cdot,0,0)\equiv 0
for
0\leq k\leq n
. The same holds even at a general nonzero
s
since the section
\frac{d}{ds}\hat{J}_{s}
and its first
n
derivatives also vanish along
C\subset X
. Further, (
4.146
) shows that any term of
\nabla^{n+1}_{\tau}\nabla_{s}\psi(\cdot,0,0)
involving
\leq n
derivatives of
\hat{K}
has to vanish. Thus, the only term in
\nabla^{n+1}_{\tau}\nabla_{s}\psi(\cdot,0,0)
that can survive at
s=\tau=0
is the one involving the
(n+1)^{\text{st}}
derivative of
\hat{K}
(in the direction of
\hat{\xi}
).

This yields (
4.138
) once we recall the definition of
\hat{K}_{n+1}
, rewrite
\hat{J}
appearing in (
4.146
) as
i=\sqrt{-1}
and recall that
\hat{\varphi}
is
\hat{J}
-holomorphic. In more detail, the reason for why only
\hat{\xi}^{N}
(and not the full
\hat{\xi}
) appears in (
4.138
) is because of the second paragraph of Remark
4.32
and the reason for why
\Pi^{N}
appears in (
4.138
) is because
\hat{K}_{n+1}
is the image of the full
(n+1)^{\text{st}}
derivative of
\hat{K}
over
C
under the projection
\overline{\text{End}}_{\hat{J}}(T_{X}|_{C})\to\overline{\text{Hom}}_{\hat{J}}(T_{C},N_{C})
. Using the notation in the proof of Lemma
4.33
, the full
(n+1)^{\text{st}}
derivative of
\hat{K}
over
C
is the bundle map
\tilde{L}
, while
\hat{K}_{n+1}=L
.
∎

Lemma
4.34
shows that
D\hat{\Phi}_{s}(0):\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\to\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
is independent of
s
and, as a result, we get canonical identifications of kernels and cokernels

\displaystyle T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J}_{s},dA)

\displaystyle=T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)

(4.147)

\displaystyle E_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J}_{s},dA)

\displaystyle=E_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)

(4.148)

and a
1
-parameter family of Kuranishi maps

\displaystyle F_{\hat{p}(s)}:T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)\to E_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)

(4.149)

obtained by applying Kuranishi reduction to the maps
\hat{\Phi}_{s}
(using the same choice of splittings of
\ker D\hat{\Phi}(0)\to\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
and
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\text{coker }}D\hat{\Phi}(0)
for each
s
; see Appendix
A
for details on splittings). Lemma
A.4
applies (by virtue of Lemma
4.34
) to show that the maps
D^{k}F_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)
are
s
-independent for
0\leq k\leq n
and that the first variation of
D^{n+1}(F_{\hat{p}(s)}-F_{\hat{p}})(0)
can be computed using (
4.138
). Since
\hat{p}=\hat{p}(0)
lies on the degeneracy locus, we have a preferred splitting (see the discussion surrounding (
4.111
) for details)

\displaystyle T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)=T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\oplus\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}

(4.150)

of (
4.105
). Thus, we may express the submanifold
\hat{Z}_{s}\subset T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)
corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
contained in the zero locus of
F_{\hat{p}(s)}
as the graph of a smooth function

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{s}:T_{\hat{\varphi}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\to\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}

(4.151)

with
\hat{f}_{s}(0)=0
and
D\hat{f}_{s}(0)=0
. In fact, since the submanifolds
\hat{Z}_{s}
are
s
-independent, so are the functions
\hat{f}_{s}
. Indeed, we have a (manifestly
s
-independent) submanifold of
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
and each
\hat{Z}_{s}
is just the isomorphic image in
T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)=\ker D\hat{\Phi}(0)
of this submanifold under the
s
-independent linear projection
\hat{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\to\ker D\hat{\Phi}(0)
. This leads to a factorization of the Kuranishi map

\displaystyle F_{\hat{p}(s)}=\langle\hat{\varsigma}-\hat{f},F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}\rangle

(4.152)

as explained in the paragraph preceding (
4.113
), where we have dropped the subscript from
\hat{f}_{s}
in view of its
s
-independence. The following purely algebraic observation will be useful.

Lemma 4.35
.

The
D^{k}F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)
is independent of
s
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
. After restricting to
\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}\subset T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)
, we have

\displaystyle D^{n+1}F_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)|_{\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}}=(n+1)\cdot D^{n}F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)|_{\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}}

(4.153)

for all
s
, where we make the natural identification

\displaystyle\text{Hom}((\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\otimes n+1},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})=

(4.154)

\displaystyle\text{Hom}((\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\otimes n},\text{Hom}(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D},{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})).

(4.155)

Proof.

The
s
-independence of
D^{k}F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
is a direct consequence of (
4.152
) and the
s
-independence of
D^{k}F_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)
for
0\leq k\leq n
. Using coordinates
(\hat{\bf z},\hat{\varsigma})
on
T_{\hat{\varphi}}^{\text{Zar}}\mathcal{M}_{h}(X,\hat{J},dA)
, similar to (
4.97
) and respecting the direct sum decomposition (
4.150
), we get

\displaystyle F_{\hat{p}(s)}({\bf 0},\hat{\varsigma})=\hat{\varsigma}\cdot F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}({\bf 0},\hat{\varsigma})

(4.156)

from (
4.152
). Differentiating (
4.156
)
n+1
times at
\hat{\varsigma}=0
yields (
4.153
).
∎

Lemma
4.35
shows that, for
2\leq k\leq n
, we must have
T^{(k)}_{\hat{p}(s)}\equiv T^{(k)}_{\hat{p}}=0
. The next observation computes
T^{(n+1)}_{\hat{p}(s)}
in terms of
D^{n}F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}(0)
.

Lemma 4.36
.

The linear map

\displaystyle(\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\otimes n+1}\to({\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})/W_{\hat{\varphi}}

(4.157)

obtained by restricting
D^{n}(F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}-F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}})(0)
to
\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
, using the identifications (
4.154
)–(
4.155
) and then passing to the quotient modulo the subspace
W_{\hat{\varphi}}\subset{\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
coincides with
T^{(n+1)}_{\hat{p}(s)}
.

Proof.

Recall that
T^{(n+1)}_{\hat{p}}=0
by assumption. If
n=1
, the result follows by inspection. If
n>1
, we apply Lemma
A.4
to the smooth maps
F^{\text{red}}_{\hat{p}(s)}
(which have
s
-independent
(n-1)
-jet at
0
by Lemma
4.35
) to get the result.
∎

Remark 4.37
.

Lemmas
4.35
and
4.36
show that the first variation of
T^{(n+1)}_{\hat{p}(s)}
is a nonzero multiple of the map of the type (
4.157
) induced by the first variation of
D^{n+1}(F_{\hat{p}(s)}-F_{\hat{p}})(0)
which, in turn, can be computed explicitly from Lemma
4.34
(with the help of Lemma
A.4
).

We are now in a position to prove our main technical result.

Lemma 4.38
.

The inclusion (
4.116
) has codimension
\geq 1
.

Proof.

Near any point of
\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{g}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})_{\spadesuit}
, we can choose auxiliary data (as in §
4.3
) and define Kuranishi models
F_{\hat{p}}
as in (
4.104
) for all points
\hat{p}\in\mathcal{M}^{d}_{{\bf b},G}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{*}(X,A);{\bf k},{\bf c})
in this neighborhood. Thus, in this neighborhood, the inclusion (
4.116
) is defined by the
d
equations

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{\hat{p}}=\cdots=T^{(d+1)}_{\hat{p}}=0.

(4.158)

Given any point
\hat{p}
satisfying (
4.158
), we will show that the linearization of this system of
d
equations has rank
\geq 1
. This will suffice to show that (
4.116
) is the inclusion of a
C^{\infty}
subvariety of codimension
\geq 1
(in the sense of
[
27
, Appendix C]
).

For the remainder of the proof, we freely use the notation established in the discussion starting from (
4.131
) and leading up to the statement of the present lemma. In particular, recall that we have
0\neq\xi\in\ker\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D}
,
0\neq\xi^{\prime}\in\ker(\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{\dagger}=({\text{coker }}\hat{\varphi}^{*}\hat{D})^{*}
annihilating
W_{\hat{\varphi}}
, an integer
1\leq n\leq d
and a tensor
\hat{K}
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
4.33
. By Remark
4.37
, in order to show that the first variation of
T^{(n+1)}_{\hat{p}(s)}
at
s=0
is nonzero, it suffices to check the that the number

\displaystyle\int_{C^{\prime}}\langle\hat{\varphi}^{*}(i\hat{K}_{n+1}),\xi^{\otimes n+1}\otimes\xi^{\prime}\rangle

(4.159)

is nonzero. This is exactly the content of Lemma
4.33
which concludes the proof that (
4.116
) is of codimension
\geq 1
near
\hat{p}
.
∎

5.
An extension of Taubes’ Gromov invariant to dimension
6

In this section, we will apply the results of the last two sections to prove Theorem
1.6
. The resulting invariant can be viewed as a partial extension of Taubes’ Gromov invariant (defined in
[
24
]
) to symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-folds.

5.1.
Linear wall crossing

The purpose of this subsection is to introduce the linear wall-crossing invariant (Definition
5.17
), which will serve as a “correction term” in the definition of virtual count of embedded curves of class
2A
in equation (
1.5
).

Fix a smooth closed oriented
2
-manifold
\Sigma
of genus
g
and a complex vector bundle
\pi:N\to\Sigma
of rank
2
such that
c_{1}(N)\cdot[\Sigma]=2g-2
. Such pairs
(\Sigma,N)
arise by considering the normal bundle of any embedded pseudo-holomorphic curve of genus
g
in a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold.

Definition 5.1

(Space of operators)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
denote the space of almost complex structures on
\Sigma
inducing the given orientation on
\Sigma
. For
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
, define the space

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)

(5.1)

to consist of pairs
(j,D)
, where
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
and
D
is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator on
N
with respect to
j
, i.e.,

\displaystyle D:\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,N)\to\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,N)

(5.2)

is a linear first order differential operator such that for all
s\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,N)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathbb{K})
, we have
D(fs)=(\bar{\partial}_{j}f)s+fDs
. This is a bundle of affine spaces over the contractible space
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
and therefore is also contractible. Every
(j,D)\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)
satisfies
{\text{ind }}D=0
.

Definition 5.2

(Ordinary Brill–Noether loci)
.

For
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and any integer
k\geq 1
, define the subspace

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)

(5.3)

to consist of those pairs
(j,D)
for which
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\ker D=k
. Also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{K}}(N).

(5.4)

Finally, define
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(N)\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
to be the set of pairs
(j,D)
such that there exists a section
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D
and a point
z\in\Sigma
such that
\sigma(z)=0
.

We remind the reader of the convention that all (co)dimensions appearing below are to be understood over
\mathbb{R}
, unless otherwise specified.

Lemma 5.3
.

We have the following codimension estimates on
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)
.

(i)

\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
is a submanifold of
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
of codimension
1
.

(ii)

For each
k\geq 2
,
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
is of codimension
\geq 4
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
.

(iii)

For each
k\geq 1
,
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)
is of codimension
\geq 2
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)
.

Proof.

Given any element
(j,D)\in\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)
, there exists (using
[
9
, Lemma 1.1.4]
for example) a smooth function

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)\supset\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(\ker D,{\text{coker }}D)

(5.5)

defined in a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
(j,D)
such that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{K}}^{k}(N)=\mathcal{S}^{-1}(0)
. Moreover, its linearization
d\mathcal{S}|_{(j,D)}
restricted to
\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(N,N))
, i.e. the space of variations of
D
with
j
held fixed, is given by

\displaystyle A\mapsto(\kappa\mapsto A\kappa\pmod{\text{im }D})

(5.6)

where
\kappa\in\ker D
.
The statements (i)–(iii) will therefore follow by proving suitable rank estimates on this map. It suffices to prove rank estimates for the “Petri map” (recall that
D^{\dagger}
is the Serre dual of
D
as in Definition
2.5
)

\displaystyle\ker D\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\ker D^{\dagger}\to\Omega^{0}(C,N\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N^{\dagger})

(5.7)

\displaystyle s\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}s^{\prime}\mapsto(z\mapsto s(z)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}s^{\prime}(z)).

(5.8)

Indeed,
A\in\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(N,N))
from (
5.6
) naturally lies in the dual space of the right side of (
5.7
) and thus, a rank estimate for (
5.7
) will imply one for (
5.6
) by duality.
By taking a
0\neq s\in\ker D
and
0\neq s^{\prime}\in\ker D^{\dagger}
and using unique continuation for solutions of Cauchy–Riemann equations, it follows that the Petri map always has
\mathbb{K}
-rank
\geq 1
. This proves (i) and (iii). Statement (ii) follows by using
[
9
, Remark 1.1.14]
(which is itself based on
[
11
, Lemma 4.4]
) to conclude that the rank of the Petri map is
\geq 2k
whenever
k\geq 2
and
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(N)
is of codimension
\geq 3
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(N)\subset\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
, Lemma
5.3
(ii) implies that it is enough to show that
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(N)\cap\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
is of codimension
\geq 3
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
.
To prove this, we argue as follows. Define

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}(N)\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times\Sigma

(5.9)

to consist of
(j,D,z)
such that
(j,D)\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
and the elements of
\ker D
vanish at
z
. It will be enough to show that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}(N)
is a submanifold of codimension
4
in
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times\Sigma
. Fix any point
(j,D,z)\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}(N)
. Choose metrics on
\Sigma
,
N
– this yields an
L^{2}
inner product on the space of sections of
N
. Fix a section
\sigma\in\ker D
with
\|\sigma\|_{L^{2}}=1
(note that
\sigma
is unique up to multiplication by
\pm 1
). Then, for every
(j^{\prime},D^{\prime})
in a sufficiently small neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
(j,D)
in
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
, there is a distinguished element
\sigma_{(j^{\prime},D^{\prime})}\in\ker D^{\prime}
determined by the condition that its
L^{2}
inner product with
\sigma
is
1
. Note that
\sigma_{(j,D)}=\sigma
by definition. We now have the smooth map

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\times\Sigma

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}N

(5.10)

\displaystyle(j^{\prime},D^{\prime},z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sigma_{(j^{\prime},D^{\prime})}(z^{\prime})

(5.11)

which is compatible with the projection to
\Sigma
and satisfies
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}^{\prime}(N)\cap(\mathcal{U}\times\Sigma)=\mathcal{E}^{-1}(0)
. It will now suffice to show that the linearization

\displaystyle d\mathcal{E}|_{(j,D,z)}:T_{(j,D)}\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\oplus T_{z}\Sigma\to N_{z}

(5.12)

is surjective. We will in fact show the stronger statement that variations in
D
with
j
and
z
held constant suffice to surject onto
N_{z}
. First, we need to find an explicit expression for this linearization. To this end, let
\Pi:\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,N)\to{\text{coker }}D
be the natural projection and let
Q:\text{im }D\to(\ker D)^{\perp}
be the right inverse of
D
which we extend to
\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,N)
by declaring
Q|_{(\text{im }D)^{\perp}}\equiv 0
.

Let
A\in\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(N,N))
be such that
A\in T_{(j,D)}\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
(from the proof of Lemma
5.3
(i), this means that
\Pi A\sigma=0
). Then, there exists a smooth path
t\mapsto D_{t}\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
such that
\dot{D}_{0}=A
. Denoting
\sigma_{t}=\sigma_{(j,D_{t})}=\sigma+\xi_{t}
with
\xi_{t}\perp\sigma
and differentiating the identity
D_{t}\sigma_{t}\equiv 0
, we find

\displaystyle 0=A\sigma+D\dot{\xi}_{0}

(5.13)

which gives
\dot{\xi}_{0}=-QA\sigma
.

We claim that the surjectivity of (
5.12
), restricted to the subspace where variations of
j
and
z
are zero, will follow once we show the surjectivity of

\displaystyle L:\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(N,N))\to N_{z}\oplus{\text{coker }}D

(5.14)

\displaystyle L(A):=(-(QA\sigma)(z),\Pi A\sigma).

(5.15)

Indeed, if
L
is surjective, then so is its restriction
L^{-1}(N_{z}\oplus\{0\})\to N_{z}
. As recalled in the preceding paragraph,
L^{-1}(N_{z}\oplus\{0\})
is identified with the subspace of
T_{(j,D)}\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
where
j
is held constant. The claim now follows by observing that
L^{-1}(N_{z}\oplus\{0\})\to N_{z}
is the desired restriction of (
5.12
).

Now, if we let
D_{z}:\Omega^{0}_{z}(\Sigma,N)\to\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,N)
be the restriction of
D
to the space of sections of
N
vanishing at
z
and
\Pi^{\prime}:\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,N)\to{\text{coker }}D_{z}
be the corresponding projection, we can construct a map

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}D_{z}\to N_{z}\oplus{\text{coker }}D

(5.16)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}\eta\mapsto(-(Q\eta)(z),\Pi\eta).

(5.17)

It follows from the long exact sequence relating the (co)kernels of
D_{z}
and
D
with
N_{z}
, that the map (
5.16
) is an isomorphism. Indeed, since we have
\ker D_{z}=\ker D
(both are spanned by
\sigma
), the long exact sequence mentioned above yields the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to N_{z}\to{\text{coker }}D_{z}\to{\text{coker }}D\to 0

(5.18)

and the map
\Pi^{\prime}\eta\mapsto(Q\eta)(z)
splits the connecting map
N_{z}\to{\text{coker }}D_{z}
in (
5.18
).
Under the isomorphism (
5.16
), the map
L
is simply
A\mapsto\Pi^{\prime}A\sigma
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
L
is not surjective. Then, there is a nonzero distributional section
\tau
of
N^{\dagger}
such that
\langle A\sigma,\tau\rangle=0
for all smooth
A
(i.e.,
\sigma\otimes\tau\equiv 0
as a distributional section of
N\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}N^{\dagger}
) and
\tau|_{\text{im }D_{z}}\equiv 0
. The second condition implies that
D^{\dagger}\tau
, viewed as a functional on
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,N)
, vanishes on the subspace
\Omega^{0}_{z}(\Sigma,N)
. As a result, there is a linear functional
\Lambda_{\tau}:N_{z}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any
\sigma\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,N)
, we have
\langle D^{\dagger}\tau,\sigma\rangle=\Lambda_{\tau}(\sigma(z))
. Thus,
D^{\dagger}\tau
is of class
(L_{1}^{p})^{*}
for any
2<p<\infty
(where
L_{1}^{p}
is the space of
L^{p}
functions with one derivative in
L^{p}
and we are using the Sobolev embedding
L_{1}^{p}\subset C^{0}
). By elliptic regularity,
\tau
must be of class
(L^{p})^{*}=L^{q}
(where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
) and smooth in the complement of
z
(since it satisfies
D^{\dagger}\tau=0
here). But now
\sigma\otimes\tau\equiv 0
with
\sigma\not\equiv 0
and
\tau\not\equiv 0
on the complement of
z
contradicts the fact that
\sigma
and
\tau
satisfy unique continuation on the complement of
z
.
∎

Now, let
h\geq g
be an integer such that
r:=(2h-2)-2(2g-2)\geq 0
. Then, for any
j\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, the complex orbifold
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,j,2)
of genus
h
holomorphic double covers of
(\Sigma,j)
branched at
r
points is non-empty. In fact, the underlying coarse moduli spaces
11
11

11

In this context, the coarse moduli space is just obtained by forgetting the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
stabilizers in the purely ineffective orbifold
\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,j,2)
. In particular, the coarse moduli space has no quotient singularities.
are canonically identified for all
j
(since the isomorphism class of a cover is determined by purely topological data once
j
is specified). Call this common coarse moduli space
M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
; it is naturally a smooth manifold of dimension
2r
.

Now, given
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
, the operator
D_{\varphi}=\varphi^{*}D
on
\varphi^{*}N
admits a splitting
D_{\varphi}=D_{\varphi}^{+}\oplus D_{\varphi}^{-}
into a direct sum of Cauchy–Riemann operators on isotypic subspaces corresponding to the trivial and sign representations of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\text{Aut}(\varphi)
. We have
{\text{ind }}D_{\varphi}^{+}={\text{ind }}D=0
and
{\text{ind }}D_{\varphi}^{-}={\text{ind }}D_{\varphi}=-2r
.

Definition 5.5

(Equivariant Brill–Noether loci)
.

Given
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
, an integer
k\geq 1
and integers
h
and
r
as above, define the subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{K}}(N)\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)

(5.19)

to consist of those
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])
for which
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}=k
. Also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,h,\mathbb{K}}(N)=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{K}}(N).

(5.20)

Lemma 5.6
.

The following codimension estimates on
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{K}}(N)
hold.

(i)

\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}
is a submanifold of
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
of codimension
2r+1
.

(ii)

For each
k\geq 2
,
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}
has codimension
\geq 4r+4
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
.

(iii)

For each
k\geq 1
,
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{C}}
has codimension
\geq 2r+2
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
.

Proof.

The proof is a slight variation of the argument from Lemma
5.3
. As in that argument, given any point
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])\in\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{K}}
, we can find a smooth function

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{K}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)\supset\mathcal{U}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(\ker D_{\varphi}^{-},{\text{coker }}D_{\varphi}^{-})

(5.21)

defined on a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])
such that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{K}}=\mathcal{S}^{-1}(0)
. Moreover, the linearization
d\mathcal{S}|_{(j,D,[\varphi])}
restricted to
\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}(N,N))
, i.e. the space of variations of
D
with
j
and
[\varphi]
held fixed, is given by

\displaystyle A\mapsto(\kappa\mapsto(\varphi^{*}A)\kappa\pmod{\text{im }D_{\varphi}^{-}})

(5.22)

where
\kappa\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
. Thus, exactly as in Lemma
5.3
, the proofs of (i)–(iii) will follow from suitable rank estimates on this map. By duality, it is enough to prove rank estimates for the “Petri map”

\displaystyle\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\ker((D_{\varphi})^{\dagger})^{-}

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}(\varphi^{*}N)^{\dagger})^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\sigma\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\zeta\mapsto\sigma(\zeta)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\sigma^{\prime}(\zeta))

(5.24)

where
(\cdot)^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\text{Aut}(\varphi)
-invariant part.
The key observation is that
\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\ker((D_{\varphi})^{\dagger})^{-}
already consists of
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
-invariant elements – indeed,
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
acts by the sign representation on both
\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
and
\ker((D_{\varphi})^{\dagger})^{-}
– and so, the map (
5.23
) is well-defined. Thus, we are reduced again to the argument of Lemma
5.3
(i)–(iii). More precisely, we can show by unique continuation that the Petri map has
\mathbb{R}
-rank
=2r+1
in case of (i) and
\mathbb{C}
-rank
\geq r+1
in case of (ii). For statement (iii), we invoke
[
9
, Remark 1.1.14]
(or
[
9
, Remark 1.3.12]
) to conclude that the rank of the Petri map is
\geq 2(2+2r)
in case of (ii).
∎

Remark 5.7
.

The injectivity of Petri maps in the proofs of both Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.6
follows without appealing to Wendl’s work on generic injectivity of Petri maps
[
27
]
because the degrees of the corresponding covers are small enough.

Definition 5.8

(Co-orientation on
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
)
.

Given a tuple
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])
lying in
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
, its normal space in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
is canonically identified with

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(\ker D_{\varphi}^{-},{\text{coker }}D_{\varphi}^{-}).

(5.25)

We define an orientation on (
5.25
) in the usual fashion, i.e., we deform
D
in a
1
-parameter family to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear operator and use the spectral flow. This assignment of orientations to the normal spaces of the forgetful map
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
, at points where this map is an immersion, is called the natural co-orientation on
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
. Note that here we are using the natural orientation on
M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
coming from the orientation on
\Sigma
.

In order to make the next definition, we will need the following well-known fact which is implicit in
[
27
, Appendix B]
and
[
8
, Lemma 3.6]
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Given a complex curve
C
(not necessarily compact) and a complex vector bundle
\pi:N\to C
on it, there is a natural bijection
D\mapsto J_{D}
between

(i)

\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operators
D:\Omega^{0}(C,N)\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,N)
and,

(ii)

almost complex structures
J
on the total space of
N
which

(a)

restrict to the standard complex structure on each fiber of
\pi
,

(b)

restrict to the given complex structure on
C
along the zero section,

(c)

are invariant under the
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
action
\lambda\cdot(z,v)=(z,\lambda v)
on
N
and,

(d)

such that the projection
\pi
is
J
-holomorphic.

Under this bijection, if
C^{\prime}
is another complex curve (not necessarily compact), then a smooth map
\hat{s}:C^{\prime}\to N
is
J_{D}
-holomorphic if and only if
\varphi:=\pi\circ\hat{s}:C^{\prime}\to C
is holomorphic and the corresponding section
s\in\Omega^{0}(C^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)
given by

\displaystyle z\in C^{\prime}\mapsto\hat{s}(z)\in N_{\varphi(z)}

(5.26)

satisfies
(\varphi^{*}D)s=0
.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case of the trivial bundle
U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}\to U
where
U\subset\mathbb{C}
is an open subset with complex coordinate
z
and
\mathbb{C}^{r}
is endowed with the complex coordinates
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r})
. Let
J
be any almost complex structure on
U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}
which satisfies conditions (a)–(d). Using (a), (b) and (d), we find that the bundle
\Omega^{1,0}_{J}
near
U\times 0\subset U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}
has a local frame consisting of
dz
and

\displaystyle dw_{i}+A_{i}(z,w)d\bar{z}

(5.27)

for
1\leq i\leq r
where the
A_{i}
are smooth functions vanishing when
w_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
. Now, (c) implies that the
A_{i}
are actually
\mathbb{R}
-linear in
w
, i.e., they are of the form

\displaystyle A_{i}(z,w)=\hat{A}_{i}(z)[w]

(5.28)

where
\hat{A}=(\hat{A}_{i})_{i=1}^{r}:U\to\text{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{C}^{r})
is a smooth function. Moreover, the expression (
5.27
) for a frame of
\Omega^{1,0}_{J}
is valid on the whole of
U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}
. To this almost complex structure, we can associate the
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator
D=\bar{\partial}+\hat{A}[\cdot]
on the bundle
U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}\to U
. Conversely, any
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator
D
on
U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}
can be written as
\bar{\partial}+\hat{A}[\cdot]
for some smooth function
\hat{A}:U\to\text{End}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{C}^{r})
and thus, defines an almost complex structure
J_{D}
on
N
, satisfying (a)–(d), by taking the
(1,0)
forms to be spanned by
dz
along with
dw_{i}+\hat{A}_{i}(z)[w]d\bar{z}
for
1\leq i\leq r
.

It is easy to see that this gives a bijective correspondence
D\mapsto J_{D}
and that the bijection is preserved under the natural action of the group
C^{\infty}(U,GL_{r}(\mathbb{C}))
on both sides. Finally, for any open subset
U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}
and smooth map
\hat{s}:U^{\prime}\to U\times\mathbb{C}^{r}
given by
\zeta\mapsto(\varphi(\zeta),s(\zeta))
is
J_{D}
-holomorphic if and only if we have

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\varphi=0,\quad\bar{\partial}s+\hat{A}(\varphi)[s]\bar{\partial}\bar{\varphi}=0

(5.29)

as desired. Now, the lemma extends to the general case by naturality.
∎

Let us now observe that for any
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
, all non-zero elements of
\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
are non-zero scalar multiples of each other. Thus, for any
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
, the corresponding
J_{D}
-holomorphic map
\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N
is immersed/injective if and only if the same property holds for
a\cdot\sigma
where
0\neq a\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, we can make sense of the locus within
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}
where this immersion/injectivity property fails. The discussion which follows constructs Baire sets which avoid this locus (i.e. the immersion and injectivity property hold). These Baire sets will be used in the definition of linear wall crossing invariant (Definition
5.17
).

Definition 5.10
.

Define the subsets

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}},

(5.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}

(5.31)

of
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
to consist of exactly those
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])
for which the
J_{D}
-holomorphic map
\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N
corresponding to a nonzero
\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
fails to be immersed or injective respectively. Also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{emb}}=\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}\cup\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}.

(5.32)

Lemma 5.11
.

We have the following codimension estimates.

(i)

\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}
has codimension
\geq 2r+5
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
.

(ii)

\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}
has codimension
\geq 2r+3
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
.

In particular,
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{emb}}
has codimension
\geq 2r+3
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
.

Proof.

The proof is just a combination of the ideas from Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
. More precisely, the proof of (ii) exactly mirrors the proof of Lemma
5.4
while (i) requires a slightly more involved argument.

(i)

Consider the finite cover
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{imm}}\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)
which consists of tuples
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])
along with the choice of a point
\zeta\in\text{Crit }\varphi
. Within
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{imm}}
, we can consider the subset
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}
consisting of
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma],\zeta)
such that
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
and
d\hat{\sigma}|_{\zeta}=0
for all
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
(where
\hat{\sigma}
is the
J_{D}
-holomorphic map corresponding to
\sigma
via Lemma
5.9
). Since
\hat{\sigma}
is
J_{D}
-holomorphic, its derivative at
\zeta
is
\mathbb{C}
-linear. Equivalently, since
\sigma\in\ker D^{-}_{\varphi}
, the derivative
(\varphi^{*}\nabla)\sigma|_{\zeta}
(which is well-defined independent of the choice of
\nabla
on
N
since
\zeta\in\text{Crit }\varphi
) is
\mathbb{C}
-linear. In other words, we see that
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{imm}}
is locally given by the zeros of the map

\displaystyle(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma],\zeta)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\varphi^{*}\nabla)^{1,0}\sigma_{(j,D)}|_{\zeta}

(5.33)

where
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])\mapsto\sigma_{(j,D)}\neq 0
is a (locally defined) distinguished choice of element in the
1
-dimensional vector space
\ker D^{-}_{\varphi}
(specified by an
L^{2}
normalization condition as in the definition the analogous map (
5.11
) in the proof of Lemma
5.4
).

To prove the desired codimension estimate for
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}
, it is sufficient to show that
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{imm}}
is a submanifold of codimension
2r+5
. To this end, fix a point
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma],\zeta)\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{imm}}
. Let
\sigma
be a nonzero element of the
1
-dimensional vector space
\ker D^{-}_{\varphi}
. Denote by
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}_{\zeta}
the subspace of
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}
consisting of sections whose derivative at
\zeta
is
\mathbb{C}
-anti-linear. Note that since
\zeta\in\text{Crit }\varphi
, the derivative of elements in
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}
is well-defined and independent of choice of connection on
N
(since the difference of two connections on
N
is a tensor on
\Sigma
whose pullback along
\varphi
vanishes at
\zeta
). Write
(D_{\varphi}^{-})_{\zeta}:\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}_{\zeta}\to\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}
for the restriction of
D_{\varphi}^{-}
to
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}_{\zeta}
and let
\Pi^{\prime}
be the projection from
\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}
to
{\text{coker }}(D_{\varphi}^{-})_{\zeta}
. Arguing as the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we are reduced to showing that

\displaystyle L:\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(N,N))\to{\text{coker }}(D_{\varphi}^{-})_{\zeta}

(5.34)

\displaystyle L(A):=\Pi^{\prime}((\varphi^{*}A)\sigma)

(5.35)

is surjective. We remark that the map (
5.33
) naturally leads us to consider the space
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}_{\zeta}
, analogous to how the space
\Omega^{0}_{z}(\Sigma,N)
naturally arises from the map (
5.11
) in the proof of Lemma
5.4
. Thus, we see that
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}_{\zeta}
plays a similar role in this proof as
\Omega^{0}_{z}(\Sigma,N)
did in the proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
L
is not surjective. Then, arguing as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we deduce the existence of a nonzero distributional section
\tau
of
(\varphi^{*}N)^{\dagger}
which

(a)

is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\text{Aut}(\varphi)
-anti-invariant,

(b)

vanishes on the image of
(D_{\varphi}^{-})_{\zeta}
and,

(c)

is such that
\sigma\otimes\tau\equiv 0
as a distributional section of
\varphi^{*}N\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi^{*}N)^{\dagger}
(for this last condition, we are using the observation that
\sigma\otimes\tau
is invariant under
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
– see the parallel argument in the proof of Lemma
5.6
).

Now, note that (a) and (b) imply that
(D_{\varphi})^{\dagger}\tau
, viewed as a functional on
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)
, vanishes on the subspace
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{+}\oplus\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)^{-}_{\zeta}
and therefore factors through the projection

\displaystyle\Omega^{0}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)

\displaystyle\to\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(T_{\zeta}\Sigma^{\prime},N_{\varphi(\zeta)})

(5.36)

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\mapsto(\varphi^{*}\nabla)^{1,0}s|_{\zeta}.

(5.37)

Thus,
(D_{\varphi})^{\dagger}\tau
is of class
(L_{2}^{p})^{*}
for any
2<p<\infty
(where
L_{2}^{p}
is the space of
L^{p}
functions with two derivatives in
L^{p}
and we are using the Sobolev embedding
L_{2}^{p}\subset C^{1}
). By elliptic regularity it now follows that
\tau
is of class
(L_{1}^{p})^{*}
and smooth in the complement of
\zeta
(since it satisfies
(D_{\varphi})^{\dagger}\tau=0
here). Now, if the support of
\tau
is not just the point
\zeta
, then by unique continuation for
\sigma
and
\tau
(on the complement of
\zeta
), we must have
\sigma\otimes\tau\not\equiv 0
. Thus, we must have
\text{supp }\tau=\{\zeta\}
. It follows that there is an integer
m\geq 0
such that
\tau
applied to any element of
\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)
depends only on the
m
-jet of this element at
\zeta
. Since
\tau
is known to be of class
(L_{1}^{p})^{*}
and
L_{1}^{p}\not\subset C^{1}
, it follows that
m=0
and as a result
\tau
acts on the space
\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi^{*}N)
via evaluation at
\zeta
followed by a linear functional

\displaystyle M:(\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{\Sigma^{\prime},j}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\varphi^{*}N)_{\zeta}\to\mathbb{R}.

(5.38)

But now, it is easy to produce a smooth section
s
of
\varphi^{*}N
such that

\displaystyle-\langle(D_{\varphi})^{\dagger}\tau,s\rangle=\langle\tau,D_{\varphi}s\rangle=M(D_{\varphi}s|_{\zeta})=M((\varphi^{*}\nabla)^{0,1}s|_{\zeta})\neq 0

(5.39)

and
(\varphi^{*}\nabla)^{1,0}s|_{\zeta}=0
. This contradicts the fact that
(D_{\varphi})^{\dagger}\tau
factors through the map (
5.36
).

(ii)

The proof is similar to (i) but easier and so, we only indicate the setup. Define
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{inj}}
to be the subset of
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)\times M_{h}(\Sigma,2)\times\Sigma
consisting of tuples
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma],z)
such that
z\not\in\varphi(\text{Crit }\varphi)
. We can then define the subset
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{inj}}
to consist of those elements
(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma],z)
for which
(j,D,[\varphi])\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
and
\sigma|_{\varphi^{-1}(z)}\equiv 0
for all
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
. It can now be shown as in (i) that
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\text{inj}}
is a submanifold of codimension
2r+5
. Noting that
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}
is the image of
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{inj}}
under the natural projection gives the desired result.

The statement concerning
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{emb}}
follows from (i) and (ii).
∎

Remark 5.12
.

The codimension estimates from Lemma
5.11
assert that the holomorphic curve associated to the kernel of a generic element in
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
is embedded. The injectivity of the relevant Petri map is not hard to establish because our discussion just involves
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
-equivariant Cauchy–Riemann operators from
2
-fold covers. In fact, it is possible to extend the statements in Lemma
5.11
to non-trivial elementary walls as in Definition
4.1
. The fact that
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{k}^{H}=1
, which follows from the definition of non-trivial elementary wall type, is necessary in order to define the holomorphic sections uniquely up to rescaling. However, one needs to adapt the proof of genetric injectivity of Petri maps from
[
27
]
to this context.

Remark 5.13
.

Any element
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
defines a
multi-valued section
of the bundle
N\to\Sigma
. The conclusion of Lemma
5.11
can therefore be phrased as the “generic embeddedness” of multi-valued sections. As pointed out in Remark
4.13
, it may be possible to use similar arguments to establish similar generic behavior for
2
-valued harmonic forms relevant to special Lagrangian geometry.

The next definition introduces the notion of “
2
-rigid” operators, paths between them and fixed endpoint homotopies between such paths. Such operators arise naturally as normal deformation operators of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves with respect to any super-rigid almost complex structure.

Definition 5.14

(2-rigidity)
.

(a)

We say that
\alpha=(j,D)\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
is
2
-pre-rigid
if it misses the images of
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(N),\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
and
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{emb}}
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
for all
h\geq g
and
k\geq 2
. We say that
\alpha
is
2
-rigid if, in addition, it also misses the image of the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)

(5.40)

for all
h\geq g
. Denote the set of
2
-rigid elements by
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)_{2}
and its intersection with
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)\setminus\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)
by
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)_{2}
.

(b)

Given
\alpha_{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)_{2}
, define
\mathcal{P}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})
to be the space of smooth paths in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
from
\alpha_{-}
to
\alpha_{+}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})\subset\mathcal{P}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})

(5.41)

to be the set of paths which consist entirely of
2
-pre-rigid elements and are transverse to (
5.40
).

(c)

Given
\gamma_{\pm}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})
, define
\mathcal{P}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})
to be the space of smooth paths in
\mathcal{P}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})
from
\gamma_{-}
to
\gamma_{+}
, i.e., smooth homotopies from
\gamma_{-}
to
\gamma_{+}
with fixed endpoints. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})\subset\mathcal{P}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})

(5.42)

to be the set of homotopies which consist entirely of
2
-pre-rigid elements and are transverse to (
5.40
).

For the convenience of the reader, we now summarize the consequences of Lemmas
5.4
,
5.6
and
5.11
which are relevant for the next lemma:
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(N)
has codimension
\geq 3
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
, the image of
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
has codimension
\geq 4
for
k\geq 2
and the image of
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{emb}}
has codimension
\geq 3
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
. Moreover, both
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)
and the image of
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{2,h,\mathbb{C}}(N)
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)
have codimension
\geq 2
for all
k\geq 1
.

Lemma 5.15
.

We have the following genericity statements.

(i)

\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)_{2}\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)
is a Baire subset.

(ii)

\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)_{2}\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)
is a path connected Baire subset.

(iii)

For
\alpha_{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)_{2}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})\subset\mathcal{P}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})
is a Baire subset.

(iv)

For
\gamma_{\pm}\in P^{\pitchfork}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})
,
\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})\subset\mathcal{P}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})
is a Baire subset.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the Sard–Smale theorem combined with the codimension formulas/estimates from Lemmas
5.4
,
5.6
and
5.11
.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Fix
\alpha_{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)_{2}
,
\gamma_{\pm}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha_{-},\alpha_{+})
and
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})
. Consider
\tilde{\gamma}
as smooth map

\displaystyle[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)

(5.43)

such that
\tilde{\gamma}(\cdot,\pm 1)\equiv\gamma_{\pm}(\cdot)
and
\tilde{\gamma}(\pm 1,\cdot)\equiv\alpha_{\pm}
. Then, for any fixed
h\geq g
, the fiber product of (
5.43
) and (
5.40
) is a naturally oriented smooth
1
-manifold with boundary which is proper over
[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
and has boundary exactly over
[-1,1]\times\pm 1
.

Proof.

The fact that the fiber product in question is a manifold with boundary (with boundary only over
[-1,1]\times\pm 1
) follows from the transversality assumption in the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})
. The orientation comes from the standard orientation on
[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
and the natural co-orientation from Definition
5.8
. Thus, what remains to be proved is properness over
[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
and this will be a consequence of the fact that all elements in the image of
\tilde{\gamma}
are
2
-pre-rigid. To establish properness, let
p_{n}\to p
be a convergent sequence in
[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
and let
\tilde{\alpha}_{n}=(j_{n},D_{n},[\varphi_{n}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to\Sigma])\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
be a sequence such that we have
\tilde{\gamma}(p_{n})=\alpha_{n}:=(j_{n},D_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
, with limit
\tilde{\gamma}(p)=\alpha:=(j,D)
. We will show that
\tilde{\alpha}_{n}
has a subsequence converging to an element
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
.
In other words, we will use the assumption
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+})
to argue that there is a limiting
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
-anti-invariant multi-valued section associated to
\alpha=(j,D)
which is defined over a branched cover of
\Sigma
with smooth domain.

Fix a Hermitian inner product on
N
and let
\omega_{0}
be a symplectic form on the unit ball
B_{1}(N)
taming the almost complex structure
J_{D}
(this exists by
[
8
, Lemma 3.6]
). Since
\alpha_{n}\to\alpha
, it follows that
\omega_{0}
tames
J_{D_{n}}
also for
n\gg 1
.

Let
\hat{\sigma}_{n}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to N
be the simple
J_{D_{n}}
-holomorphic map representing the class
2[\Sigma]
corresponding to a section
\sigma_{n}\in\ker(D_{n})_{\varphi_{n}}^{-}
with
\|\sigma_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\frac{1}{2}
. By Gromov compactness, we can pass to a subsequence to assume that we have a limiting
J_{D}
-holomorphic stable map
\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N
representing class
2[\Sigma]
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
being of arithmetic genus
h
. Note that the image of each
\hat{\sigma}_{n}
(and therefore of
\hat{\sigma}
) is invariant under the automorphism
\mathcal{I}:(z,v)\mapsto(z,-v)
of
N
. Define
\varphi=\pi\circ\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
and note that the corresponding section
\sigma\in\ker\varphi^{*}D
satisfies
\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}=\frac{1}{2}
. Each effective component of
\Sigma^{\prime}
(i.e. an irreducible component on which
\hat{\sigma}
is non-constant) must map with positive degree onto
\Sigma
via
\varphi
. Thus, there are at most two effective components in
\Sigma^{\prime}
. We now consider two cases.

(a)

(There are two effective components
C_{1},C_{2}\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
.) Since
\varphi|_{C_{i}}:C_{i}\to\Sigma
are both of degree
1
, they must be isomorphisms. Thus,
\hat{s}_{1}:=\hat{\sigma}|_{C_{1}}
and
\hat{s}_{2}:=\hat{\sigma}|_{C_{2}}
give us two sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in\ker D
at least one of which must be nonzero. If
\hat{s}_{1}\not\equiv 0
, then since the image of
\hat{\sigma}
is connected invariant under
\mathcal{I}
, it follows that
s_{1}
must vanish at a point
z\in C_{1}
. This contradicts the
2
-pre-rigidity of
\alpha=(j,D)
.

(b)

(There is a unique effective component
C\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
.) Let
\psi:\tilde{C}\to C
be the normalization. Since the image of
\hat{\sigma}\circ\varphi:\tilde{C}\to N
is invariant under
\mathcal{I}
, it follows that
0\neq\psi^{*}\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi\circ\psi}^{-}
. Since
\alpha=(j,D)
is
2
-pre-rigid, it follows that
\dim\ker D^{-}_{\varphi\circ\psi}=1
and the
J_{D}
-holomorphic map
\hat{\sigma}\circ\psi
is an embedding because
\alpha
necessarily lies in the complement of
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)_{\text{emb}}
. This implies that
\psi
is an isomorphism,
C
is smooth and
\hat{\sigma}|_{C}
is an embedding representing class
2[\Sigma]
. Since
\hat{\sigma}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N
is the Gromov limit of the simple (in fact, embedded by
2
-pre-rigidity)
J_{D_{n}}
-curves
\hat{\sigma}_{n}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to N
, Remark
3.2
(b) shows that
\Sigma^{\prime}
cannot have any ghost components. Thus,
\Sigma^{\prime}
is smooth and coincides with
C
. Moreover,
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}
and
\dim\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}=1
.

We have now shown that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is necessarily smooth. We conclude that the limit
\tilde{\alpha}=(j,D,[\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma])
lies in
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,h,\mathbb{R}}(N)
as desired.
∎

Definition 5.17

(Linear wall crossing invariant)
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N)_{2}
be
2
-rigid and let
h\geq g
be given. We then define the invariant

\displaystyle\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}

(5.44)

as follows. Set
r=(2h-2)-2(2g-2)
. If
r<0
, we define
\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha)=0
.

If
r\geq 0
, choose a point
\beta\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)_{2}
and choose a path
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha,\beta)
. Then, using Lemma
5.16
and the natural co-orientation from Definition
5.8
, the fiber product of
\gamma
with (
5.40
) is a compact oriented
0
-manifold and we define
\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha)
to be the signed count of points in this fiber product.

The following lemma shows that
\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha)
does not depend on the auxiliary data therefore it is well-defined.

Lemma 5.18
.

\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha)
in Definition
5.17
is independent of the choice of
\beta
and
\gamma
.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case
r\geq 0
. Let us temporarily denote the quantity from Definition
5.17
as
\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
to emphasize the dependence on the choices
\beta
and
\gamma
.

(i)

Fix the choice of
\beta
and consider
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha,\beta)
. Choose
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
and apply Lemma
5.16
(combined with the fact that an oriented
1
-manifold with boundary has
0
boundary points when counted with sign) to get

\displaystyle\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma^{\prime}).

(5.45)

Thus, we may write
\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta)
.

(ii)

Now, suppose
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)_{2}
are given. Choose a path
\delta
in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{C}}(N)_{2}
from
\beta
to
\beta^{\prime}
– such a path exists by Lemma
5.15
and it is, moreover, automatically in
\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
. Now, given any
