\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha,\beta)
, we can set
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(\alpha,\beta^{\prime})
to be the concatenation of
\gamma
and
\delta
. Since there are no intersections of
\delta
with (
5.40
), it follows that

\displaystyle\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta)=\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})=\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta^{\prime}).

(5.46)

Combining (i) and (ii), we conclude that
\text{w}_{2,h}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
is independent of the choices of
\beta
and
\gamma
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

The proof of Theorem
1.6
is an application of the arguments of §
5.1
combined with Theorems
3.1
and
4.12
.

The primitive class
A\in\overline{H}_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
remains fixed for the whole argument. As explained after Definition
1.1
, as long as
J
is away from the subset
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
of codimension
2
, every
J
-holomorphic stable map to
X
is either an embedded
J
-curve or a multiple cover
12
12

12

In a slight abuse of terminology, we will be referring to any
J
-holomorphic stable map which is not an embedded
J
-curve as a “multiple cover” (even if it maps with degree
1
onto its image but has a ghost component).
(with possibly nodal domain) of an embedded
J
-curve. For such
J
, we record the following observation which will be used throughout the proof below. Since
A
is primitive, any stable
J
-holomorphic map of class
dA
with
d\leq 2
either represents an embedded
J
-curve of class
dA
or a (possibly nodal) multiple cover
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
(with
d^{\prime}=\deg(\varphi)\leq d
) of an embedded
J
-curve
C
such that
d^{\prime}[C]=dA
and
\varphi
is not an isomorphism. We now describe the non-trivial multiple covers
\varphi
which can appear. When
d^{\prime}=1
(in this case,
d
is either
1
or
2
), the domain
C^{\prime}
must have exactly one effective, i.e., non-constant component (which necessarily maps isomorphically onto
C
under
\varphi
) and every other component is a ghost, i.e., non-effective component. Moreover,
C^{\prime}
has at least one ghost component since
\varphi
is not an isomorphism. When
d^{\prime}=2
(in this case,
d=2
),
C^{\prime}
has at most two effective components and
[C]=A
. Further, if
C^{\prime}
has only one effective component, then restricting
\varphi
to (the normalization of) this component yields a (possibly branched) double cover of
C
. If, instead,
C^{\prime}
has two effective components, then
\varphi
maps each of these isomorphically onto
C
.

Let
J_{\pm}\in\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
be super-rigid almost complex structures. We need to show that for all
h\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,J_{-})=\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,J_{+})

(5.47)

where
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,J_{\pm})
is the right side of (
1.5
) for
J=J_{\pm}
. We will accomplish this by studying the bifurcations that occur along a suitably chosen path
\gamma:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
. Before this, we need some preparatory facts analogous to Lemmas
5.4
,
5.11
,
5.15
and
5.16
.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
h\geq g\geq 0
be integers.

(i)

Define
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{A,g}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
to be the set of all
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J
on
X
for which there exists an embedded
J
-curve
C\subset X
of genus
g
in class
A
, a point
z\in C
and
0\neq\sigma\in\ker D^{N}_{C,J}
with
\sigma(z)=0
.

(ii)

Define
\mathcal{W}_{2,h}(\mathcal{J}_{A,g})\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
to be the set of all
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
J
on
X
for which there exists an embedded
J
-curve
C\subset X
of genus
g
in class
A
, a holomorphic branched double cover
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
with
C^{\prime}
being smooth of genus
h
and
0\neq\sigma\in\ker(D^{N}_{C,J})^{-}_{\varphi}
such that the corresponding
J_{D^{N}_{C,J}}
-holomorphic map
\hat{\sigma}:C^{\prime}\to N_{C}
defined using (
5.26
) is not an embedding.

Lemma 5.20

(Analogue of Lemmas
5.4
and
5.11
)
.

We have the following codimension estimates.

(i)

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{A,g}\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
has codimension
\geq 3
for all
g\geq 0
.

(ii)

\mathcal{W}_{2,h}(\mathcal{J}_{A,g})\subset\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
has codimension
\geq 3
for all
h\geq g\geq 0
.

Proof.

We first define the relevant universal moduli spaces and reduce the problem of estimating the codimension to proving a rank lower bound for the linearization of the defining equations of these universal moduli spaces. The proofs of the rank estimates from Lemmas
5.4
and
5.11
(which prove the analogous results for Cauchy–Riemann operators) apply verbatim once we combine them with
[
27
, Lemma 5.27]
which shows that deformations in the space of Cauchy–Riemann operators can be realized by deformations in the space of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures.
∎

Lemma 5.21

(Analogue of Lemma
5.15
)
.

The subset
\mathcal{B}^{*}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
of paths
\gamma:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)
with
\gamma(\pm 1)=J_{\pm}
which are disjoint from
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{A,g}
and
\mathcal{W}_{2,h}(\mathcal{J}_{A,g})
for all
h\geq g\geq 0
is a Baire subset.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of a Sard–Smale argument applied to the codimension estimates from Lemma
5.20
.
∎

For the statement of the next lemma, recall the Baire set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.8
.

Lemma 5.22

(Analogue of Lemma
5.16
)
.

Fix a path
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{*}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
and integers
0\leq g\leq h
.

(i)

Consider all tuples of the form
(t,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma)
where
t\in[-1,1]
,
\Sigma\subset X
is an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve of genus
g
in class
A
and
(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\Sigma,2)
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
being smooth. Among these, the tuples satisfying

\displaystyle\dim\ker D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}=0,\quad\dim\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}=1

(5.48)

form a finite set.

(ii)

Let
t_{n}\to t
in
[-1,1]
be a convergent sequence and suppose
\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\subset X
is a sequence of embedded
\gamma(t_{n})
-curves of genus
h
in class
2A
which converge to a
\gamma(t)
-holomorphic stable map
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma\subset X
in the Gromov topology with
\Sigma
being an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve of genus
g
in class
2A/d
for some
d\in\{1,2\}
and
(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\Sigma,d)
.

Then,
\Sigma^{\prime}
is necessarily smooth. If
d=1
, then
\varphi
is an isomorphism. If
d=2
, then the operator
D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
is an isomorphism and we have

\displaystyle\dim\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}=1.

(5.49)

Proof.

The key ingredients of the proof are Theorem
3.1
and Remark
3.2
(b).

(i)

Since
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, it follows that the set (
5.48
) is discrete. It will therefore suffice to prove compactness. To this end, suppose that
(t_{n},\Sigma_{n},\varphi_{n}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}\to\Sigma_{n})
is a sequence of points in this set with
t_{n}\to t
. As observed at the beginning of §
5.2
, the sequence
\Sigma_{n}
has (after passing to a subsequence) a Gromov limit
\Sigma
which is either an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve of the same genus
g
or an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve of lower genus with ghost components attached.
By Remark
3.2
(b), the latter situation is impossible. Now, by identifying the normal bundles
N_{\Sigma_{n}}\to\Sigma_{n}
for
n\gg 1
with the fixed smooth vector bundle
N_{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
, we can repeat the argument of Lemma
5.16
to conclude the proof (the condition
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{*}
is the analogue of the
2
-pre-rigidity condition used in that proof).

(ii)

First, suppose
\Sigma
itself is of class
2A
. Then, Remark
3.2
(b) shows that
d=1
,
\varphi
is an isomorphism and, in particular, that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is smooth. For the remainder suppose that
\Sigma
is of class
A
and
d=2
. Theorem
3.1
now yields a section
s\in\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
which is not pulled back from
\Sigma
. Let
\hat{s}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N_{\Sigma}
be the associated
J_{D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}}
-holomorphic map.

In accordance with the observation made at the beginning of §
5.2
, since
(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(\Sigma,2)
, it follows that
\Sigma^{\prime}
has at most two effective components (i.e. irreducible components of
\Sigma^{\prime}
on which
\varphi
is non-constant). We now split into two cases.

(a)

(There are two effective components
C_{1},C_{2}\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
.) Since
\varphi|_{C_{i}}
has degree
1
, it must be an isomorphism for
i=1,2
. Thus,
s|_{C_{i}}
yields a section
s_{i}\in\ker D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
for
i=1,2
. Since
s
is not pulled back from
\Sigma
, it follows that
s_{1}\not\equiv s_{2}
. Since
C_{1}
can be connected to
C_{2}
within
\Sigma^{\prime}
by a (possibly empty) chain of ghost components, it also follows that there must be a point
z\in\Sigma
such that
s_{1}(z)=s_{2}(z)
. The section
s_{1}-s_{2}\in\ker D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
now shows that
\gamma(t)\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{A,g}
. This contradicts
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

(b)

(There is a unique effective component
C\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
.) Let
\psi:\tilde{C}\to C
be the normalization and set
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi\circ\psi:\tilde{C}\to\Sigma
. Since
\gamma(t)\not\in\mathcal{W}_{2,h}(\mathcal{J}_{A,g^{\prime}})
for
g^{\prime}=\text{genus}(\tilde{C})
, we deduce that
\hat{s}\circ\psi:\tilde{C}\to N_{\Sigma}
is an embedding. This implies that
\psi
must be injective and therefore an isomorphism. Thus,
C
is smooth and
\hat{s}|_{C}
is an embedding. Since
\ker(D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)})_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{-}\neq 0
, it follows from
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
that this vector space must in fact be
1
-dimensional and that
D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
is an isomorphism. Using the inverse function theorem, we can now obtain a sequence
\Sigma_{n}\subset X
of embedded
\gamma(t_{n})
-curves of genus
g
in class
A
converging to
\Sigma
. Arguing as in
[
27
, Appendix B]
, we can now identify tubular neighborhoods of
\Sigma_{n}
for
n\gg 1
with the normal bundle of
\Sigma
equipped with the pullback of the almost complex structure
\gamma(t_{n})
. Rescaling the images of the embedded curves
\Sigma^{\prime}_{n}
in
N_{\Sigma}
to have distance
\frac{1}{2}
from the zero section gives a new sequence of embedded curves which are holomorphic with respect to the rescaled versions of
\gamma(t_{n})
. The rescalings of the
\gamma(t_{n})
converge to
J_{D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}}
and moreover, all these almost complex structures are tamed by a symplectic form for
n\gg 1
(
[
8
, Lemma 3.6]
). Therefore, after passing to a subsequence, we can extract a Gromov limit given by a
J_{D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}}
-holomorphic map
\hat{s}^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime}\to N_{\Sigma}
whose image doesn’t lie in the zero section. Since
C
is the only effective component of
\hat{s}^{\prime}
(since a holomorphic mapping into any normal fiber must be constant), it follows that
\hat{s}^{\prime}
a scalar multiple of
\hat{s}
. We have therefore exhibited
\hat{s}
as a limit of embedded pseudo-holomorphic curves of genus
h
and class
2[\Sigma]
. Recalling that
\hat{s}|_{C}
is an embedding and applying Remark
3.2
(b) shows that we must have
C=\Sigma^{\prime}
.

We have now succeeded in showing that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is smooth. The remaining facts concerning the operator
D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
have already been proved during the course of (b) above.

∎

Remark 5.23
.

Although the proof of Lemma
5.22
is formally parallel to the proof of Lemma
5.16
, it is crucial to note that in case (ii) we use Theorem
3.1
to construct the multi-section
s
over
\Sigma
because the rescaling argument from
[
27
, Appendix B]
or Remark
3.2
(a) cannot be used unless we already know that
D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
is an isomorphism.

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
. Choose a path
\gamma
from the Baire subset
\mathcal{B}^{*}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{P}(J_{-},J_{+})
. (Recall the definition of the Baire set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
from Lemma
4.10
.) Define the moduli space

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{h}^{*}(X,\gamma,2A)\to[-1,1]

(5.50)

consisting of pairs
(t,\Sigma^{\prime})
, where
t\in[-1,1]
and
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset X
is an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve of genus
h
in class
2A
. For each
0\leq g\leq h
, also consider the moduli spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{g}(X,\gamma,A)\to[-1,1]

(5.51)

consisting of pairs
(t,\Sigma)
, where
t\in[-1,1]
and
\Sigma\subset X
is an embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve curve of genus
g
in class
A
. Both
\mathcal{M}_{h}^{*}(X,\gamma,2A)
and
\mathcal{M}_{g}(X,\gamma,A)
are oriented
1
-manifolds with boundary over
t=\pm 1
since
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
A
is primitive and the path
\gamma
misses
\mathcal{W}_{\text{emb}}
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g}(X,\gamma,A)
is compact by Remark
3.2
(b). Since
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{*}
, Lemma
5.22
shows that the set of points in
(t,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma)
with
(t,\Sigma)\in\mathcal{M}_{g}(X,\gamma,A)
for some
g\leq h
,
(\Sigma^{\prime},\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{h}(\Sigma,2)
with
\Sigma^{\prime}
smooth and

\displaystyle\ker D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}=0,\quad\ker\varphi^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}=1

(5.52)

forms a finite set. Moreover,
\gamma
is transverse to the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{2}_{{\bf b}^{r},\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{*}(X,A);\theta,\theta^{\oplus 1+2r})\to\mathcal{J}(X,\omega)

(5.53)

at any such point
(t,\Sigma,\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma)
, where we have set
r=2h-2-2(2g-2)\geq 0
,
{\bf b}^{r}=((2),\ldots,(2))
and
\theta=
the sign representation of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

Enumerate the times
t
for which there exists
\varphi:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
satisfying (
5.52
) as

\displaystyle-1<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<1

(5.54)

and, for each
1\leq i\leq k
, enumerate the intersections with the maps (
5.53
) as

\displaystyle p_{i,j}=(t_{i},\Sigma_{i,j}\subset X,\varphi_{i,j}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{i,j}\to\Sigma_{i,j})

(5.55)

for
1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}
, where
\ell_{i}
is a positive integer depending on
i
. Theorem
4.12
now provides numbers
\epsilon_{i}>0
and neighborhoods
\mathcal{O}_{i,j}
of
\varphi_{i,j}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{i,j}\to\Sigma_{i,j}\subset X
and by shrinking these further we can assume that the products

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{i,j}=(t_{i}-\epsilon_{i},t_{i}+\epsilon_{i})\times\mathcal{O}_{i,j}

(5.56)

are all pairwise disjoint. We now make a series of claims.

(1)

The
\mathbb{Z}
-valued function

\displaystyle(t,\Sigma)\mapsto\text{w}_{2,h}(D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)})

(5.57)

defined on
\mathcal{M}_{g}(X,\gamma,A)
away from
t=t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
is locally constant.

Proof.

Indeed, fix any point
(t,\Sigma)
such that
t\neq t_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. Then,
D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)}
is
2
-rigid and the same is true for all nearby points
(t^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
. Thus, any nearby
(t^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
can be connected to
(t,\Sigma)
by a path of curves with
2
-rigid normal deformation operators and so,
\text{w}_{2,h}(D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t)})=\text{w}_{2,h}(D^{N}_{\Sigma^{\prime},\gamma(t^{\prime})})
.
∎

(2)

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{h}^{*}(X,\gamma,2A)\setminus\bigcup_{i,j}\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
is compact. Moreover, the restriction of (
5.50
) over the complement of
\{t_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
is proper.

Proof.

Both assertions follow directly from Lemma
5.22
.
∎

(3)

The expression (
1.5
) is well-defined with
J=\gamma(t)
provided
t\in[-1,1]\setminus\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\}
is a regular value of (
5.50
) and each of the maps (
5.51
). If
t_{i}<t^{\prime}\leq t^{\prime\prime}<t_{i+1}
are two such regular values (for some
1\leq i<k
), then we have the equality

\displaystyle\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,\gamma(t^{\prime}))=\text{Gr}_{2A,h}(X,\omega,\gamma(t^{\prime\prime})).

(5.58)

Proof.

Claim (2) shows that the first sum in the expression (
1.5
) is well-defined for such a
t
. The remaining sums in (
1.5
) are well-defined by Claim (1). For regular values
t^{\prime}<t^{\prime\prime}
in
(t_{i},t_{i+1})
, we can now use the cobordisms given by restricting (
5.50
) and (
5.51
) to
[t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}]
and the local constancy statement from Claim (1) to deduce (
5.58
).
∎

(4)

In the previous claim, if we instead have
t_{i}-\epsilon_{i}<t^{\prime}<t_{i}<t^{\prime\prime}<t_{i}+\epsilon_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq k
, then (
5.58
) still holds.

Proof.

By Claim (3), we can assume
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
are arbitrarily close to
t_{i}
. For small
|t-t_{i}|
and
\Sigma\in\{\Sigma_{i,j}:1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}\}
, define
\Sigma(t)
to be the unique embedded
\gamma(t)
-curve close to
\Sigma
(obtained by noting that
\ker D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{i})}=0
and applying the inverse function theorem). For such a
\Sigma
, define the set
I_{\Sigma}(i)=\{1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}:\Sigma=\Sigma_{i,j}\}
, and note that we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{\Sigma}(i)}\text{sgn}(\gamma,t_{i};p_{i,j})=\text{sgn}(D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{i})})\cdot(\text{w}_{2,h}(D^{N}_{\Sigma(t^{\prime\prime}),\gamma(t^{\prime\prime})})-\text{w}_{2,h}(D^{N}_{\Sigma(t^{\prime}),\gamma(t^{\prime})}))

(5.59)

where we check orientations by using the identification

\displaystyle\det(D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{i})})\otimes\det(D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{i})})_{\varphi_{i,j}}^{-}=\det(\varphi_{i,j}^{*}D^{N}_{\Sigma,\gamma(t_{i})})

(5.60)

for each
j\in I_{\Sigma}(i)
. The quantities
\text{sgn}(\gamma,t_{i};p_{i,j})
are as in Definition
4.9
. Now recall that the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
were constructed by applying Theorem
4.12
to each point
p_{i,j}\in\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
and note that the isotropy group
\Gamma
is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
in all cases. Restrict (
5.50
) to
[t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}]
and note that this gives a compact
1
-manifold with boundary over the complement of
\bigcup_{i,j}\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
by Claim (2). Thus, as in Claim (3), this portion contributes equally to both sides of (
5.58
).

For the remaining contribution (coming from
\bigcup_{i,j}\mathcal{U}_{i,j}
), we see from Theorem
4.12
that the first sum in the definition of
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}
changes by the negative of the left side of (
5.59
) as we go from
t^{\prime}
to
t^{\prime\prime}
, while the second sum in the definition of
\text{Gr}_{2A,h}
changes by the right side of (
5.59
) when we go from
t^{\prime}
to
t^{\prime\prime}
. These two changes cancel each other out and so, we again have (
5.58
) as desired.
∎

Combining claims (3) and (4) and noting that
\pm 1
are regular values of (
5.50
) as well as each of the maps (
5.51
) completes the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

6.
Application to the Gopakumar–Vafa invariants

In this section, we will show that the invariant from Theorem
1.6
coincides with the Gopakumar–Vafa invariant for genus
0
. The comparison is carried out in §
6.2
and is an application of the discussion of obstruction bundles in §
6.1
. The main result of §
6.1
(Theorem
6.7
) is stated in greater generality than is needed for the application in §
6.2
as it may be of independent interest.

6.1.
Obstruction bundles and their Euler classes

Let
C
be a smooth curve of genus
g
and let
h\geq g
and
d\geq 2
be integers such that the integer

\displaystyle r=(2h-2)-d(2g-2)

(6.1)

is non-negative.
The moduli stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
, consisting of stable holomorphic maps
f:C^{\prime}\to C
with the arithmetic genus of
C^{\prime}
being
h
and
f_{*}[C^{\prime}]=d[C]
, has complex virtual dimension given by
r
. Moreover, the non-empty open substack
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
, consisting of those
[f:C^{\prime}\to C]
for which the domain
C^{\prime}
is smooth, is a complex orbifold of complex dimension
r
.

Definition 6.1

(Virtual fundamental class)
.

Define the class

\displaystyle[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)]^{\text{vir}}\in\check{H}_{2r}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d),\mathbb{Q})

(6.2)

to be the virtual fundamental class of this moduli space as in
[
22
, Section 9]
. Note that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
is algebraic and so, we do not actually need to distinguish between its Čech and singular (co)homologies.

Now, let
N
be a rank
2
complex vector bundle on
C
with
\deg(N)=2g-2
. We are interested in
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operators on
N
because
N
naturally arises as the normal bundle of
J
-curves of genus
g
in a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
-fold. Then, for any
\mathbb{R}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator
D
on
N
, we have

\displaystyle\text{ind}(D)=2\deg(N)+(2-2g)\cdot\text{rank}(N)=0

(6.3)

by the Riemann–Roch theorem. For the Cauchy–Riemann operator
D
and a point
[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
, we can consider the vector spaces

\displaystyle\ker(f^{*}D)^{\prime}

\displaystyle:={\text{coker }}(\ker D\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\ker(f^{*}D))

(6.4)

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D)^{\prime}

\displaystyle:={\text{coker }}({\text{coker }}D\xrightarrow{f^{*}}{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D)).

(6.5)

Note that these spaces can also be defined as subspaces of
\ker(f^{*}D)
and
{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D)
, when
C^{\prime}
is smooth, using Remark
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
.

Definition 6.2

(Cokernel/obstruction bundle)
.

The operator
D
is said to be
(d,h)
-rigid
if and only if for all
[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
, we have

\displaystyle\ker(f^{*}D)^{\prime}=0.

(6.6)

In this case, the assignment

\displaystyle[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\mapsto(\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,D})_{f}:={\text{coker }}(f^{*}D)^{\prime}

(6.7)

defines a real orbi-bundle
13
13

13

For a proof that
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,D}
is an orbi-bundle, see Appendix
B
.

\mathcal{N}_{C,D}^{d,h}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
of rank
2r
, which is called the
cokernel/obstruction bundle
.

Remark 6.3
.

It is important to note that the condition of
(d,h)
-rigidity
does not
require
\ker(f^{*}D)=0
, and thus, it could be satisfied even if the operator
D
has a non-trivial kernel on
C
. This extra generality is important for the proof of Theorem
6.12
(comparison to Gopakumar–Vafa invariants) as it allows the obstruction bundle to remain well-defined even when a birth-death bifurcation occurs.

Next, define the canonically oriented
determinant line
of
(f^{*}D)^{\prime}
to be

\displaystyle\det(f^{*}D)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\det\ker(f^{*}D)^{\prime}\otimes\left(\det{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D)^{\prime}\right)^{*}

(6.8)

\displaystyle=\det(f^{*}D)\otimes\det(D)^{*}

(6.9)

where
\det(D)
and
\det(f^{*}D)
are the determinant lines of the Cauchy–Riemann operators
D
and
f^{*}D
, which are canonically oriented by considering the spectral flow of a homotopy between
D
and a
\mathbb{C}
-linear Cauchy–Riemann operator. If
D
is
(d,h)
–rigid, this gives rise to an orientation of the corresponding cokernel bundle. Accordingly, we have a well-defined
virtual Euler number
of the cokernel bundle

\displaystyle e_{d,h}(C,D)=\langle[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)]^{\text{vir}},e(\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,D})\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}.

(6.10)

More generally, suppose
P
is a topological space and
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in P}
is a family of real Cauchy–Riemann operators

D_{\lambda}:\Omega^{0}(C,N)\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,N)

(6.11)

parametrized by
P
. The family
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in P}
is called
continuous
if the coefficients of
\{D_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in P}
(and all their derivatives in the
C
direction) are continuous as functions on
P\times C
once we identify the space of real Cauchy–Riemann operators with
\Omega^{0,1}(C,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(N,N))
by choosing a reference operator. The notion of a
smooth
family
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in P}
is defined similarly when
P
is a smooth manifold. It is easy to see that this notion of continuity/smoothness is well-defined.
For a continuous family
\mathcal{D}
, let
\hat{\Delta}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P
be the closed substack over which (
6.6
) fails, with
\mathcal{D}
in place of
D
. Then, the assignment (
6.7
), with
\mathcal{D}
in place of
D
, gives a well-defined oriented rank
2r
(orbi-)vector bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}\to(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P)\setminus\hat{\Delta}

(6.12)

which has an Euler class
e(\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}})\in\check{H}^{2r}((\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P)\setminus\hat{\Delta},\mathbb{Q})
. For a proof that
\hat{\Delta}
is closed and
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
is an orbi-bundle, see Appendix
B
.

Remark 6.4
.

The determinant line defined in (
6.8
)–(
6.9
) deserves some more explanation. We have the relation

\det(f^{*}D)=\det(f^{*}D)^{\prime}\otimes\det(D),

(6.13)

which means that to compute the Gromov–Witten contribution of a super-rigid curve
C
with normal deformation operator
D
, one should multiply (
6.10
) by the sign (
1.3
) of
C
.

Definition 6.5

(Simple wall crossing)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
be a smooth family of real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
N
. We say that
\mathcal{D}
is a
simple wall crossing
of type
(d,h)
at
t=0
if
\ker D_{0}=0
and there is a finite set
\Delta\subset\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
such that

(i)

condition
(
6.6
)
holds on
(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1])\setminus(\Delta\times 0)
, and so, the vector bundle
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
is well-defined here;

(ii)

for each
[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\in\Delta
, the composition

\displaystyle T_{f}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\oplus\mathbb{R}_{t}

\displaystyle\to\text{Hom}(\ker(f^{*}D_{0}),{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D_{0}))

(6.14)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Hom}(\ker(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime},{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime})

is an isomorphism, where the first map is a linearized map as in Definition
2.10
while second isomorphism comes from the assumption that
\ker(D_{0})=0
, which also implies
{\text{coker }}(D_{0})=0
for index reasons.

Remark 6.6
.

Note that the set
\Delta
in Definition
6.5
is assumed to be a subset of the locus
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
parametrizing holomorphic maps with smooth domains. This is consistent with equation (
1.11
) in Theorem
1.11
. This assumption is sufficient for the application in Section
6.2
.

By counting dimensions and indices, it follows that we have

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=1

(6.15)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\text{coker }}(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=1+2r

(6.16)

over the finite set
\Delta
.
Both sides of (
6.14
) are canonically oriented by combining the discussion surrounding equation (
4.55
) with the orientation on
\det(f^{*}D)^{\prime}
. Thus, to each
[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\in\Delta
, we can assign a well-defined number

\displaystyle\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},[f:C^{\prime}\to C])\in\{+1,-1\}.

(6.17)

which is the sign of the isomorphism (
6.14
).

We want to compute the change in the virtual Euler number
e_{d,h}(C,D_{t})
as we go from
t<0
to
t>0
for a simple wall crossing
\mathcal{D}
in terms of isotropy groups of the points in
\Delta
and the signs
\{\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)\}_{p\in\Delta}
. Our main result is the following.

Theorem 6.7

(Change in Euler number)
.

In the situation of Definition
6.5
,

\displaystyle[-1,1]\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Q}

(6.18)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto e_{d,h}(C,D_{t})

(6.19)

is a locally constant function. Let
e_{d,h}(C,D_{\pm})
denote the value
e_{d,h}(C,D_{t})
for
\pm t>0
. Then, we have the identity

\displaystyle e_{d,h}(C,D_{+})-e_{d,h}(C,D_{-})=\sum_{p\in\Delta}\frac{2\cdot\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)}{|\text{Aut}(p)|}.

(6.20)

The local constancy assertion is obvious. The remaining assertion will be proved in the next two lemmas. We continue to use the notation from Definition
6.5
.

Lemma 6.8

(Local to global)
.

For each point
p\in\Delta
, choose an open neighborhood

\displaystyle V_{p}\Subset\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\times(-1,1)

(6.21)

whose closure
14
14

14

A\Subset B
denotes the assertion that
A\subset B
and the closure of
A
in
B
is compact.
has smooth orbifold boundary
\partial V_{p}
. Endowing
\partial V_{p}
with the boundary orientation from
V_{p}
and assuming that the closures of
\{V_{p}\}_{p\in\Delta}
are pairwise disjoint, we have the identity

\displaystyle e_{d,h}(C,D_{+})-e_{d,h}(C,D_{-})=\sum_{p\in\Delta}\langle[\partial V_{p}],e(\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}})\rangle

(6.22)

where
[\partial V_{p}]
denotes the standard orbifold fundamental class for each
p\in\Delta
.

Proof.

Introduce the notation
\mathcal{M}_{I}:=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1]
. It will be enough to show that the identity

\displaystyle[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)]^{\text{vir}}\times\{+1\}-[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)]^{\text{vir}}\times\{-1\}=\sum_{p\in\Delta}[\partial V_{p}]

(6.23)

holds in
H_{*}(\mathcal{M}_{I}\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\Delta}V_{p};\mathbb{Q})
. This will follow from
[
22
, Lemmas 5.2.4, 5.2.5 and 5.2.6]
applied to the space

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{I}\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\Delta}V_{p}

(6.24)

provided we show that it has an implicit atlas with boundary which, when restricted to the boundary, gives (a) the usual atlas (from section
[
22
, Section 9]
) on
[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)]^{\text{vir}}\times\{\pm 1\}
and (b) the standard orbifold atlas on each
V_{p}
. Constructing such an atlas is straightforward. Indeed, choose open neighborhoods

\displaystyle V_{p}\Subset W_{p}\Subset W^{\prime}_{p}\Subset\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\times(-1,1)

(6.25)

with
\{W^{\prime}_{p}\}_{p\in\Delta}
still being pairwise disjoint. Endow each
W^{\prime}_{p}\setminus V_{p}
with the natural orbifold-with-boundary atlas (with trivial obstruction spaces) as in
[
22
, Section 2.1.2]
and endow the open subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{I}\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\Delta}\overline{W}_{p}

(6.26)

with the restriction of the product atlas (see
[
22
, Definition 6.3.1]
) on
\mathcal{M}_{I}
. These
1+|\Delta|
open sets cover the moduli space in question. Over the open overlaps
W^{\prime}_{p}\setminus\overline{W}_{p}\subset\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\times(-1,1)
– note that there are no triple overlaps – both implicit atlases are equivalent (in the sense of
[
22
, Remark 2.1.5]
). This is because the standard orbifold atlas on
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
is equivalent to the restriction of the implicit atlas on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
. Thus, we can combine this cover into an implicit atlas on the space (
6.24
) with the desired properties.
∎

Lemma 6.9

(Local contribution)
.

In the situation of Lemma
6.8
, the neighborhoods
V_{p}
can be chosen such that their closures are pairwise disjoint and we have

\displaystyle\langle[\partial V_{p}],e(\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}})\rangle=\frac{2\cdot\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)}{|\text{Aut}(p)|}

(6.27)

for each
p\in\Delta
.

Proof.

Fix
p=[f:C^{\prime}\to C]\in\Delta\subset\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
is a smooth orbifold, we can identify a neighborhood
U_{p}
of
p
diffeomorphically with the orbifold
[\mathbb{V}/\Gamma]
, where
\Gamma=\text{Aut}(C,f)
is the isotropy group of
p
with its natural complex representation on
\mathbb{V}=T_{f}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
and the point
p
is identified with
[0]
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that this neighborhood meets no other points of
\Delta
.

Assume, for the sake of concreteness, that
\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)=+1
. This can be achieved by reparametrizing the interval
[-1,1]
in reverse if necessary. The local chart
[\mathbb{V}/\Gamma]
gives a map
\mathbb{V}\xrightarrow{\nu}\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
which, in turn, yields the following
\Gamma
-equivariant commutative diagram (with the square being a fiber square).
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
ev
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Explicitly,
\pi:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{V}
is obtained via pulling back the universal curve over
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
to
\mathbb{V}
along the local étale chart
\nu
and defines a
\Gamma
-equivariant smooth family of curves over
\mathbb{V}
. The map
\text{ev}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to C
is the pullback along
\nu
of the evaluation map to
C
defined on the universal curve over
\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
, and it is a
\Gamma
-invariant smooth map which is holomorphic on each fiber of
\pi
. The maps
\pi
and
ev
together with the
\Gamma
-action realize the open embedding
[\mathbb{V}/\Gamma]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)
. We can then define
V_{p}=[B_{\mathbb{V}}/\Gamma]
, where
B_{\mathbb{V}}
is the ball bounded by
S_{\mathbb{V}}
, and conclude by Lemma
6.10
and noting that
\langle[S_{\mathbb{V}}],e(TS_{\mathbb{V}})\rangle=\chi(S_{\mathbb{V}})=2
. It is useful to note that, for this last argument to work, we
do not
require the isomorphism (
6.28
) to be
\Gamma
-equivariant.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

Use the same notations as in Lemma
6.9
. For any sufficiently small sphere
S_{\mathbb{V}}\subset\mathbb{V}\times[-1,1]
centered at
0
(defined with respect to the orthogonal direct sum of a
\Gamma
-invariant Hermitian inner product on
\mathbb{V}
and the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}
), we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle(\tilde{\nu}^{*}\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}})|_{S_{\mathbb{V}}}\simeq TS_{\mathbb{V}}

(6.28)

of oriented vector bundles on
S_{\mathbb{V}}
, where
\tilde{\nu}:\mathbb{V}\times[-1,1]\to\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1]
is given by
\tilde{\nu}=\nu\times{\text{id}}
.

Proof.

Let us first make the following auxiliary choices.

(1)

A Hermitian metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
and a compatible
\mathbb{C}
-linear connection
\nabla
on
N
.

(2)

A Riemannian metric (and therefore, an area form) on
C^{\prime}
whose conformal class coincides with the one on
C^{\prime}
induced by
j
.

(3)

A smooth trivialization
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{V}
of the bundle
\pi
. Let
\tilde{f}:C^{\prime}\times\mathbb{V}\to C
denote the map induced by
ev
, and set
f_{v}:=\tilde{f}(\cdot,v):C^{\prime}\to C
for
v\in\mathbb{V}
. Also, let
\tilde{j}=(j_{v})_{v\in V}
denote the induced family of almost complex structures on
C^{\prime}
.

(4)

A smooth family of isomorphisms

\displaystyle I_{v}:(T_{C^{\prime}},j_{0})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(T_{C^{\prime}},j_{v}).

(6.29)

We now have the following smooth family of Cauchy–Riemann operators on
N
parametrized by
(v,t)\in\mathbb{V}\times[-1,1]

\displaystyle D_{v,t}:L^{2}_{1}(C^{\prime},f^{*}N)\to L^{2}(C^{\prime},\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}_{C^{\prime},j_{0}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}f^{*}N)

(6.30)

defined by pulling back the operator
f_{v}^{*}D_{t}
on
f_{v}^{*}N
to an operator on
f^{*}N
(recall:
f=f_{0}
) using the
\nabla
-parallel transport along the paths
\tau\in[0,1]\mapsto f_{\tau v}(z)\in C
for each
z\in C^{\prime}
and the isomorphism
I_{v}^{-1}
. Obviously, we have
D_{0,0}=f^{*}D_{0}
on smooth sections. Since
\ker D_{0}={\text{coker }}D_{0}=0
(and this will continue to hold for small
t
), we see from (
6.5
) that
{\text{coker }}D_{v,t}={\text{coker }}D_{v,t}/{\text{coker }}D_{t}={\text{coker }}(f_{v}^{*}D_{t})^{\prime}
for small
t
. Moreover, for small
(v,t)\neq(0,0)
, we have
\ker D_{v,t}=0
(even though
\ker D_{0,0}
is
1
-dimensional) and so,
{\text{coker }}D_{v,t}
forms a vector bundle for small
(v,t)\neq(0,0)
.
Therefore, we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}D_{v,t}\simeq(\tilde{\nu}^{*}\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}})_{v,t}

(6.31)

for small
(v,t)\neq(0,0)
. Orient
A=\ker(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime}
and
B={\text{coker }}(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime}
so that the induced orientation on
\det(f^{*}D_{0})^{\prime}
is the canonical one from Definition
6.2
. Define the bounded linear map

\displaystyle R:\text{im}\,D_{0,0}\to(\ker D_{0,0})^{\perp}

(6.32)

to be the inverse of
D_{0,0}|_{(\ker D_{0,0})^{\perp}}
, with
(\ker D_{0,0})^{\perp}\subset L^{2}_{1}
denoting the
L^{2}
orthogonal complement of
\ker D_{0,0}
. Let
\Pi_{\text{im}}:L^{2}\to\text{im}\,D_{0,0}
be the orthogonal projection and let
\Pi_{\text{coker }}
be the quotient projection onto the cokernel. For small
(v,t)
, the quantity
\|1-\Pi_{\text{im}}D_{v,t}R\|
is small on
\text{im}\,D_{0,0}
and so, we can define the map

\displaystyle R_{v,t}=R(\Pi_{\text{im}}D_{v,t}R)^{-1}:\text{im}\,D_{0,0}\to(\ker D_{0,0})^{\perp}

(6.33)

which is the inverse of
\Pi_{\text{im}}D_{v,t}|_{(\ker D_{0,0})^{\perp}}
. It is immediate that
\ker(\Pi_{\text{im}}D_{v,t})
is given by the graph of the map
-R_{v,t}\Pi_{\text{im}}D_{v,t}:\ker D_{0,0}\to(\ker D_{0,0})^{\perp}
. Define the smooth family of linear maps

\displaystyle L_{v,t}

\displaystyle:\ker D_{0,0}\to{\text{coker }}D_{0,0}

(6.34)

\displaystyle L_{v,t}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\text{coker }}D_{v,t}(1-R_{v,t}\Pi_{\text{im}}D_{v,t})

(6.35)

for small
(v,t)
. We now observe that that the natural maps
\ker L_{v,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\ker D_{v,t}
and
{\text{coker }}L_{v,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\text{coker }}D_{v,t}
are isomorphisms. It follows (using the identifications
A=\ker D_{0,0}
and
B={\text{coker }}D_{0,0}
which hold because
\ker D_{0}={\text{coker }}D_{0}=0
) that the oriented vector bundle
\tilde{\nu}^{*}\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
is given, on a punctured neighborhood of
(0,0)
, by the assignment

\displaystyle(v,t)\mapsto{\text{coker }}(L_{v,t}:A\to B)

(6.36)

with the bundle orientation derived from the orientations already chosen on
A
and
B
. Note that
L_{0,0}=0
and that the first order Taylor expansion of
L_{v,t}
about
(0,0)
is given by the positively oriented linear isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Hom}(A,B)

(6.37)

from (
6.14
), which we recall is obtained from Definition
2.10
. Fixing a unitary
\Gamma
-invariant metric on
\mathbb{V}
and restricting to any sufficiently small sphere
S_{\mathbb{V}}\subset\mathbb{V}\times[-1,1]
centred at the origin, we can discard (i.e. homotope away) the higher order terms from the Taylor expansion of
L_{v,t}
. It follows that the fiber of the vector bundle (
6.36
) at a point
(v_{0},t_{0})\in S_{\mathbb{V}}
is identified with the quotient of the oriented vector space
\mathbb{V}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{t}
by the oriented
1
-dimensional space spanned by
(v_{0},t_{0})
. But this is simply the standard description of the (oriented) tangent bundle of the sphere
S_{\mathbb{V}}\subset\mathbb{V}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{t}
, once we replace “quotient” by “orthogonal complement” and (
6.28
) now follows.
∎

Note that Lemma
6.10
holds for any
d
and
h
. It is ultimately a consequence of the isomorphism (
6.14
) via the finite dimensional reduction argument presented above.

Remark 6.11
.

A coordinate invariant way of summarizing the above proof is the following. The bundle
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}\to(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1])\setminus(\Delta\times 0)
extends to the real oriented blow-up of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1]
along the smooth points
\Delta\times 0
as a vector bundle
\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
. Moreover, for each point
p\in\Delta
, we have a natural vector bundle isomorphism with orientation
\text{sgn}(\mathcal{D},p)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}|_{\mathbb{S}_{p}}=T\mathbb{S}_{p}

(6.38)

over
\mathbb{S}_{p}
, the exceptional boundary component created by blowing up at
p
. Now, a direct cobordism argument as in Lemma
6.8
, applied to the real oriented blow-up
\text{Bl}^{+}_{\Delta\times 0}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times[-1,1])
gives (
6.20
).

6.2.
Comparision to Gopakumar–Vafa invariants

In this subsection, we identify Taubes’ Gromov invariant in dimension
6
(Equation (
1.5
)) with the Gopakumar–Vafa invariants in the case of genus
0
and class
2A
for
A\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
primitive. We omit the underlying Calabi–Yau
3
-fold
(X,\omega)
from the notation.

Theorem 6.12
.

The invariant
\text{Gr}_{2A,0}
satisfies the Gopakumar–Vafa formula. More precisely, define, for super-rigid
J
,

\displaystyle\text{GW}_{A,0}

\displaystyle:=\text{Gr}_{A,0}:=\sum_{C:\,A,0}\text{sgn}(D^{N}_{C,J})

(6.39)

\displaystyle\text{GW}_{2A,0}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{C^{\prime}:\,2A,0}\text{sgn}(D^{N}_{C^{\prime},J})+\sum_{C:\,A,0}\text{sgn}(D^{N}_{C,J})e_{2,0}(C,D^{N}_{C,J})

(6.40)

where
e_{2,0}
denotes the virtual Euler number as defined in (
6.10
) and the summations follow the notation of (
1.5
).
15
15

15

For super-rigid
J
, this definition indeed recovers the Gromov–Witten invariants, see
[
28
, Theorem 1.2]
.
Then, we have the following relation between
GW
and
Gr
.

\displaystyle\text{GW}_{2A,0}=\text{Gr}_{2A,0}+\frac{1}{2^{3}}\cdot\text{Gr}_{A,0}.

(6.41)

Proof.

From (
6.39
)–(
6.40
) and (
1.5
), it is clear that (
6.41
) will follow once we show that

\displaystyle e_{2,0}(C,D^{N}_{C,J})-\frac{1}{2^{3}}=\text{w}_{2,0}(D^{N}_{C,J}).

(6.42)

Let
\bar{\partial}
denote an operator on
N_{C}
which is conjugate to the standard operator on
\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The Aspinwall–Morrison formula (see, for example
[
25
]
) shows that we have the identity

\displaystyle e_{2,0}(C,\bar{\partial})=\frac{1}{2^{3}}.

(6.43)

Thus, we are left to prove that the difference of Euler numbers is given by the linear wall crossing invariant
\text{w}_{2,0}(D^{N}_{C,J})
. To compute
\text{w}_{2,0}(D^{N}_{C,J})
, choose a smooth path
\gamma
lying in the Baire set
\mathcal{P}^{\pitchfork}(D^{N}_{C,J},\bar{\partial})
from Definition
5.14
. Let
-1<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}<1
be the values for which
\gamma(t_{i})
lies in the image of
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,0,\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})
. Using Lemma
5.3
, we can ensure that
\gamma
has only finitely many transverse intersections (at times
-1<t^{\prime}_{1}<\cdots<t^{\prime}_{n}<1
) with
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})
and, moreover, that
\gamma
misses
\mathcal{W}^{k}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})
for all
k\geq 2
. Moreover, we can also ensure (by Lemma
6.13
below) that
t_{i}\neq t^{\prime}_{j}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Clearly,
t\mapsto e_{2,0}(C,D_{t})
is a locally constant function away from
\{t_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
and
\{t^{\prime}_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
.

For any
1\leq j\leq n
, the operator
D_{t^{\prime}_{j}}
has a
1
-dimensional kernel. However, since
t^{\prime}_{j}\neq t_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
, it follows that
D_{t^{\prime}_{j}}
is
2
-rigid (in the sense of Definition
5.14
) and, therefore, also
(2,0)
-rigid (in the sense of Definition
6.2
). It follows that
\text{w}_{2,0}(D_{t})
remains constant for
t
near
t^{\prime}_{j}
. It also follows that the cokernel bundle
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,D_{t}}
is well-defined for all
t
near
t^{\prime}_{j}
(see Appendix
B
) and thus its Euler number
e_{2,0}(D_{t})
also remains constant for
t
near
t^{\prime}_{j}
. The reader may refer to Remark
6.4
to recall the orientation convention and to check that
e_{2,0}(D_{t})
is indeed unchanged near
t_{j}^{\prime}
.

For any
1\leq i\leq m
, we have
\ker D_{t_{i}}=0
(since
t_{i}\neq t^{\prime}_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
). For any point lying on the (necessarily transverse) fiber product of
\gamma
and
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,0,\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})
, i.e., a branched double cover
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
(with
C^{\prime}
smooth of genus
0
) such that
\ker(D_{t_{i}})^{-}_{\varphi}=\ker\varphi^{*}D_{t_{i}}=\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{t_{i}})^{\prime}
is
1
-dimensional, we get an isomorphism

\displaystyle T_{t_{i}}\gamma=\mathbb{R}_{t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Hom}(\ker(D_{t_{i}})^{-}_{\varphi},{\text{coker }}(D_{t_{i}})^{-}_{\varphi})/T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{0}(C,2)

(6.44)

whose sign is the amount that
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
contributes to the change in
\text{w}_{2,0}(D_{t})
as
t
goes from
t<t_{i}
to
t>t_{i}
. Recall that the right side of (
6.44
) is oriented using the natural co-orientation of
\mathcal{W}^{1}_{2,0,\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})
from Definition
5.8
. From (
6.44
), arguing as in Lemma
4.16
, we get that the analogue

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{t}\oplus T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{0}(C,2)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Hom}(\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{t_{i}})^{\prime},{\text{coker }}(\varphi^{*}D_{t_{i}})^{\prime})

(6.45)

of the map (
6.14
) is also an isomorphism. Thus, the family
D_{t}
undergoes a simple wall crossing (in the sense of Definition) at
t=t_{i}
and Theorem
6.7
now shows that the contribution of
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
to the change in
e_{2,0}(C,D_{t})
as
t
goes from
t<t_{i}
to
t>t_{i}
is precisely the sign of (
6.45
). Moreover, (
6.45
) induces (
6.44
) when we pass to the quotient by
T_{\varphi}\mathcal{M}_{0}(C,2)
and so, these two maps have the same sign. Here, we are using the identification
\ker(D_{t_{i}})^{-}_{\varphi}=\ker\varphi^{*}D_{t_{i}}=\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{t_{i}})^{\prime}
, and similarly for cokernels, which comes from recalling that
\ker D_{t_{i}}=0
. To summarize,
\text{w}_{2,0}(C,D_{t})
and
e_{2,0}(C,D_{t})
change by the same amount as
t
goes from
t<t_{i}
to
t>t_{i}
.

The last two paragraphs show that
e_{2,0}(D_{t})-\text{w}_{2,0}(D_{t})
is a constant function in
t
and this finishes the proof of (
6.42
) once we recall the identity (
6.43
) and that
\text{w}_{2,0}(\bar{\partial})=0
.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

Using the notation in the proof of Theorem
6.12
, the subset

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,0,\mathbb{R}}^{1,1}(N_{C})\subset\mathcal{C}\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})\times M_{0}(C,2)

(6.46)

consisting of
(j,D,[\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C])
with

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker D

\displaystyle=1

(6.47)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\ker D_{\varphi}^{-}

\displaystyle=1

(6.48)

is a submanifold of real codimension
6
.

Proof.

Let
(j,D,[\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C])\in\mathcal{W}^{1,1}_{2,0,\mathbb{R}}(N_{C})
. Using the argument which appears repeatedly in §5 (e.g. see the proofs of Lemmas
5.3
and
5.6
), it suffices to show that the linear map

\displaystyle\Omega^{0,1}_{j}(C,\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(N,N))

\displaystyle\to\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}(\ker\varphi^{*}D,{\text{coker }}\varphi^{*}D)

(6.49)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto(\kappa\mapsto(\varphi^{*}A)\kappa\pmod{\text{im }\varphi^{*}D})

(6.50)

analogous to (
5.22
) is surjective, since (
6.47
)–(
6.48
) imply that the right side of (
6.49
) is 6-dimensional over
\mathbb{R}
. Exactly as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
, this is equivalent to the injectivity of the “Petri map”

\displaystyle(\ker\varphi^{*}D\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ker(\varphi^{*}D)^{\dagger})^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{0}(C^{\prime},(\varphi^{*}N)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}(\varphi^{*}N)^{\dagger})^{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}

(6.51)

\displaystyle\sigma\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\zeta\mapsto\sigma(\zeta)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\sigma^{\prime}(\zeta))

(6.52)

dual to (
6.49
) and the latter follows from, e.g.,
[
9
, Remark 1.3.12]
.
∎

Appendix A
Kuranishi reduction

In this appendix, we collect some useful elementary observations about Kuranishi reduction (i.e., application of the implicit function theorem in Fredholm, but not necessarily transverse, situations) for which we are not aware of appropriate references in the literature.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be (possibly finite dimensional) Banach spaces and let

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}

(A.1)

be a smooth Fredholm map, defined on an open neighborhood of
0\in\mathcal{X}
, such that we have
\Phi(0)=0\in\mathcal{Y}
. Consider the linearization

\displaystyle\tilde{D}=D\Phi(0):\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}

(A.2)

and define the (necessarily finite dimensional) vector spaces
T:=\ker\tilde{D}
and
E:={\text{coker }}\tilde{D}
. Split the inclusion
T\to\mathcal{X}
and the projection
\mathcal{Y}\to E
by choosing a Banach space complement
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
of
T
and a linear subspace lift
E\subset\mathcal{Y}
. Applying the implicit function theorem now shows that
\Phi^{-1}(E)\subset\mathcal{X}
is identified (near
0\in\mathcal{X}
) with the graph of a smooth map
\Psi:T\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
defined on a neighborhood of
0\in T
. This map satisfies
\Psi(0)=0\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
D\Psi(0)=0
. The associated
Kuranishi map

\displaystyle F:T\to E,

(A.3)

defined near
0\in T
, is given by
F(\kappa):=\Phi(\kappa+\Psi(\kappa))
for
\kappa\in T
. It satisfies
F(0)=0
and
DF(0)=0
by construction. We refer to the process of constructing
F
from
\Phi
as
Kuranishi reduction
and note that it depends on the choices of splittings of the maps
T\to\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}\to E
. We first investigate this dependence.

Lemma A.1

(Splitting dependence of Kuranishi map)
.

Let
\tilde{F}:T\to E
be a Kuranishi map obtained using a different choice of splittings than in (
A.3
). Then, there exists a local diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\psi:T\to T

(A.4)

with
\psi(0)=0
and
D\psi(0)=\text{id}_{T}
and a smooth map

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}:T\to\text{End}(E)

(A.5)

with
\tilde{\psi}(0)=\text{id}_{E}
such that, for all
\kappa\in T
near
0
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(\kappa)=\tilde{\psi}(\kappa)F(\psi(\kappa)).

(A.6)

Proof.

Via the splitting used to define (
A.3
), we get direct sum decompositions
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\oplus T
and
\mathcal{Y}=\text{im}(\tilde{D})\oplus E
.

Consider first the case when
\tilde{F}
is obtained by the changing the splitting of
T\to\mathcal{X}
but using the same splitting of
\mathcal{Y}\to E
as in (
A.3
). Denote the resulting complement of
T
by
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{X}
.
Using the implicit function theorem, we write
\Phi^{-1}(E)
as the graph of a smooth map
\tilde{\Psi}:T\to\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
near
0\in\mathcal{X}
, with
\tilde{\Psi}(0)=0
and
D\tilde{\Psi}(0)=0
. Since
\Phi^{-1}(E)
is locally also the graph of the smooth map
\Psi:T\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, we must have

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\tilde{\Psi}(\kappa)

\displaystyle=\Psi(\kappa+\Pi_{T}\tilde{\Psi}(\kappa))

(A.7)

for all
\kappa\in T
near
0
, where
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
and
\Pi_{T}
denote the projections onto the summands of the decomposition
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\oplus T
. This immediately yields
\tilde{F}(\kappa)\equiv F(\psi(\kappa))
with
\psi:T\to T
defined by

\displaystyle\psi(\kappa)=\kappa+\Pi_{T}\tilde{\Psi}(\kappa).

(A.8)

Since
\tilde{\Psi}(0)=0
and
D\tilde{\Psi}(0)=0
, we see that
\psi
satisfies
\psi(0)=0
and
D\psi(0)=\text{id}_{T}
and is thus a local diffeomorphism.

Consider next the case when
\tilde{F}
is obtained by keeping the same splitting of
T\to\mathcal{X}
as in (
A.3
) but changing the splitting of the map
\mathcal{Y}\to E
. Denote the resulting complement
\text{im}(\tilde{D})
by
\tilde{E}\subset\mathcal{Y}
. Consider the map

\displaystyle\Phi_{E}:\mathcal{X}\oplus E\to\mathcal{Y}

(A.9)

given by
\Phi_{E}(x,e)=\Phi(x)-e
. Using the implicit function theorem, we may write
\Phi_{E}^{-1}(\tilde{E})
, near
0
, as the graph of a smooth map
\tilde{\Psi}_{E}:T\oplus E\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
. Using
\tilde{\Psi}_{E}
, we define the smooth map
\tilde{F}_{E}:T\oplus E\to E
by the formula

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{E}(\kappa,e)=\Pi_{E}(\Phi(\kappa+\tilde{\Psi}_{E}(\kappa,e))-e)

(A.10)

where
\Pi_{E}:\mathcal{Y}\to E
denotes the projection. Restricting
\tilde{\Psi}_{E}
to
T\oplus\{0\}
recovers the map
\tilde{\Psi}:T\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
whose graph coincides with
\Phi^{-1}(\tilde{E})
near
0\in\mathcal{X}
(which also shows that
\tilde{F}_{E}(\kappa,0)=\tilde{F}(\kappa)
for
\kappa\in T
). Therefore, as before, we have
\tilde{\Psi}(0)=0
and
D\tilde{\Psi}(0)=0
which shows that
D\tilde{F}_{E}(0,0)
is given by (the negative of) the projection onto
E
. Now, if
(\kappa,e)
is a point where
\tilde{F}_{E}
vanishes, then since
\Pi_{E}|_{\tilde{E}}:\tilde{E}\to E
is an isomorphism it follows that
\Phi(\kappa+\tilde{\Psi}_{E}(\kappa,e))-e=0
. This, in turn, implies that we must in fact have
\tilde{\Psi}_{E}(\kappa,e)=\Psi(\kappa)
and
e=F(\kappa)
. Conversely, if
e=F(\kappa)
, then
\Phi_{E}(\kappa+\Psi(\kappa),e)=\Phi(\kappa+\Psi(\kappa))-e=0\in\tilde{E}
and thus,
\tilde{\Psi}_{E}(\kappa,e)=\Psi(\kappa)
and
\tilde{F}_{E}(\kappa,e)=0
. In summary,
\tilde{F}_{E}
is a defining equation, near
(0,0)
, for the submanifold of
T\oplus E
given by the graph of the map
F:T\to E
, i.e., the equation
\tilde{F}_{E}\ =0
transversely cuts out the graph of
F
near
(0,0)
. Since
F_{E}(\kappa,e):=F(\kappa)-e
is also a defining equation for the graph of
F
, and
DF_{E}(0,0)=D\tilde{F}_{E}(0,0)
, it follows that there exists a smooth map
\tilde{\psi}_{E}:T\oplus E\to\text{End}(E)
with
\tilde{\psi}_{E}(0,0)=\text{id}_{E}
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{E}(\kappa,e)=\tilde{\psi}_{E}(\kappa,e)\cdot F_{E}(\kappa,e)

(A.11)

for all
(\kappa,e)\in T\oplus E
near
(0,0)
. Restricting to
e=0
and defining the map
\tilde{\psi}:T\to GL(E)
by
\tilde{\psi}(\kappa)=\tilde{\psi}_{E}(\kappa,0)
, we obtain
\tilde{F}(\kappa)\equiv\tilde{\psi}(\kappa)F(\kappa)
.

Combining the arguments of the preceding two paragraphs produces
\psi,\tilde{\psi}
and the identity (
A.6
) in the general case.
∎

Lemma
A.1
has the following important consequence.

Corollary A.2

(Invariance of leading order term)
.

If
n\geq 1
is an integer with
D^{j}F(0)=0
for
0\leq j\leq n
, then we also have
D^{j}\tilde{F}(0)
for
0\leq j\leq n
. Moreover, we have the equality

\displaystyle D^{n+1}F(0)=D^{n+1}\tilde{F}(0)\in\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T,E).

(A.12)

Remark A.3
.

It is important to note that, in the setting of Corollary
A.2
, the leading term
D^{n+1}F(0)
is also invariant under replacing the map
\Phi
by the map
x\mapsto\Phi_{\chi,\tilde{\chi}}(x)=\tilde{\chi}(x)\Phi(\chi(x))
, where
\chi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
is any local diffeomorphism (defined near
0\in\mathcal{X}
) with
\chi(0)=0
and
D\chi(0)=\text{id}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\tilde{\chi}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y})
is any smooth map to the space of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{Y}
(defined near
0\in\mathcal{X}
) with
\tilde{\chi}(0)=\text{id}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. The proof is analogous to that of Lemma
A.1
. Note that we have
\tilde{D}=D\Phi(0)=D\Phi_{\chi,\tilde{\chi}}(0)
.

We next investigate how a perturbation of
\Phi
(vanishing to high order) affects the Kuranishi map
F
. More precisely, let
n\geq 1
be an integer and let
P:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a smooth map with the property that
D^{j}P(0)=0
for
0\leq j\leq n
. Observe that
D^{n+1}P(0)
defines an element of
\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T,E)
by restriction along
T\to\mathcal{X}
and projection along
\mathcal{Y}\to E
.

Define
\hat{\Phi}=\Phi+P:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Then,
D\hat{\Phi}(0)=D\Phi(0)=\tilde{D}
and thus,
\hat{\Phi}
is still Fredholm in a neighborhood of
0\in\mathcal{X}
. Thus, we may perform Kuranishi reduction on
\hat{\Phi}
using the same choices of splittings of
T\to\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}\to E
as in (
A.3
) to obtain the Kuranishi map

\displaystyle\hat{F}:T\to E.

(A.13)

Write
Q=\hat{F}-F
. In this situation, we have the following useful fact.

Lemma A.4

(Effect of high order perturbation)
.

D^{j}Q(0)=0
for
0\leq j\leq n
. Moreover, we have the equality

\displaystyle D^{n+1}Q(0)\equiv D^{n+1}P(0)

(A.14)

as elements of
\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T,E)
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\Psi}:T\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be the map whose graph coincides with
\hat{\Phi}^{-1}(E)\subset\mathcal{X}
near
0
. Since
D^{j}\Phi(0)=D^{j}\hat{\Phi}(0)
for
0\leq j\leq n
, it follows by using the chain rule that the we also have
D^{j}\Psi(0)=D^{j}\hat{\Psi}(0)
for
0\leq j\leq n
. This readily yields
D^{j}F(0)=D^{j}\hat{F}(0)
for
0\leq j\leq n
. Next, we compute

\displaystyle D^{n+1}Q(0)

\displaystyle=D^{n+1}\hat{F}(0)-D^{n+1}F(0)

(A.15)

\displaystyle=D^{n+1}(\hat{\Phi}\circ(\text{id}_{T}+\hat{\Psi}))(0)-D^{n+1}(\Phi\circ(\text{id}_{T}+\Psi))(0).

(A.16)

We break the last sum into the two following parts:

\displaystyle D^{n+1}(P\circ(\text{id}_{T}+\hat{\Psi}))(0)

(A.17)

\displaystyle D^{n+1}(\Phi\circ(\text{id}_{T}+\hat{\Psi}))(0)-

\displaystyle D^{n+1}(\Phi\circ(\text{id}_{T}+\Psi))(0).

(A.18)

Simplifying (
A.17
) using the chain rule just yields
D^{n+1}P(0)
since all lower derivatives of
P
vanish at
0
and
D\hat{\Psi}(0)=0
. On the other hand, simplifying (
A.18
) using the chain rule just yields (a term proportional to) the term

\displaystyle D\Phi(0)\circ D^{n+1}(\hat{\Psi}-\Psi)(0).

(A.19)

Indeed, all other terms cancel since
D^{j}\hat{\Psi}(0)=D^{j}\Psi(0)
for
0\leq j\leq n
. But now we observe that (
A.19
) lies in
\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T,\text{im}(\tilde{D}))
and, as a result, has zero image in
\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T,E)
. This shows that we must have
D^{n+1}Q(0)\equiv D^{n+1}P(0)
in
\text{Hom}(\text{Sym}^{n+1}T,E)
as claimed.
∎

Appendix B
Obstruction bundles

In this appendix, we show how to construct obstruction/cokernel bundles under the (somewhat non-standard) assumption of
(d,h)
-rigidity in Definition
6.2
. The proof will be given in the generality needed to establish that the condition (
6.6
) is open and that (
6.12
) defines an orbi-bundle. The argument will make use of “thickenings” as in §
3
(and we use very similar notation to facilitate the comparison). For the convenience of the reader, we begin by briefly recalling the relevant setup from §
6.1
.

We fix a smooth genus
g
curve
C
and integers
h\geq g
and
d\geq 2
such that the moduli stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
has (real) virtual dimension
2r\geq 0
. We also fix a complex vector bundle
N\to C
of rank
2
and degree
2g-2
along with a continuous family
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in P}
of real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
N
, parametrized by a topological space
P
. Let
\hat{\Delta}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P
consist of exactly those pairs
([\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C],\lambda)
for which the condition

\displaystyle\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{\lambda})^{\prime}:={\text{coker }}(\ker D_{\lambda}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{*}}\ker(\varphi^{*}D_{\lambda}))=0

(B.1)

fails. We will show that
\hat{\Delta}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P
is closed and that the assignment, to
([\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C],\lambda)
, of the real vector space

\displaystyle{\text{coker }}(\varphi^{*}D_{\lambda})^{\prime}:={\text{coker }}({\text{coker }}D_{\lambda}\xrightarrow{\varphi^{*}}{\text{coker }}(\varphi^{*}D_{\lambda}))

(B.2)

defines an orbi-bundle
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
(of rank
2r
) over the open complement of
\hat{\Delta}
.

To this end, fix any point
([\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C],\lambda)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P
. Choose points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in C
, which are not the images of critical points of
\varphi
or nodal points of
C^{\prime}
, such that
(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
is a stable curve. For
1\leq i\leq m
, choose a numbering
\varphi^{-1}(p_{i})=\{q_{i}^{1},\ldots,q_{i}^{d}\}
and observe that
(C^{\prime},\{q_{i}^{j}\})
gives a point of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h,dm}
. Choose a complex manifold
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
(equipped with a basepoint
\bullet
), a finite group
\Gamma^{\prime}
(acting on
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
holomorphically and fixing
\bullet
) and an open embedding of complex orbifolds

\displaystyle[\mathscr{M}^{\prime}/\Gamma^{\prime}]\hookrightarrow[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h,dm}/(S_{d})^{m}]

(B.3)

mapping the image of
\bullet
to the image of
(C^{\prime},\{q_{i}^{j}\})
, where the
i^{\text{th}}
factor in the
m
-fold product of
S_{d}
acts on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h,dm}
by permuting the marked points
\{q_{i}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
. By pulling back the universal curve over the right side of (
B.3
) to
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
, we obtain a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant (flat) complex analytic family
\pi^{\prime}:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
of (possibly nodal) arithmetic genus
h
curves with sections
\tau_{i}^{j}
(corresponding to
dm
marked points which stabilize the fibers). For
1\leq i\leq m
, the sections
\tau_{i}^{j}
for
1\leq j\leq d
are permuted among themselves by the action of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. For later use, we also fix an isomorphism

\displaystyle i^{\prime}:(C^{\prime},\{q_{i}^{j}\})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{\bullet},\{\sigma_{i}^{j}(\bullet)\}).

(B.4)

Define
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)
to be the space consisting of pairs
(s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime},\psi:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s}\to C)
with
\psi
being a holomorphic map satisfying
\psi_{*}[\mathscr{C}_{s^{\prime}}]=d[C]
and
\psi(\tau_{i}^{j}(s^{\prime}))=p_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq d
. The space
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)
carries an evident action of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and the quotient
[\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)/\Gamma^{\prime}]
now provides an orbispace chart for a neighborhood of
[\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C]
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
.
16
16

16

Note that the purpose of taking the quotient by
(S_{d})^{m}
in (
B.3
) is to describe
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)
locally near
\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C
as the quotient stack
[\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)/\Gamma^{\prime}]
.
We will construct the orbi-bundle with fibers (
B.2
) by constructing a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant vector bundle on (an open subset of)
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)\times P
, which then descends to the quotient.

Choose a finite dimensional vector space
E
and a linear map

\displaystyle\mu:E\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,N)

(B.5)

such that the induced map
E\to{\text{coker }}D_{\lambda}
is surjective. Next, choose a finite dimensional
\Gamma^{\prime}
-representation
E^{\prime}
and a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant map

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\times C,\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}/\mathscr{M}^{\prime}}\boxtimes_{\mathbb{C}}N)

(B.6)

such that its restriction along
i^{\prime}\times\varphi:C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\times C
induces a surjective map
E^{\prime}\to{\text{coker }}(\varphi^{*}D_{\lambda})^{\prime}
. Here,
\mathscr{C}^{\prime\circ}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
denotes the complement of the (fiberwise) nodal locus of
\pi^{\prime}
and the sections in the image of
\mu^{\prime}
are required to have proper support over
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
. For more details on how to construct (
B.6
), the reader is referred to the proof of
[
22
, Lemma 9.2.9]
.

Now, consider the space
K
of
(t\in P,\sigma\in C^{\infty}(C,N),e\in E)
satisfying

\displaystyle D_{t}\sigma=\mu(e)

(B.7)

with its natural projection
K\to P
. By the choice of
\mu
, (
B.7
) describes the kernel of a family of surjective (thickened) Cauchy–Riemann operators (near
\lambda
). Thus, using a standard implicit function theorem argument, we see that
K
defines a vector bundle over
P
near
\lambda
and there is a (forgetful) linear bundle map from
K\to\underline{E}
, where
\underline{E}
is the trivial bundle with fiber
E
. Similarly, consider the space
K^{\prime}
of tuples of the form
((s^{\prime},\psi)\in\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C),t\in P,\sigma^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}},\psi^{*}N),e\in E,e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime})
satisfying

\displaystyle(\psi^{*}D_{t})\sigma^{\prime}=\psi^{*}(\mu(e))+\mu^{\prime}(e^{\prime})(\cdot,\psi(\cdot))

(B.8)

with its natural projection
K^{\prime}\to\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)\times P
. By the choice of
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
, (
B.8
) describes the kernel of a family of surjective (thickened) Cauchy–Riemann operators. Thus, standard arguments involving the implicit function theorem and (linear) gluing analysis (e.g. following
[
22
, Appendix B]
or
[
28
, Theorem 1.2(2)]
) show that
K^{\prime}
defines a vector bundle over
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)\times P
near
((\bullet,\varphi\circ i^{\prime-1}),\lambda)
and there is a (forgetful) linear bundle map
K^{\prime}\to\underline{E}\oplus\underline{E^{\prime}}
. Moreover,
K^{\prime}
and this bundle map respect the action of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Denoting the projection
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}(C)\times P\to P
by
\pi
, we now have a continuous fiberwise linear map of two-term of complexes of vector bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{E}\oplus\underline{E^{\prime}}}}

(B.9)

described as follows. The horizontal arrows are the linear bundle maps defined earlier, while the right vertical arrow is the natural inclusion. The left vertical arrow is defined by setting
e^{\prime}=0
and pulling back
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(C,N)
to
\sigma^{\prime}=\psi^{*}\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{C}_{s^{\prime}},\psi^{*}N)
. Both vertical arrows in (
B.9
) are injective maps of vector bundles and passing to the quotient yields

\displaystyle L:K^{\prime}/\pi^{*}K\to\underline{E^{\prime}}.

(B.10)

L
is a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant linear bundle map whose fiberwise kernel and cokernel are identified with the left sides of (
B.1
) and (
B.2
) respectively. We deduce that the complement of (the pre-image of)
\hat{\Delta}
is open (since the condition defining it amounts to
L
being injective) and, over this open set,
{\text{coker }}L
forms a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-equivariant vector bundle. This provides an orbi-bundle structure on
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
locally near any point
([\varphi:C^{\prime}\to C],\lambda)
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{h}(C,d)\times P
at which (
B.1
) is satisfied. The rank of this orbi-bundle can be computed by noting that
\text{ind}(D_{\lambda})=0
and
\text{ind}(\varphi^{*}D_{\lambda})=-2r
.

To compare the local orbi-bundle presentations of
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
obtained by this method for two different sets of choices, we embed both into the “doubly thickened” versions of (
B.7
)–(
B.8
). This allows us to construct the local orbi-bundle isomorphisms needed to patch
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
globally over the locus where (
B.1
) is satisfied. For more details on how to define a common thickening to dominate two given thickenings, the reader is referred to
[
22
, Definition 9.2.3]
. This completes the proof that
\hat{\Delta}
is closed and that
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
is a well-defined rank
2r
orbi-bundle over its complement.

Remark B.1
.

Since we appealed to (linear) gluing analysis following
[
22
, Appendix B]
, we only obtain a continuous orbi-bundle structure on
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
. Nevertheless, this construction suffices for our topological arguments. However, over the locus
(\mathcal{M}_{h}(C,d)\times P)\setminus\hat{\Delta}
involving only stable maps to
C
with smooth domains it is easy to see from the above argument (which, in this case, only requires the implicit function theorem and no gluing analysis) that
\mathcal{N}^{d,h}_{C,\mathcal{D}}
actually has a natural smooth orbi-bundle structure provided
\mathcal{D}
is a smooth family parametrized by a smooth manifold
P
.
